\begin{center}
{\LARGE\bfseries Diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices\par}
\vspace{4pt}
{\LARGE\bfseries (OEIS A005163)\par}
\vspace{9pt}
{\large The full leading equivalent and its amplitude, the bounded logarithmic\par
remainder, the fugacity pressure and limit laws, and elementary growth envelopes}\par
\vspace{9pt}
{\small Four research reports of one external research session (bundle Reports 128, 124, 121 and 120),\par merged into one ProveIt report}\par
\vspace{4pt}
{\small Parts I--IV: 2 October 2026\quad merged: 5 October 2026}
\end{center}
\begin{abstract}
Let $a_n$ count the diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices (DSASMs) of order $n$, the sequence A005163, and let $Z_n(s)$ weight each DSASM by $s$ to the number of its nonzero diagonal entries, so that $a_n=Z_n(1)$. Put $\alpha=\frac12\log(3\sqrt3/4)$, $\beta=\frac14\log3$ and $\kappa=5/72$. This report prints four manuscripts as Parts~I--IV. Part~I (the base) proves the full leading equivalent $a_n\sim C_*e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}$ along all $n$, the leading term of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan's Conjecture~11.1, and more generally $Z_n(s)\sim C(s)e^{\alpha n^2+nB(s)}n^{-\kappa}$ locally uniformly on $\Re s>0$; the positive amplitude is characterized by convergent Fredholm determinants and a convergent Fourier series, but is not evaluated. Part~I also gives an amplitude-aware inverse threshold and the constant terms of every fixed diagonal cumulant. Its proof rests on Part~II, which proves stability of the diagonal fugacity polynomials, weak cross-size interlacing, an exactly solvable calibration at $s=\sqrt3$, the bounded remainder $\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1)$, a holomorphic pressure with bounded error, bounded cumulant errors and an explicitly centred central limit theorem. Part~III proves the fugacity pressure with $o(n)$ error by a second route and adds results found nowhere else here: the limiting root measure, half of whose mass escapes to infinity, Hoeffding concentration, a parity-lattice local limit theorem and a large-deviation principle with explicit rate. Part~IV gives elementary growth envelopes by Jensen and chord inequalities on the roots and a projected contraction, with certified constants $p<\beta<q$. The numerical value of the amplitude, the finer corrections conjectured by Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan, any rate of convergence and effective constants remain open. The report is unrefereed, and nothing in it is formalized. (Dated note, 7~October 2026: ``weak'' added before ``cross-size interlacing'', as the independent check did in Sections~42 and~49: Part~II proves interlacing with multiplicities, and strict interlacing remains open.)
\end{abstract}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

\section*{Guide to this report}\label{dsasm:sec:guide}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Guide to this report}
The four Parts are four manuscripts of the session bundle of Reports 1--243 (arrival commit \texttt{60f54ea06}), placed in this directory by commit \texttt{612787fb4} (batch~104). All four are dated 2~October 2026 and form one programme on one object, written in the order 120, 121, 124, 128. They are printed in order of strength, the strongest first, because Report~128 implies every growth statement of the other three:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.47\textwidth} l@{}}
\toprule
Part & Bundle report & Delivered title & Labels\\
\midrule
I & 128 (base) & The full leading equivalent for diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices & \texttt{dsasm:}\\
II & 124 & Logarithmic asymptotics of diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices & \texttt{dsasm:log:}\\
III & 121 & DSASM fugacity pressure and diagonal statistics & \texttt{dsasm:pr:}\\
IV & 120 & Diagonal fugacity roots and DSASM growth bounds & \texttt{dsasm:env:}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Part~I is not self-contained.} Report~128 takes four groups of results, (I1)--(I4) in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:main}, from Report~124, which it shipped unchanged as an ``earlier input''. Report~124 is therefore printed in full as Part~II, and Part~I's eight citations of it are internal references here. Read Part~II's Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:transfer} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:complex} before, or alongside, Part~I's Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion}--\ref{dsasm:sec:consequences}.

Each Part prints its manuscript's text, with every result, proof, remark, open question and limitation; statements keep their delivered wording. Sections are numbered continuously and statements within sections, so a delivered statement number moves with its section only: Part~I keeps Report~128's Sections~1--8 and adds Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}; Report~124's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+9$ (Part~II, Sections~10--24); Report~121's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+24$ (Part~III, Sections~25--40); Report~120's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+40$ (Part~IV, Sections~41--49). The four manuscripts number equations consecutively through the whole text; each Part keeps its manuscript's equation numbers, prefixed by the Part in Parts~II--IV: Report~124's equation $(k)$ is $(\mathrm{II}.k)$ here, Report~121's is $(\mathrm{III}.k)$ and Report~120's is $(\mathrm{IV}.k)$, and Part~I's equations keep their delivered numbers $(k)$.

Reports~120, 121 and~124 share whole sections, almost word for word (8-gram overlap 0.89--1.00 by section; differences checked line by line with \texttt{diff}). Each is printed once. The stability section, the exact calibration and the logarithmic calibration are printed in Part~II (Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration}); ``Two consequences for the diagonal statistic'', shared by Reports~120 and~121 only, is printed in Part~III (Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:consequences}). The repeated sections of Parts~III and~IV keep their titles and numbers, a pointer, every sentence in which they differ from the printed text, and their equation numbers (an equation not reprinted there is cited at its printed place in Part~II or~III). Report~121's interlacing section repeats Report~124's except for an exact derivative identity and a mean bracket, which Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing} prints.

Notes added when the manuscripts were merged are set in small type and tagged \textbf{[write]}, with their date (5~October 2026). They give cross-references between the Parts, mark results proved twice (second routes), mark statements that a stronger Part supersedes, and record what the write checked. Superseded statements, scope sentences and questions are printed as delivered, with a dated note pointing to the Part that settles them. Each Part ends with a section of open questions: Part~I with Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, added in the write, and Parts~II--IV with their own closing sections, re-scoped by dated notes. Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026 these collect every claim of the Part stated without proof, with its source, its sketch and what is missing.

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What is proved, and where}
Here $c=\sqrt3$, $P(s)=(s+1)^2/(2(s+2))$ is the pressure, $D_n(t)=Z_n(\sqrt t)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt t)/\sqrt t$ is the companion polynomial, $f(t)=\Log P(\sqrt t)$, and $S_n$ is the number of nonzero diagonal entries under the fugacity-$s$ law $s^{S(A)}/Z_n(s)$.

Inverse threshold with the amplitude in the centre; cumulants $n\mathcal D^{k-1}m(s)+\mathcal D^kL(s)+o(1)$ & Part~I, Corollaries~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse} and~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants}\\
$\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1)$ for all $n\ge1$; $\log(D_n(1)/D_n(3))=n\log(2/3)+O(1)$; trace limit $D_n'(3)/D_n(3)=(n+1)(1-\sqrt3/2)+o(1)$ & Part~II, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main} (bounds and determinant remainder superseded by Part~I; the trace limit is not restated there)\\

Pressure $\frac1n\log(D_n(t)/D_n(3))\to f(t)$ with all derivatives on $\Re t>0$ (second route); $\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n+o(n)$ & Part~III, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth}\\
Root measure $\nu_n\Rightarrow\frac12\delta_\infty+3\,d\lambda/(\pi\sqrt\lambda(\lambda+1)(\lambda+4))$; Newton inequalities; Hoeffding bound; actual-mean CLT; parity-lattice local CLT; speed-$n$ large deviations with explicit rate & Part~III, \eqref{dsasm:pr:rootlaw}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:newton}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:hoeffding}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:clt}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:lclt}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:ldp}\\

Finite computations in all four Parts (exact rational and integer checks, interval certificates, adversarial input campaigns) check identities, normalizations and implementations; no asymptotic statement rests on them. Part~IV's interval certificates for $p$ and $q$ are exact computations with explicit tails, and only certify those two constants.

\section*{The leading equivalent and Conjecture 11.1}\label{dsasm:sec:bfk}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The leading equivalent and Conjecture 11.1}
The equivalent of Part~I is the leading term of a conjecture of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan \cite{bfk}. In version~3 of their paper (arXiv:2309.08446v3, dated 1~October 2026; text checked by the write on 5~October 2026), Conjecture~11.1, equation~(11.8), states that
\[
 |\DS(n)|=C\Bigl(\frac{3\sqrt3}{4}\Bigr)^{n^2/2}3^{n/4}n^{-5/72}
 \begin{cases}
 1-\dfrac{385}{31104n^2}+\dfrac{(-1)^{n/2}a}{n^{5/2}}+O(n^{-3}), & n\text{ even},\\[8pt]
 1-\dfrac{385}{31104n^2}+\dfrac{(-1)^{(n+1)/2}b}{n^{5/2}}+O(n^{-3}), & n\text{ odd},
 \end{cases}
\]
with constants $C$, $a$, $b$ that they estimate numerically from the exact counts for $n\le1000$ ($C\approx0.72352852136732\ldots$, $a\approx0.0783$, $b\approx0.1892$) and the further conjecture $b=(1+\sqrt2)a$; they report no closed expression for $C$. Their Theorem~11.2 proves the leading growth $|\DS(n)|^{2/n^2}\to3\sqrt3/4$. Since $(3\sqrt3/4)^{n^2/2}3^{n/4}=e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}$, Part~I, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}, proves the \emph{leading equivalent} of~(11.8): the limit $C=C_*$ exists, is positive, and is given by \eqref{dsasm:aequiv} and \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}. It does \emph{not} prove the finer corrections, the term $-385/(31104n^2)$, the parity-dependent $n^{-5/2}$ terms, the relation $b=(1+\sqrt2)a$, or any rate of convergence, and it does not evaluate $C_*$; so it neither confirms nor uses the digits of~$C$. The credit for the conjectured form, the exponent $-5/72$ and the numerical constant belongs to Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan; Part~II's earlier theorem proves the conjectured shape up to a bounded factor, and Parts~III and~IV the linear coefficient and envelopes around it. The manuscripts cite this conjecture in the same version; their quotation of it (with an unnamed constant $C_{\DS}>0$ and ``further parity-sensitive corrections'') is accurate. The numerical evidence about $C$ and the correction terms is in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, labelled uncertified.

\section*{Notation across the four Parts}\label{dsasm:sec:notation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Notation across the four Parts}
Common to all Parts: $a_n=|\DS(n)|$; $S(A)$ the number of nonzero diagonal entries of $A$; $Q_n(s_1,\ldots,s_n)$ the multivariate diagonal polynomial and $Z_n(s)=Q_n(s,\ldots,s)$, with $Z_n(s)=X_n(1,s,1)$ in the notation of \cite{bfk}; $A_N=|\ASM(N)|$; $\alpha=\frac12\log(3\sqrt3/4)=g/2$, $\beta=\frac14\log3$, $\kappa=5/72$, $b=\frac34\log3-\frac12\log2$ (Parts~II--IV also write $g=\log(3\sqrt3/4)$); $c=\sqrt3$, the solvable calibration; $\eps=\eps_n=n\bmod2$; $N(T)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge T\}$; $\HH$ is a half-plane in Parts~II--IV and, in Part~I, $\HH_+=\{\Re s>0\}$ while $\mathcal H$ (same macro) is the Hilbert space $L^2(w_y\,dy)$ of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}. The symbols below change meaning between the Parts or inside one. No symbol was renamed; each Part's own text fixes its meaning there, and this table and the reading conventions at the head of each Part are the convention.

\textbf{The letter $P$.} Read $P_n$ by its argument. \emph{(a)} With a polynomial argument, $P_n(t)$, $P_n(s^2)$, $P_n(0)$, $P_n(4)$, it is the parity-reduced polynomial of \eqref{dsasm:log:paritypoly}, $Z_n(s)=s^{\eps_n}P_n(s^2)$, monic of degree $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ with strictly negative roots, and $D_n=P_nP_{n+1}$ (Parts~II--IV; Part~I does not use it). \emph{(b)} As an operator factor, $P_n\mathcal BP_n$, $\Ran P_n$, $XP_nY^*$, it is a projection of rank $n$: onto polynomials of degree $<n$ in Part~I, Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}, and Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}; onto the first $n$ coordinates in Part~I, Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion} and~\ref{dsasm:sec:edge} (Part~II writes this one $P_n^0$, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap}). \emph{(c)} Without subscript, $P(s)=(s+1)^2/(2(s+2))$ is the pressure of Part~I, \eqref{dsasm:pressure}; $P_{n-1}^{(a_J,b_J)}$ in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scalar}, is a Jacobi polynomial. \emph{Tempting false reading:} the identity $D_n=P_nP_{n+1}$ of Parts~II--IV is a product of polynomials, not of projections, and Part~I's determinant $\det(I+(t-3)P_n\mathcal BP_n)$ contains no polynomial $P_n$.

\toprule
Symbol & Meanings\\
\midrule
\endhead

Also, in Part~I: $f_j=\sqrt{w_y}\psi_j$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:smoothing}) and $f(\theta)=\log\tan(\theta/2)$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:columns}); the residual $L+(-1)^nd$ of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:main} uses $L$, $d$ as generic constants, not $L(s)=\Log C(s)$. Part~II uses $f$ for a polynomial in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing} (as do Parts~III and~IV through it).\\
$B$, $\beta$ & $B(s)=b+\frac12\Log P(s)$, the linear coefficient at fugacity $s$ (Part~I), which Part~III writes $\beta(s)$ in \eqref{dsasm:pr:freeenergy}; $B(1)=\beta(1)=\beta$. Also $\mathcal B$ the bounded operator (Parts~I, II), $B$ a matrix and $B_0(z)$ a symbol (Part~I, Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary} and~\ref{dsasm:sec:edge}), $B_n$, $B_e$ operators (Part~I, Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion}), $B_{n,j}$ Bernoulli variables (Parts~II, III), $B_\pm$ envelopes (Part~IV).\\
$E$, $E_n$ & Part~I: $E_n(t)=\Log(D_n(t)/D_n(3))-nf(t)$ and its limit $E(t)$; $\mathcal E$ an operator. Part~II, question~1 of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}: the number $E_n=\log[D_n(1)/D_n(3)]-n\log(2/3)$, which is Part~I's $E_n(1)$. Part~IV: $E_n=\log(P_n(3)/P_n(1))$ in \eqref{dsasm:env:DE}, unrelated. $\mathbb E$ is always an expectation.\\
$D_n$ & The companion polynomial $D_n(t)=Z_n(\sqrt t)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt t)/\sqrt t$ in Parts~I--III. Part~IV: $D_n=\log(P_n(4)/P_n(1))$ in \eqref{dsasm:env:DE}, a number. \emph{False reading:} Part~IV's $D_n$ is not a polynomial. Also $\mathcal D=s\,d/ds$ (Part~I) and $\mathcal D=HM_wH+(I-H)M_w(I-H)$ (Part~I, \eqref{dsasm:Xidef}).\\
$m$, $v$ & $m(s)=\frac12-1/((s+1)(s+2))$ and $v(s)=s\,m'(s)$, the mean and variance slopes (Parts~I--III); $m(1)=1/3$, $v(1)=5/36$. Also, in Part~I: $m(y)=1/(1+e^{2ay})$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}), $v(F)=\sum kF_k^2$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion}), the gamma phase $v$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:smoothing}); Part~III: $m_n=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, $v_n=\Var(T_n)$; Part~IV: $m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$.\\
$M_n$ & Parts~II, III: the mean $M_n(s)=sZ_n'(s)/Z_n(s)$ of the diagonal count. Part~I: $M_n$ is multiplication by $e^{-in\theta}$ (a modulation) in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion}, and $(M_n)_{ij}=\langle\psi_i,m\psi_j\rangle$ in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}.\\
$C$ & $C(s)$ the individual amplitude, $C_*=C(1)$, $C_{\rm cal}$ the calibration amplitude, $C_{\rm ASM}$, $C_{\rm odd}$ (Part~I); $C_{\DS}$ the conjectured amplitude (Parts~II--IV), which is Part~I's $C_*$; $C_0$ an operator (Parts~I, II); $C$, $C_K$, $C_\eta$ unspecified constants; $C_n$ a matrix (Part~III).\\
$c$ & $c=\sqrt3$ (Parts~I, III; Parts~II and~IV write $\sqrt3$); $c_0=11\pi/12$ a phase (Part~I); $c_n$ leading coefficients, $c_{n,k}$ coefficients (Part~III, Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:consequences}); $c_\pm$ the constants in \eqref{dsasm:log:bounds}; $c(T)$ the inverse centre in Part~IV.\\
$a$, $h$ & $a_n$ the count. Part~I: $a=\pi/(3h)$ with $h=\sqrt3$, $a_j^{\rm src}$ the source coefficients, $h_j=j!(3)_j$, $H_j$ norms. Part~IV: $a=(1-\theta)/\theta$. $a$, $b$ in ``The leading equivalent and Conjecture~11.1'' are Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan's correction constants.\\
$d$ & Part~III: $d=3-3\sqrt3/2=m(\sqrt3)$, the calibration mean slope; Part~IV's logarithmic calibration writes $d=5/36$ where Parts~II and~III write $d_{\rm ASM}$. Part~I: $d_j=\sqrt{(j+1)(j+2)}$, $d(\theta)=\min(\theta,\pi-\theta)$. Part~II: $d(r)$ in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:inverse}.\\
$r$ & The inverse scale $r=\sqrt{\log T/\alpha}$ (Parts~I, II), which Parts~III and~IV call $n_0$; $r(x)$ the tilted fugacity of the rate function (Part~III, Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:ldp}); $r(z)=1-z+z^2$ (Parts~I, III); $r_{n,j}$ roots (Parts~II, III); $r_\pm(T)$ roots (Part~IV).\\
$\nu$ & Part~III: $\nu_n$, $\nu$ the root measures of \eqref{dsasm:pr:rootlaw}. Part~I: $\nu_t$ the source density of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}.\\
$S$ & $S(A)$ and $S_n$ the diagonal count; Part~I: $S_n(\delta)$ a determinant, $S$ the lower shift matrix, $\mathcal S$ a trace-class operator; Part~III: $S$ the random diagonal count.\\
$T$ & $T$ the count threshold (all Parts). Part~I: $T_n(\delta)$ a determinant, $T$ a convolution operator, $T_n(g)=g(S)$ a Toeplitz matrix, $T_r$ a tilted compression. Part~III: $T_n=(S-\eps)/2$.\\
$z$, $n_0$ & The real centre of the inverse: $z_C(T)$ in Part~I, \eqref{dsasm:zinverse}; $z(T)$ in Part~II, \eqref{dsasm:log:zdef} (without the amplitude term); $n_0-\beta/(2\alpha)$ in Part~III; $c(T)$ in Part~IV.\\
$Q_n$ & The multivariate polynomial (all Parts); Part~I: $Q_n(\delta)$ a determinant in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion} and $Q_n=I-P_n$ in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:edge}; Part~III: $Q_n^{\rm mat}$ a matrix.\\
$F$ & Part~I: $F_n(s)$ the boundary ratio \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget}, $F$, $F_k$, $F_\delta$ angular functions, $F_n^\phi$, $F_n^\psi$ Gram matrices. Part~II: $F(x)$ the inverse function of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:inverse}. Part~IV: $F=\ell\circ u$.\\
$\kappa$, $\theta$ & $\kappa=5/72$ throughout; Part~IV: $\theta=\log3/\log4$; elsewhere $\theta$ is an angle.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\addtocounter{table}{-1}% the uncaptioned longtable must not consume a table number
\endgroup

\section*{Provenance and merge decisions}\label{dsasm:sec:provenance}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Provenance and merge decisions}
Four manuscripts of the session bundle of Reports 1--243, all dated 2~October 2026, each headed ``REPORT $N$''; none names an author or a tool, none carries ``prepared for private review'' wording, and none pins a ProveIt commit or names a repository path. They arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} and were placed by \texttt{612787fb4} (batch~104).
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~I}, bundle Report~128, archive \file{A005163_Full_Leading_Equivalent_and_Individual_Amplitude_Source.zip} (\file{report128.tex} with eight files \file{sections/01_main.tex}--\file{08_references.tex}, 27 pages). The base: its text became this \file{article.tex}. It ships Report~124's TeX and PDF byte for byte as \file{earlier_input/} (checked at placement). Contributes the full equivalent at every fugacity, the companion limit, the common calibration amplitude, the boundary theorem, the Szeg\H{o}-type proposition, the amplitude-aware inverse and the cumulant constants.
\item \textbf{Part~II}, bundle Report~124, archive \file{A005163_Exact_Logarithmic_Scale_and_Inverse_Thresholds_Source.zip} (\file{report124.tex}, 34 pages). Contributes the shared exact prerequisites, the operator theory that Part~I uses, the bounded remainder, the trace limit, the inverse with the $\log\log$ term, the holomorphic pressure, bounded cumulant errors and the explicitly centred CLT.
\item \textbf{Part~III}, bundle Report~121, archive \file{A005163_Linear_Growth_Fugacity_Pressure_and_Limit_Laws_Source.zip} (\file{report121.tex}, 27 pages). Contributes the Jacobi-matrix route to the pressure, the linear coefficient, the root measure, Newton inequalities, Hoeffding, the actual-mean and local CLTs and the large deviations.
\item \textbf{Part~IV}, bundle Report~120, archive \file{A005163_Diagonal_Roots_Growth_Bounds_and_Inverse_Brackets_Source.zip} (\file{report120.tex}, 15 pages). Contributes the Jensen and chord inequalities on the roots, the projected two-step contraction, the certified constants $p,q$ and the two-ceiling inverse.
\end{itemize}
Where the merge had to choose:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Order and base.} Strength order, with Report~128 as base (the placement decision): its theorem implies every growth statement of the others. Logical order would put Report~124 first, since Part~I depends on it; the Guide says so, and Part~I's references to Report~124 are internal.
\item \emph{Shared sections.} Printed once, as listed in the Guide: Report~124's Sections~2, 4, 5 (Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration}) stand for Reports~121's and~120's Sections~2, 4, 5; Report~121's Section~3 (Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:consequences}) for Report~120's Section~3; Report~124's Section~3 (Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}) for Report~121's Section~9 except its two additional equations. The differences, checked by \texttt{diff} of the delivered sources, are: in Reports~120 and~121 the stability proof ends ``This proves the structural part of Theorem~[main]'' and writes $\eps$ for $\eps_n$; Report~120's closing non-claim of that section is longer (printed in Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:stability}) and Report~121's is Report~124's; Report~120's logarithmic calibration writes $d$ for $d_{\rm ASM}$; Report~121's interlacing section writes the root formula \eqref{dsasm:log:rootmean} without the definition of $M_n$, has the identities \eqref{dsasm:pr:adjderivative}--\eqref{dsasm:pr:meanbracket} with their paragraph, and has a different last sentence (printed in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing}; the first of these differences, recorded there, was added to this list on 5~October 2026, after the independent check).
\item \emph{Citations of Report~124 in Part~I.} The eight \verb|\cite{r124}| of Report~128 became references to Part~II, with section numbers converted (Report~124's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+9$); the item list (I1)--(I4) of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:main} and the bibliography entry keep the delivered numbers beside the converted ones. Wherever Part~I says ``the earlier article'', ``the earlier proof'' or ``the supplied article'', it means Part~II.
\item \emph{Front matter and bibliography.} The four title blocks and abstracts are recorded at the head of their Parts; one table of contents replaces four. The four bibliographies are merged into one, keeping every detail of every entry (where two manuscripts annotate an entry differently, both annotations are kept, each with its Part). The write added no reference.
\item \emph{Write additions.} This front matter, the reading conventions of each Part, Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, the pointer sections of Parts~III and~IV, and the dated notes. No theorem was added; the notes contain short deductions, each stated with its justification (listed in the README).
\end{enumerate}

\section*{What was checked, and what was not}\label{dsasm:sec:trust}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What was checked, and what was not}
The report is unrefereed and not formalized. At intake (dossier of batch~104, 5~October 2026) the four manuscripts were read in full, and the following were checked independently of the delivered programs. Exact counts from an independent Pfaffian and leading-minor computation of the kernel of \cite[(4.1)]{bfk} reproduce the twenty OEIS terms in Report~120's fixtures and extend A005163 exactly to $n=177$; $Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}$ ($n\le39$), $Z_{2m}(\sqrt3)=4^mO_m$ ($m\le20$) and $Z_n(c)Z_{n+1}(c)=c2^nA_{n+1}$ ($n\le39$) hold exactly. The exact polynomials $P_n$, $n\le31$, are monic with $P_n(0)>0$ and only negative real roots, and satisfy weak interlacing, the Newton inequalities and $\max|M_n-M_{n-1}|=1$ at the fugacities tested. Quadratures confirm $\mu(\Log(1+\delta w))=2f(3+\delta)$ to twenty digits (the input of \eqref{dsasm:scalarreallimit}) and that the Stieltjes transform of Part~III's root density is $f'(t)$. The boundary limit \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget} at $s=\frac12,1,3$, the calibration constant $C_{\rm ASM}/C_{\rm cal}^2=\sqrt{2/3}$ (30 digits), the constant $\sqrt2\,3^{-1/4}$ of \eqref{dsasm:calcoefficient}, the expansion \eqref{dsasm:inverseexpanded}, the relations $m=\mathcal DB$, $v=\mathcal Dm$, Part~III's Legendre transform and rate endpoints, and the mean bracket \eqref{dsasm:pr:meanbracket} were checked by hand or numerically. The delivered verification suites pass on copies (README). Nothing tested failed, and no claim of the four manuscripts was found to be wrong.

What was \emph{not} done: \textbf{Part~I's operator-theoretic Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion} and~\ref{dsasm:sec:smoothing}--\ref{dsasm:sec:edge} were read for structure and every displayed algebraic identity that could be checked, but were not re-derived line by line at intake}; the same holds for the analytic estimates of Part~II's Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:fredholm}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:complex}, on which Part~I rests. The limit theorems are therefore the manuscripts' proofs, corroborated numerically, and not an independent verification. The external inputs (Borcea--Br\"and\'en, Krattenthaler, Rosengren, Frenzen--Wong, Simon, Breuer--Duits, Casper--Zurri\'an, Alseidi--Margaliot--Garloff, Cao--Li--Lin, DLMF) were not re-checked against their sources, except Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan: on 5~October 2026 the write checked against the HTML text of arXiv:2309.08446v3 that version~3 is dated 1~October 2026 and that its equations (4.1), (4.11), (5.10) (Proposition~5.1), (11.3)--(11.6), (11.8) (Conjecture~11.1) and Theorem~11.2 are as the manuscripts use them. The finite checks of the delivered suites and of the dossier corroborate identities; none proves an asymptotic statement, and the numerical amplitude estimates of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further} are uncertified. An independent adversarial check made after the write is recorded at the end of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further} (dated note, 5~October 2026).

\section*{Relation to neighbouring reports and formal projects}\label{dsasm:sec:neighbours}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Relation to neighbouring reports and formal projects}
No other report of the collection treats alternating sign matrices, DSASMs, A005163 or the paper \cite{bfk}: a search of the repository on 5~October 2026 found only a bibliography item (Bressoud's \emph{Proofs and Confirmations}) in a $q$-Pochhammer monograph of the Fabius tree and a library-search log line naming A005163 in \file{a271619-strict-twice-partitions}. Nothing here answers or continues another report, and no reciprocal note is needed. Placement in the collection confers no formal status: no Lean or Rocq file in the repository treats alternating sign matrices, and no statement of this report is formalized.
\clearpage
\part{The full leading equivalent and individual amplitudes}\label{dsasm:part}
\parteqs{}
\partsource{Bundle Report~128, archive \file{A005163_Full_Leading_Equivalent_and_Individual_Amplitude_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{report128.tex} with the eight modules \file{sections/01_main.tex}--\file{sections/08_references.tex} (27 pages), dated 2~October 2026. Delivered title block: ``REPORT 128 / The full leading equivalent for diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices / Individual fugacity amplitudes and finite-section boundary limits / 2 October 2026 / A proof with an unchanged earlier-input appendix and offline replay''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes; section, statement and equation numbers are those of the delivery. Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further} is added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~I & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$P(s)$ the pressure; $P_n$ a projection (degree $<n$ in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}, first $n$ coordinates in Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion} and~\ref{dsasm:sec:edge}) & Parts~II--IV: $P_n(t)$ also the parity-reduced polynomial\\
$E_n(t)$, $E(t)$ companion remainder and its limit & Part~II: the number $E_n=E_n(1)$; Part~IV: $E_n=\log(P_n(3)/P_n(1))$\\
$B(s)$ linear coefficient; $\mathcal B$, $B$, $B_n$, $B_e$ operators & Part~III: $\beta(s)=B(s)$\\
$M_n$ modulation by $e^{-in\theta}$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion}) & Parts~II, III: $M_n(s)$ the mean\\
$m(y)$, $v(F)$, gamma phase $v$, $f_j$, $f(\theta)$ & Parts~I--III: $m(s)$, $v(s)$ slopes; $f(t)$ pressure\\
``the earlier article / proof / supplied article'' & Part~II (Report~124)\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
For the number $a_n$ of diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices,
A005163, we prove a full-sequence equivalent
$a_n\sim C e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}$, where
$\alpha=\tfrac12\log(3\sqrt3/4)$ and $\beta=\tfrac14\log3$.
The positive constant is characterized by convergent Fredholm determinants
and a convergent scalar Fourier series. More generally, the partition
function at every positive diagonal fugacity has a common individual
amplitude, with a locally holomorphic limit on the right half-plane.
There are two distinct new steps: convergence of the companion determinant
remainder, and a quantitative finite-section boundary theorem that removes
the alternating ambiguity in adjacent products. The latter retains the
leading Galerkin correction and uses an exact Christoffel norm identity
for weighted continuous-Hahn tightness. We give the amplitude-aware inverse
threshold with a vanishing remainder before integer ceilings, and the
constant terms of all fixed diagonal cumulants. No numerical amplitude,
explicit rate of convergence, inverse-power correction to the general
count, or all-orders expansion is claimed.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[Reading Part~I against Part~II, 5~October 2026]
Report~128 was delivered with Report~124 as an unchanged ``earlier input'' (\file{earlier_input/report124.tex} and \file{.pdf}, byte-identical to the delivered Report~124). That copy is not shipped here: Report~124 is printed in full as Part~\ref{dsasm:log:part}. The eight citations \verb|\cite{r124}| of the delivery are references to Part~II below, and Report~124's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+9$ here. Where Part~I speaks of ``the earlier article'', ``the earlier proof'', ``the supplied earlier proof'', ``the earlier exact identities'', ``the earlier interlacing argument'' or ``the earlier global kernel bound'', the result meant is in Part~II; the list (I1)--(I4) in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:main} locates them. Part~I's equivalent is the leading term of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan's Conjecture~11.1; see ``The leading equivalent and Conjecture~11.1'' in the front matter for exactly what is and is not proved.
\end{writenote}
\section{Definitions, theorem and proof dependencies}\label{dsasm:sec:main}
An alternating sign matrix has entries in $\{-1,0,1\}$, alternating
nonzero entries along each row and column, and all row and column sums
one. It is diagonally symmetric when it equals its transpose. Write
\[
 a_n=|\DS(n)|,\qquad S(A)=|\{i:A_{ii}\ne0\}|,\qquad
 Z_n(s)=\sum_{A\in\DS(n)}s^{S(A)},\qquad Z_0(s)=1.
\]
Thus $a_n=Z_n(1)$ and $a_n$ is A005163. All logarithms of positive
numbers are natural. Put
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:constants}
 c=\sqrt3,\quad \alpha=\tfrac12\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4},\quad
 \beta=\tfrac14\log3,\quad \kappa=\tfrac5{72},\quad
 b=\tfrac34\log3-\tfrac12\log2.
\end{equation}
On $\HH_+=\{\Re s>0\}$ define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pressure}
 P(s)=\frac{(s+1)^2}{2(s+2)},\qquad
 B(s)=b+\tfrac12\Log P(s).
\end{equation}
Here $\Log P(s)=2\Log(s+1)-\Log(s+2)-\log2$, with the indicated
right-half-plane logarithms. In particular $P(c)=1$ and $B(1)=\beta$.
The companion polynomial and its centered logarithm are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:companiondefs}
 D_n(t)=\frac{Z_n(\sqrt t)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt t)}{\sqrt t},\quad
 f(t)=\Log P(\sqrt t),\quad
 E_n(t)=\Log\frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}-nf(t),
\end{equation}
where $t\in\Omega_t=\C\setminus(-\infty,0]$ and the square root
has positive real part. The quotient defining $D_n$ is interpreted
polynomially. Its logarithm is normalized to vanish at $t=3$.

\begin{theorem}[Full leading equivalent]\label{dsasm:thm:main}
There is a holomorphic function $E$ on $\Omega_t$, real on $(0,\infty)$,
with $E(3)=0$, such that $E_n\to E$ locally uniformly. Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Ccal}
 C_{\rm cal}=
 \frac{3^{11/72}\pi^{1/6}e^{\zeta'(-1)/6}}
      {2^{1/24}\Gamma(1/3)^{1/3}}>0
\end{equation}
and, on $\HH_+$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:amplitude}
 C(s)=C_{\rm cal}\exp\{E(s^2)/2\}
       (s/c)^{1/2}P(s)^{-1/4}.
\end{equation}
The powers use the analytic logarithms normalized at $s=c$.
Then $C$ is nowhere zero and positive for $s>0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:fullequivalent}
 Z_n(s)=C(s)e^{\alpha n^2+nB(s)}n^{-\kappa}(1+o(1))
\end{equation}
locally uniformly on $\HH_+$, along the full integer sequence.
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:aequiv}
 a_n\sim C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}e^{E(1)/2}
               e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}.
\end{equation}
A convergent operator/Fourier expression for $E(t)$ is given in
\eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}; it defines the constant without a numerical fit.
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Conjecture~11.1 and the earlier Parts, 5~October 2026]
Formula \eqref{dsasm:aequiv} is the leading term of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan's Conjecture~11.1, equation~(11.8) of \cite{bfk}, whose constant $C$ is $C_*=C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}e^{E(1)/2}$ here; the conjecture's finer terms are not proved (see ``The leading equivalent and Conjecture~11.1'' in the front matter, and Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}). The theorem supersedes the two-sided bounds \eqref{dsasm:log:bounds} of Part~II (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main}): the normalized counts $a_ne^{-\alpha n^2-\beta n}n^{\kappa}$ converge, to $C_*$. It also supersedes the free energy \eqref{dsasm:pr:freeenergy} with error $o(n)$ of Part~III and the envelopes \eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes} of Part~IV; at $s=1$, $B(1)=\beta$ lies strictly between Part~IV's $p$ and $q$. Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main} does not restate Part~II's calibration trace limit \eqref{dsasm:log:firstmain}.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{What is supplied in the reader bundle}
The unchanged earlier article (Report~124, printed here as Part~\ref{dsasm:log:part}), supplied both as a PDF and
as its complete TeX source in the bundle's \texttt{earlier\_input}
folder, proves the bounded-remainder stage. Its scope statements are
historical: its failure to assert a limit is strengthened here. All
new convergence, calibration and boundary steps are proved below;
no unpublished proof note is a required reader input.

For precision, these are the earlier results used, with their locations
in that supplied article:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(I\arabic*)}]
\item Sections 2--4 (here Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration}): imaginary-axis zeros of $Z_n$, negative-real zeros
of $D_n$, cross-size interlacing, the exact adjacent determinant and
its finite Pascal reduction, and
$Z_n(c)Z_{n+1}(c)=c2^n A_{n+1}$, where $A_N=|\ASM(N)|$.
\item Sections 6--8 (here Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:jacobiconversion}): the exact degree-space compression
$D_n(t)/D_n(3)=\det(I+(t-3)P_n\mathcal B P_n)$, the bounded operator
$\mathcal B$, its unweighted decomposition $C_0+\mathcal E$ with
$0\le C_0\le I/3$, $\mathcal E\in\Stwo$,
$\mathcal S=(\mathcal E+\mathcal E^*)/2\in\Sone$, and the locally
uniform nonzero relative determinant $\mathcal A(t)$.
The exact continuous-Hahn/Fourier/Jacobi transform and the gamma-phase
comparison are included there.
\item Sections 9--11 (here Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:mean}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap}): the normalized Jacobi expansion, its endpoint
and derivative bounds, the mean formula
$\tr(J_nFJ_n)=(n+1)\pi^{-1}\int_0^\pi F+o(1)$ for endpoint-vanishing
$F$ with $\int |F|/\min(\theta,\pi-\theta)<\infty$, the global
angular kernel bound $|K_n(\theta,\phi)|\le C/|\theta-\phi|$, the
finite tilted-projection variance bound, and the Hilbert--Schmidt
weighted Hardy wave comparisons.
\item Sections 12 and 14 (here Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:transfer} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:complex}): the convergent trace-class cross factor
$\Xi(t)$, the compact-uniform scalar bound
$\log T_n(t-3)-2nf(t)=O_K(1)$, the analogous companion bound, and
local uniform bounds on adjacent individual logarithmic differences.
\end{enumerate}
These are theorems and exact identities proved in Part~\ref{dsasm:log:part}, rather
than assertions supplied by finite checks. We restate the definitions
needed to apply them. The additional published ingredients are the
ASM/OSASM product asymptotics in \cite{bfk}, the continuous-Hahn
normalization and generating function \cite{cll,dlmf}, and the
\emph{polynomial} Jacobi-statistic central limit theorem \cite{bd}.
We state precisely where each is used and prove the necessary extensions.

\subsection{The two logically separate limits}
An adjacent-product limit does not alone determine the individual
amplitudes: a residual $L+(-1)^n d$ has a constant adjacent sum.
Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:companion} first proves $E_n\to E$.
Sections~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary}--\ref{dsasm:sec:edge} then prove the distinct
boundary-ratio limit
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Ftarget}
 F_n(s):=\frac{sZ_n(s)/Z_{n+1}(s)}{cZ_n(c)/Z_{n+1}(c)}
 \longrightarrow \frac{s/c}{\sqrt{P(s)}}.
\end{equation}
The finite-section proof is at the $1/n$ coefficient scale. In
particular, it does not substitute an infinite resolvent for a finite
one merely from strong convergence. Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:consequences}
combines the two limits, proves the inverse threshold and derives
constant-order cumulants.
\section{Convergence of the companion remainder}\label{dsasm:sec:companion}
We use $\delta=t-3\in\Omega=\C\setminus(-\infty,-3]$.
All analytic logarithms in this section vanish at $\delta=0$.
Distinguish the original real-line weight $w_y$ in
Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:boundary} from the angular multiplier
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:wang}
 w(\theta)=\frac1{1+2\cosh(\xi(\theta)/3)},\quad
 \xi(\theta)=2\log\cot(\theta/2),\quad s_0(\theta)=\sqrt{w(\theta)}.
\end{equation}
Thus $0\le w\le1/3$ and $w=O(d^{2/3})$ for
$d(\theta)=\min(\theta,\pi-\theta)$.
Let $U:\ell^2(\N_0)\to L^2(0,\pi)$ synthesize the real normalized
Jacobi angular basis with parameters $(4/3,2/3)$, and let $H$ be the
orthogonal Hardy projection obtained from the negative real half-line
under Fourier and angular transformation. Explicitly it is unitarily
conjugate to the Fourier image of multiplication by $\one_{(-\infty,0)}$.
Write $P_n$ for the first $n$ coordinate projection and $J_n=UP_nU^*$.
Set
\[
 X=M_{s_0}HU,\quad Y=M_{s_0}(I-H)U,\quad
 A_n=P_nX^*XP_n,\quad B_n=P_nY^*YP_n.
\]
Here $A_n,B_n$ act on $\Ran P_n$. Define
\[
 Q_n(\delta)=\det(I+\delta A_n)=\det(I+\delta B_n),\quad
 S_n(\delta)=\det(I+\delta(A_n+B_n)),
\]
\[
 T_n(\delta)=\det_{\Ran J_n}(I+\delta J_nM_wJ_n).
\]
Equality of the two single determinants follows from complex
conjugation in the real Jacobi basis, which interchanges $H$ and $I-H$.
The supplied earlier proof gives, locally uniformly on $\Omega$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:inheritedfactors}
 \frac{D_n(t)/D_n(3)}{Q_n(t-3)}\longrightarrow\mathcal A(t),
 \qquad \frac{S_n(t-3)}{T_n(t-3)}\longrightarrow\Xi(t).
\end{equation}
For clarity, their complete operator definitions are
\begin{align}
 \mathcal A(t)&=\det{}_2\!\left(I+\delta(I+\delta C_0)^{-1}\mathcal E\right)
 \exp\!\left\{\delta\tr[(I+\delta C_0)^{-1}\mathcal S]\right\},
 \label{dsasm:Arel}\\
 \Xi(t)&=\det\!\left[(I+\delta\mathcal D)(I+\delta M_w)^{-1}\right],
 \quad \mathcal D=HM_wH+(I-H)M_w(I-H).
 \label{dsasm:Xidef}
\end{align}
In \eqref{dsasm:Arel} the real-line operators are exactly those defined in
\eqref{dsasm:kernelreal} below. The difference $\mathcal D-M_w$ is trace
class; the two factors are invertible on $\Omega$. Both $\mathcal A$
and $\Xi$ are nowhere zero, normalized to one at calibration, and
positive on $t>0$. The gamma-phase comparison has limiting relative
determinant one, which is already included in the first limit in
\eqref{dsasm:inheritedfactors}.

\subsection{A moving-edge mixed determinant limit}
The weighted-wave result of Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap}, gives bounded maps
\[
 X_0e_j=\frac{s_0(\theta)e^{-i(j+3/2)\theta+ic_0}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},
 \qquad Y_0e_j=\frac{s_0(\theta)e^{i(j+3/2)\theta-ic_0}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},
 \quad c_0=11\pi/12,
\]
with $X-X_0,Y-Y_0\in\Stwo$. Importantly, $XY^*=0$ exactly,
since $UU^*=I$ and $H(I-H)=0$.
Define $M_n$ to be multiplication by $e^{-in\theta}$ and define the
Hilbert--Schmidt operator $K$ by its kernel
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Kedge}
 K(\theta,\phi)=\frac{s_0(\theta)s_0(\phi)}{2\pi}
 \frac{e^{2ic_0-(3i/2)(\theta+\phi)}}{1-e^{-i(\theta+\phi)}}.
\end{equation}
At either singular corner its square is bounded by
$C\theta^{2/3}\phi^{2/3}/(\theta+\phi)^2$ or its reflection,
which is integrable after setting $\theta=ru$, $\phi=r(1-u)$.
Thus $K\in\Stwo$.

The geometric sum gives the exact identity
\[
 X_0P_nY_0^*=K-M_nKM_n.
\]
Since $M_n\rightharpoonup0$ by the Riemann--Lebesgue lemma and $K$
is compact, $M_nKM_n\to0$ strongly. Taking the strong limit gives
$X_0Y_0^*=K$. Set $E_X=X-X_0$, $E_Y=Y-Y_0$. Finite-rank approximation
in $\Stwo$ gives
\[
 XP_nY^*-X_0P_nY_0^*=E_XP_nY^*+X_0P_nE_Y^*
       \longrightarrow E_XY^*+X_0E_Y^*=-K
\]
in Hilbert--Schmidt norm. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:movingK}
 K_n:=XP_nY^*=-M_nKM_n+o_{\Stwo}(1).
\end{equation}
In particular $\tr(A_nB_n)=\|K_n\|_2^2\to\|K\|_2^2$.
The moving term in \eqref{dsasm:movingK} is retained; it does not tend to
zero in Hilbert--Schmidt norm.

Let $E_I$ extend functions on $(0,\pi)$ by zero to the circle
$(0,2\pi)$. Let $P_+^\circ$ project onto circle modes $j\ge1$ and
$P_-^\circ$ onto $j\le-1$, and set $G_\pm=M_{e^{\pm3i\theta/2}}$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:edgeAB}
 A_e=M_{s_0}G_-E_I^*P_+^\circ E_IG_+M_{s_0},\qquad
 B_e=M_{s_0}G_+E_I^*P_-^\circ E_IG_-M_{s_0}.
\end{equation}
The strict mode cutoffs follow from $n-j=1,\ldots,n$ in the finite
wave sums, and yield
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:diagonallimits}
 M_n^*XP_nX^*M_n\longrightarrow A_e,\qquad
 M_nYP_nY^*M_n^*\longrightarrow B_e
\end{equation}
strongly. Here is why the fixed Hilbert--Schmidt errors do not affect
these limits. For fixed $h$, $P_nX^*M_nh$ is bounded and weakly null:
pairing with $g$ reduces to pairing $M_nh$ with $XP_ng\to Xg$ in
norm. Compactness of $E_X$ then gives
$E_XP_nX^*M_nh\to0$, and compactness also gives $E_X^*M_nh\to0$.
Expand $XP_nX^*-X_0P_nX_0^*=E_XP_nX^*+X_0P_nE_X^*$ to obtain
the first claim. The other is identical with reversed modulation.
Both limits are positive and bounded by $I/3$.

For $0\le T\le I/3$, $(I+\delta T)^{-1}$ is uniformly bounded
for $\delta$ in compact subsets of $\Omega$. Strong convergence
in \eqref{dsasm:diagonallimits} gives strong convergence of these resolvents,
locally uniformly on each fixed vector. A finite parameter net and
the resolvent identity justify this uniformity.

Apply Sylvester's determinant identity to the block map with entries
$XP_n$ and $YP_n$, and take its upper-left Schur complement. With
\[
 R_{X,n}=(I+\delta XP_nX^*)^{-1},\qquad
 R_{Y,n}=(I+\delta YP_nY^*)^{-1},
\]
one obtains, with the order of factors as written,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:schur}
 \frac{S_n(\delta)}{Q_n(\delta)^2}
 =\det[I-\delta^2R_{Y,n}K_n^*R_{X,n}K_n].
\end{equation}
Conjugate the product on the right by $M_n$, and use
\eqref{dsasm:movingK}. Products of two Hilbert--Schmidt factors are trace
class, so all terms containing its $o_{\Stwo}(1)$ error tend to zero
in trace norm, uniformly on compact parameter sets. The main term is
\[
 \delta^2(M_nR_{Y,n}M_n^*)K^*(M_n^*R_{X,n}M_n)K.
\]
Strong resolvent convergence and finite-rank approximation of $K$
now give locally uniform trace-norm convergence. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Psidef}
 \frac{S_n(\delta)}{Q_n(\delta)^2}\longrightarrow\Psi(\delta):=
 \det\!\left[I-\delta^2(I+\delta B_e)^{-1}K^*
                              (I+\delta A_e)^{-1}K\right].
\end{equation}
Every finite ratio is nonzero on $\Omega$, and $\Psi(0)=1$.
Hurwitz's theorem makes $\Psi$ nowhere zero. It is positive for
real $\delta>-3$, as the nonzero limit of positive ratios. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:mixedlimit}
 2\log Q_n(\delta)-\log S_n(\delta)\longrightarrow-\Log\Psi(\delta)
\end{equation}
locally uniformly, with the common normalization at zero. Alternatively,
the modulated positive block matrices have strong limit
$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}A_e&-K\\-K^*&B_e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$
bounded by $I/3$; its Schur complement directly proves invertibility
of the limiting factor in \eqref{dsasm:Psidef}.

\subsection{The scalar constant: a polynomial theorem and its extension}
For an angular function $F(\theta)=g(\cos\theta)$ put
\[
 \mu(F)=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi F(\theta)\,d\theta,\quad
 F_k=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi F(\theta)\cos(k\theta)\,d\theta,\quad
 v(F)=\sum_{k\ge1}kF_k^2.
\]
For real $F$, the squares in $v$ are ordinary real squares.

\begin{proposition}\label{dsasm:prop:scalarconstant}
If $F$ is real and angular H\"older of exponent
$1/2<\gamma\le1$, with $F(0)=F(\pi)=0$, then $v(F)<\infty$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:scalarconstant}
 \log\det_{\Ran J_n}(J_ne^gJ_n)
     =(n+1)\mu(F)+\tfrac12v(F)+o(1).
\end{equation}
Here $g$ denotes multiplication by the function whose angular form is $F$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The rank-$n$ Jacobi polynomial ensemble has density proportional to
its squared Vandermonde times the Jacobi weight. Andreief's finite
identity gives
\[
 \mathbb E e^{X_n(g)}=\det(J_ne^gJ_n),\quad
 \mathbb E X_n(g)=\tr(J_ngJ_n),\quad
 \Var_n(g)=\|(I-J_n)gJ_n\|_2^2.
\]
The earlier global kernel bound gives for every real angular
$C^\eta$ function $H$, $\eta>1/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:varianceholder}
 \Var_n(H)=\tfrac12\iint(H(\theta)-H(\phi))^2
                      |K_n(\theta,\phi)|^2\,d\theta\,d\phi
 \le C_\eta[H]_{C^\eta}^2,
\end{equation}
independently of $n$. Endpoint vanishing is unnecessary for this bound.

The polynomial input is Corollary 2.2 of Breuer--Duits
\cite{bd}: if the recurrence matrix has a Laurent right limit,
every fixed real polynomial statistic, centered by its exact mean,
converges to a centered Gaussian with variance
$\sum_{k\ge1}k\widehat p_k\widehat p_{-k}$, where the Fourier
coefficients are those of the polynomial applied to the Laurent symbol.
The fixed Jacobi recurrence here has off-diagonal limit $1/2$ and
diagonal limit zero, hence the unique full-sequence symbol
$(z+z^{-1})/2$. Its coefficients are bounded and the support is compact.
Thus this theorem applies to every real polynomial $p$, with variance
$v(p(\cos\theta))$. Only the polynomial theorem is used; its separate
$C^1$ extension is not applied to the fractional endpoint symbol.

A weak CLT alone is insufficient for the determinant. To obtain
exponential integrability, let $E_r=e^{rg/2}$ and
$T_r=J_ne^{rg}J_n$ on $\Ran J_n$. The isometry
$E_rJ_nT_r^{-1/2}$ has range projection
$Q_r=E_rJ_nT_r^{-1}J_nE_r$. For any bounded real multiplier $a$,
commutation with $E_r$ gives the exact identity
\[
 (I-Q_r)aE_rJ_nT_r^{-1/2}
   =(I-Q_r)E_r(I-J_n)aJ_nT_r^{-1/2}.
\]
For $r\ge0$, taking Hilbert--Schmidt norms gives
$\Var_{rg}(a)\le e^{r\operatorname{osc}(g)}\Var_n(a)$.
Taylor's integral formula for the logarithmic moment generating
function, also applied to $-g$, therefore yields for real $u$
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:exponentialbound}
 \log\mathbb E e^{u(X_n(g)-\mathbb EX_n(g))}
 \le \tfrac12u^2e^{|u|\operatorname{osc}(g)}\Var_n(g).
\end{equation}
For fixed polynomial $p$, the bound at $u=2$ and
\eqref{dsasm:varianceholder} prove uniform integrability of
$e^{X_n(p)-\mathbb EX_n(p)}$. The polynomial CLT consequently implies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:polynomialG}
 G_n(p):=\log\mathbb E e^{X_n(p)-\mathbb EX_n(p)}\longrightarrow v(p)/2.
\end{equation}

We next justify extension uniformly in $n$. For real $g,h$ put
$a=g-h$ and $g_u=h+ua$, $0\le u\le1$. Differentiation of the
finite determinant and then integration along its tilt path give
\[
 \frac{d}{du}G_n(g_u)
  =\mathbb E_{g_u}X_n(a)-\mathbb E_0X_n(a)
  =\int_0^1\Cov_{rg_u}(X_n(a),X_n(g_u))\,dr.
\]
Tilted covariance Cauchy--Schwarz and the preceding ordered identity
show
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Gcontinuity}
 \left|\frac{d}{du}G_n(g_u)\right|
 \le e^{\operatorname{osc}(g_u)}
       \sqrt{\Var_n(a)\Var_n(g_u)}.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{dsasm:varianceholder}, $G_n$ are uniformly Lipschitz on bounded
real $C^\eta$ sets for $\eta>1/2$.

Choose $1/2<\eta<\gamma$. Extend $F$ evenly to the circle.
Its Fej\'er convolutions $F_m$ are even trigonometric polynomials,
converge uniformly, and have bounded $C^\gamma$ seminorms.
The elementary increment estimate
\[
 [H]_{C^\eta}\le C\|H\|_\infty^{1-\eta/\gamma}
                        [H]_{C^\gamma}^{\eta/\gamma}
\]
proves convergence in $C^\eta$. Subtract
$a_m+b_m\cos\theta$ with
$a_m=(F_m(0)+F_m(\pi))/2$ and
$b_m=(F_m(0)-F_m(\pi))/2$. The corrected functions $Q_m$ vanish
at both endpoints, are polynomials in $\cos\theta$, and converge
to $F$ in $C^\eta$, since $a_m,b_m\to0$.

The circle $H^{1/2}$ difference-quotient identity, first verified by
Fourier orthogonality for trigonometric polynomials, identifies
$\sum |k||\widehat H_k|^2$ with a fixed positive multiple of
\[
 \iint\frac{|H(\theta)-H(\phi)|^2}
                   {\sin^2((\theta-\phi)/2)}\,d\theta\,d\phi.
\]
It is bounded by $C_\eta[H]_{C^\eta}^2$. Thus $v(F-Q_m)\to0$ and
$v(Q_m)\to v(F)$ by weighted Cauchy--Schwarz. Equations
\eqref{dsasm:polynomialG} and \eqref{dsasm:Gcontinuity}, first with $n\to\infty$
and then $m\to\infty$, give $G_n(g)\to v(F)/2$.
Finally the earlier Jacobi mean theorem applies because
$F=O(d^\gamma)$ at the endpoints. It gives
$\mathbb EX_n(g)=(n+1)\mu(F)+o(1)$, proving
\eqref{dsasm:scalarconstant}.
\end{proof}

Apply this proposition to
\[
 F_\delta(\theta)=\Log(1+\delta w(\theta)),\qquad
 F_{\delta,k}=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi F_\delta(\theta)\cos(k\theta)\,d\theta.
\]
For real $\delta>-3$ it is real, endpoint-vanishing and $C^{2/3}$,
and the exact pressure integral from Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:mean}, is
$\mu(F_\delta)=2f(3+\delta)$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:scalarreallimit}
 \log T_n(\delta)-2nf(3+\delta)
 \longrightarrow 2f(3+\delta)+\tfrac12\sum_{k\ge1}kF_{\delta,k}^2.
\end{equation}
The earlier complex scalar bound makes the left side locally bounded
and holomorphic on $\Omega$. Vitali's theorem extends this convergence
locally uniformly over $\Omega$. The displayed series is its analytic
expression there, with squares, not absolute squares. Indeed for
$1/2<\eta<2/3$ the map $\delta\mapsto F_\delta$ is holomorphic
as a $C^\eta$-valued map, by the uniform lower bound for
$|1+\delta u|$, $0\le u\le1/3$, on compact parameter sets.
Its image on a compact set is compact in the weighted Fourier
$\ell^2$ space. Finite-coordinate projections then have uniformly
vanishing tails on this image. The bilinear Cauchy--Schwarz inequality
proves local uniform convergence and holomorphy of the square series.
The real identity and analytic uniqueness prove the claimed expression.

\subsection{The explicit limit and branch normalization}
Combine \eqref{dsasm:inheritedfactors}, \eqref{dsasm:mixedlimit}, and
\eqref{dsasm:scalarreallimit}. Every constituent logarithm is normalized at
$t=3$, and their locally uniform limits give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Eexplicit}
 \boxed{\ E(t)=\Log\mathcal A(t)+\tfrac12\Log\Xi(t)
       -\tfrac12\Log\Psi(t-3)+f(t)
       +\tfrac14\sum_{k\ge1}kF_{t-3,k}^2.\ }
\end{equation}
This proves $E_n\to E$ locally uniformly on $\Omega_t$.
At $t=3$ every term is zero. For positive $t$ the three determinant
factors are positive, so $E(t)$ is finite and real. Notice both the
minus sign of the mixed factor and the scalar coefficient $1/4$:
one first solves $S_n/Q_n^2\to\Psi$ for $\log Q_n$, and then
halves the scalar variance term. These fix the amplitude normalization.
As a separate normalization check, for the polynomial $p(x)=x$ the
only positive cosine coefficient is $1/2$, so $v(p)=1/4$ and the
centered logarithmic determinant tends to $1/8$. Directly the finite
variance is the square of the single Jacobi recurrence coefficient
crossing the degree-$n$ boundary, which tends to $1/4$.
\section{Calibration and the exact boundary resolvent}\label{dsasm:sec:boundary}
\subsection{A common calibration amplitude on both parities}
The earlier exact identities give
\[
 Z_{2m}(c)=2^m\prod_{k=1}^m\frac{A_{2k}}{A_{2k-1}},\qquad
 Z_n(c)=c2^{n-1}A_n/Z_{n-1}(c)\quad(n\text{ odd}).
\]
Let $O_m$ denote the number of off-diagonally symmetric ASMs of
order $2m$. The classical products in \cite{bfk} are
\[
 A_N=\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{(3i+1)!}{(N+i)!},\qquad
 O_m=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{(6i-2)!}{(2m+2i)!}.
\]
Successive ratios give
\[
 \frac{O_m}{O_{m-1}}
 =\frac{(6m-2)!(2m)!}{(4m)!(4m-2)!}
 =\frac12\frac{A_{2m}}{A_{2m-1}}.
\]
For example, the old denominator factors in the $O_m$ quotient
telescope to $(4m-2)!/(2m)!$. The initial products equal one,
so $Z_{2m}(c)=2^{2m}O_m$ exactly.

The published product asymptotics \cite[Equations (11.3)--(11.6)]{bfk},
using the even-order OSASM branch, state
\begin{align*}
 A_n&\sim C_{\rm ASM}e^{2\alpha n^2}n^{-2\kappa},\\
 2^nO_{n/2}&\sim C_{\rm cal}e^{\alpha n^2+bn}n^{-\kappa}
                    \quad(n\text{ even}),\\
 C_{\rm ASM}&=
 \frac{2^{5/12}\pi^{1/3}e^{\zeta'(-1)/3}}
      {3^{7/36}\Gamma(1/3)^{2/3}}.
\end{align*}
These are known product asymptotics, not an assumption about DSASM
amplitudes. Substitution in the odd exact identity gives the odd
constant
\[
 C_{\rm odd}=(c/2)e^{b-\alpha}C_{\rm ASM}/C_{\rm cal}.
\]
Now $b-\alpha=\tfrac12\log2$ and
$C_{\rm ASM}/C_{\rm cal}^2=\sqrt{2/3}$ by the displayed constants.
Therefore $C_{\rm odd}=C_{\rm cal}$, proving the full-sequence
calibration
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:calibrationequiv}
 Z_n(c)\sim C_{\rm cal}e^{\alpha n^2+bn}n^{-\kappa}.
\end{equation}
Only this equivalent is needed below; no finite-size correction is
transferred to a noncalibrated fugacity.
\begin{writenote}[A common calibration amplitude, 5~October 2026]
This answers the caveat of Parts~II--IV that the calibration amplitudes of the two parities are not matched (Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration}, ``without matching parity amplitudes''; Part~IV, Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:main}, ``No equality of the two parity-dependent calibration amplitudes is asserted or used''): both parities have the amplitude $C_{\rm cal}$. The input is the published product asymptotics; at intake $C_{\rm ASM}/C_{\rm cal}^2=\sqrt{2/3}$ was confirmed to 30 digits and $\log Z_n(c)-\alpha n^2-bn+\kappa\log n-\log C_{\rm cal}$ was $-8.9\cdot10^{-4}$ and $-8.7\cdot10^{-4}$ at $n=39,40$, decaying like $1/n$ on both parities.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{The fixed operator, basis and source}
Put $h=\sqrt3$, $a=\pi/(3h)$, $p(y)=y^2+3/4$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:realdefinitions}
 w_y(y)=\frac{p(y)}{2(2\cosh(2ay)-1)},\quad
 m(y)=\frac1{1+e^{2ay}},\quad
 \varphi(u)=\frac1{3(1+2\cosh(2au))}.
\end{equation}
Let $\psi_j$ be the real orthonormal polynomials in
$\mathcal H=L^2(w_y\,dy)$ whose leading coefficients are
$(-1)^j/\sqrt{H_j}$. The degree-$<n$ projection is $P_n$.
On polynomials put
\[
 (Tg)(y)=\int_\R e^{au}\varphi(u)g(y+u)\,du,\qquad \mathcal B=mT.
\]
The boundedness of $\mathcal B$ is part of the earlier proof.
The unweighted transform of $\mathcal B$ has kernel
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:kernelreal}
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}(y,x)=g_0(y)\varphi(x-y)h_0(x),\quad
 g_0=\sqrt{w_y}\,m e^{-ay},\quad h_0=e^{ay}/\sqrt{w_y},
 \quad C_0(y,x)=\one_{y<0}\varphi(x-y)\one_{x<0}.
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal E=\widetilde{\mathcal B}-C_0$ is the operator in
\eqref{dsasm:Arel}. The polynomial inverse of $T$ is
\[
 (\mathcal Kg)(y)=g(y)+e^{i\pi/3}g(y+ih)+e^{-i\pi/3}g(y-ih).
\]
The exact degree-space determinant is
$D_n(t)/D_n(3)=\det(I+(t-3)P_n\mathcal B P_n)$.

Define $d_j=\sqrt{(j+1)(j+2)}$, $h_j=j!(3)_j$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Hnorm}
 H_j=2(27/16)^j
       \frac{j!(3)_j(5/3)_j(7/3)_j}{(3/2)_j(5/2)_j}.
\end{equation}
The affine source $q_t\in\mathcal H$ is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:qsource}
 q_t(y)=\frac{A_t e^{2ay}+(-y-t+3/2)e^{ay}/\sqrt2+C_t
           +[(1-t)y/2-t+3/2]e^{-ay}/\sqrt2}{p(y)\cosh(ay)},
\end{equation}
where $A_t=\sqrt{3/2}(t+1)/4$ and $C_t=\sqrt{3/2}(t-2)$.
Write $q_t=q_3+(t-3)q^{(1)}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:gamma}
 \gamma_j(t)=\left\langle\psi_j,
   (I+(t-3)P_{j+1}\mathcal B^*P_{j+1})^{-1}P_{j+1}q_t\right\rangle,
 \qquad a_j^{\rm src}=\langle\psi_j,q_3\rangle.
\end{equation}
The inner product is linear in its second argument. The inverse in
\eqref{dsasm:gamma} acts on the finite-dimensional range, never on an
unjustifiably substituted infinite section.

\begin{proposition}[Exact all-parity boundary formula]\label{dsasm:prop:boundary}
For every $n\ge1$ and $s>0$, with $t=s^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:boundaryexact}
 \frac{sZ_n(s)}{Z_{n+1}(s)}
 =(-1)^{n-1}d_{n-1}\sqrt{h_{n-1}/H_{n-1}}\,\gamma_{n-1}(t).
\end{equation}
It follows that $F_n(s)=\gamma_{n-1}(s^2)/a_{n-1}^{\rm src}$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:calcoefficient}
 a_j^{\rm src}\sim\frac{(-1)^j\sqrt2\,3^{-1/4}}{j+1}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We supply the borders and source normalization explicitly.
Let
\[
 K_t(u,v)=\frac{v-u}{1-uv}
       \left(t+\frac{(1+u)(1+v)}{1-u-v}\right),\qquad
 M_{ij}=[u^iv^j]K_t(u,v).
\]
The source Pfaffian formula \cite[Equation (4.1)]{bfk}
and the earlier adjacent determinant give
$H_n=(M_{i,j+1})_{0\le i,j<n}$ with $\det H_n=D_n(t)$.
Set $b_i=M_{i0}$ and $v_t=te_0-b$; directly
$b_0=0$, $b_1=-(t+1)$ and $b_i=-2$ for $i\ge2$.
For even $n$, replacing the last column of $H_n$ by $b$ and cycling
it to the front gives
$-\det(H_n)e_{n-1}^{\mathsf T}H_n^{-1}b=Z_n(s)^2$.
The $e_0$ cofactor vanishes because its complementary skew matrix
has odd size. For odd $n$ the full skew determinant vanishes, while
the $e_0$ complementary skew minor indexed $1,\ldots,n-1$ has
determinant $(Z_n(s)/s)^2$. Including the cofactor signs in either
case proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:cofactor}
 sZ_n(s)/Z_{n+1}(s)=e_{n-1}^{\mathsf T}H_n^{-1}v_t.
\end{equation}

Use the finite matrices $S$ (lower shift), $L_{ij}=\binom ij$,
$E_{ii}=(-1)^i$, $\Pi_{ij}=\binom{i+j}{i}$, and $T_n(g)=g(S)$.
The earlier finite Pascal factorization, or direct multiplication
of the displayed generating functions, is
\[
 H_n=T_n(B_0)LEJ_n(t)EL^{\mathsf T},\quad
 J_n(t)=I+T_n(G_t)\Pi,
\]
where
\[
 B_0(z)=\frac{(z-2)(z+1)(2z-1)}{(1-z)(z^2-z+1)},\quad
 G_t(z)=\frac{(t-4)z^2+(4-t)z+t-1}{(2-z)(1-z)^2(1+z)}.
\]
The last row of $L^{-\mathsf T}$ is $e_{n-1}^{\mathsf T}$.
The generating function of $v_t$ is $V_t(z)=t+(t+1)z+2z^2/(1-z)$,
so the binomial transform
$(1-z)^{-1}[V_t/B_0](-z/(1-z))$ simplifies to
\[
 w_t(z)=\frac{(t-z)(z^2-z+1)}{(2-z)(1-z)^2(1+z)}.
\]
Consequently \eqref{dsasm:cofactor} equals
$(-1)^{n-1}e_{n-1}^{\mathsf T}J_n(t)^{-1}w_t^{(n)}$,
where the vector contains its first $n$ coefficients.

Put $D=\diag(d_j)$, $A=D^{-1}SD$ and
$C=D^{-1}VD$ with $V_{ij}=\binom{i+j+2}{i}$.
For $r(z)=1-z+z^2$, $q(z)=2+z-z^2$, the exact finite identity is
$D^{-1}J_nD=I+C+(t-3)[r(A)/q(A)]C$.
Multiplying by $q(A)/r(A)$ gives the old row-action Gram matrix
$F_n^\phi=\mathcal K_n^{\rm row}(I+C)+(t-3)C$.
The forcing is especially simple:
\[
 (q/r)w_t=(t-z)/(1-z)^2,\qquad
 p_i(t)=[t(i+1)-i]/d_i.
\]
Thus \eqref{dsasm:cofactor} becomes
$(-1)^{n-1}d_{n-1}e_{n-1}^{\mathsf T}(F_n^\phi)^{-1}p(t)$.

Let $\phi_j=u_j/\sqrt{h_j}$ be the Meixner--Pollaczek basis from
Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}, with
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}u_j(y)z^j/j!=r(z)^{-3/2}e^{y\eta(z)},\qquad
 \eta'(z)=-1/r(z),\quad\eta(0)=0.
\]
The lower-triangular basis change $\psi=U_n\phi$ has last diagonal
entry $\sqrt{h_{n-1}/H_{n-1}}$. Congruence gives
$F_n^\psi=U_nF_n^\phi U_n^{\mathsf T}=\mathcal K_n^{\rm row}
+(t-3)M_n$, where $(M_n)_{ij}=\langle\psi_i,m\psi_j\rangle$.
The matrix $(\mathcal K_n^{\rm row})^{-1}M_n$ is exactly the matrix
of $P_n\mathcal B^*P_n$ in column coordinates. If
$\ell_t(\phi_i)=p_i(t)$, its transformed source has entries
$\ell_t(T\psi_i)$. We now identify their genuine representing vector.

Define the exponentially decaying density
\[
 \nu_t(y)=\frac{(y+t-3/2)e^{ay}+[(1-t)y+(3-t)/2]e^{-ay}}
                    {2\sqrt2\cosh(3ay)}.
\]
The integral
$\int e^{\eta y}/[2\cosh(3ay)]\,dy=(h/2)\sec(h\eta/2)$
and its derivative yield, for $x=h\eta/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Lsource}
 L_t(\eta):=\int e^{\eta y}\nu_t(y)\,dy
 =\frac3{\sqrt2}\frac{3t\cos x+\sqrt3(2-t)\sin x}
                            {[1+2\cos(2x)]^2},\quad |\Re\eta|<2a.
\end{equation}
The inverse change is
$z=-2\sin x/(\sqrt3\cos x-\sin x)$ and
$r(z)=3/(\sqrt3\cos x-\sin x)^2$.
Substitution gives
$r(z)^{-3/2}L_t(\eta(z))=(t-z)/[\sqrt2(1-z)^2]$.
Since $\sqrt{h_i}=i!d_i/\sqrt2$, coefficient comparison proves
$\ell_t(g)=\int\nu_tg$ on polynomials.

Set
$\rho_t(y)=\int e^{au}\varphi(u)\nu_t(y-u)\,du$.
Exponential tails justify Fubini for each polynomial and show
$\ell_t(T\psi_i)=\int\psi_i\rho_t$.
Its Laplace transform on $-2a<\Re\eta<a$ is
\[
 \int e^{\eta y}\rho_t(y)\,dy
   =\frac{L_t(\eta)}{1+2\cos(h\eta+\pi/3)}.
\]
Applying the same sech integral and derivative at the shifts
$2a,a,0,-a$ shows that its inverse is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:rhosource}
 \rho_t(y)=\frac{A_te^{2ay}+(-y-t+3/2)e^{ay}/\sqrt2+C_t
                +[(1-t)y/2-t+3/2]e^{-ay}/\sqrt2}{2\cosh(3ay)}.
\end{equation}
Fourier uniqueness identifies this expression with the convolution.
The identity
\[w_y=p\cosh(ay)/(2\cosh(3ay))\]
shows that its quotient by $w_y$ is
exactly \eqref{dsasm:qsource}. At positive infinity
$\rho_t(y)=A_te^{-ay}+O((1+y)e^{-2ay})$; at negative infinity
$\rho_t(y)=O((1+|y|)e^{2ay})$. As
$w_y(y)\sim(y^2/2)e^{-2a|y|}$, one has
$|\rho_t|^2/w_y=O(y^{-2})$ on the positive tail and exponential
decay on the negative one. Thus $q_t\in\mathcal H$.
The basis change and finite inverse formula now prove
\eqref{dsasm:boundaryexact}. The norm \eqref{dsasm:Hnorm} also follows by multiplying
the exact recurrence
$H_j/H_{j-1}=3j(j+2)(3j+2)(3j+4)/[4(2j+1)(2j+3)]$, $H_0=2$.

Finally set $A_*=3\sqrt3/4=e^{2\alpha}$. Stirling in \eqref{dsasm:Hnorm}
gives
$\sqrt{h_j/H_j}\sim [8/(9\,3^{1/4})]A_*^{-j}$.
Equation \eqref{dsasm:calibrationequiv} gives
$cZ_n(c)/Z_{n+1}(c)\sim[c/(\sqrt2 A_*)]A_*^{-n}$.
Substitution in \eqref{dsasm:boundaryexact}, with $d_{n-1}\sim n$,
proves \eqref{dsasm:calcoefficient}, including its alternating sign.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Stable inverses and the analytic coefficient reduction}
For completeness, the finite nonnormal inverses are uniformly bounded
on compact subsets of $\Omega$. In the unweighted representation,
$\widetilde{\mathcal B}=C_0+\mathcal E$, and the earlier proof gives
\[
 \delta(I+\delta P_nC_0P_n)^{-1}P_n\mathcal EP_n
 \longrightarrow\delta(I+\delta C_0)^{-1}\mathcal E
\]
in Hilbert--Schmidt norm, locally uniformly. Nonvanishing of
$\mathcal A$ makes $I+\delta(I+\delta C_0)^{-1}\mathcal E$
invertible. The exact factorization of $I+\delta P_n\widetilde{\mathcal B}P_n$
then bounds its inverse for all sufficiently large $n$; each of the
remaining finitely many sizes is controlled by the negative-root
property. Taking adjoints gives the corresponding bound for
$\mathcal B^*$. The resulting strong convergence on fixed vectors
still does not control a coefficient after division by
$a_{n-1}^{\rm src}=O(1/n)$.

Every $F_n$ is holomorphic and nowhere zero on $\HH_+$, with
$F_n(c)=1$. The earlier interlacing argument gives, for
$R_n(s)=\Log[Z_n(s)/Z_n(c)]$,
\[
 |R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)|\le
             \frac{\pi|s-c|}{\sqrt{c\Re s}}.
\]
Thus $F_n$ and $1/F_n$ are locally uniformly bounded. A locally
convergent subsequence and all its derivatives converge by Cauchy's
formula. If every fixed Taylor coefficient at $t=3$ of
$\gamma_{n-1}(t)/a_{n-1}^{\rm src}$ converges, every subsequential
analytic limit has the same Taylor series at $s=c$. Analytic
uniqueness and normal-family compactness then prove full-sequence
locally uniform convergence on $\HH_+$.

For $k\ge1$ the relevant coefficient is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Taylorcoef}
 \frac{(-1)^k}{a_{n-1}^{\rm src}}\left\{
 \langle\psi_{n-1},(P_n\mathcal B^*P_n)^kP_nq_3\rangle
 -\langle\psi_{n-1},(P_n\mathcal B^*P_n)^{k-1}P_nq^{(1)}\rangle
 \right\}.
\end{equation}
This is the finite Neumann derivative at zero; it involves no
interchange of an infinite series and a size limit. We prove
existence of each fixed-power limit next. The established companion
limit will then evaluate the analytic boundary limit without any
need to compute those powers separately.
\section{Quantitative continuous-Hahn smoothing}\label{dsasm:sec:smoothing}
The required estimate is a column rate, stronger than compactness.
Let $f_j=\sqrt{w_y}\psi_j\in L^2(\R)$ and $b_y=\sqrt{p(y)}$.
Let $v$ denote the mixed-gamma phase in the exact Jacobi transform:
in the scaled variable $z=y/(3\sqrt3)$,
\[
 G(z)=\Gamma(5/6+iz)\Gamma(7/6-iz),\qquad v=G/|G|.
\]

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:thm:smoothing}
For the kernels in \eqref{dsasm:kernelreal},
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:smoothingrates}
 \sqrt j\|\mathcal E f_j\|_2\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \sqrt j\|(M_vC_0M_v^*-C_0)f_j\|_2\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The second assertion also holds with input $M_vf_j$ and with the
conjugate phase difference $C_0-M_v^*C_0M_v$. All statements hold
on the full degree sequence, irrespective of the consistent real
leading-sign convention for $\psi_j$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{An elementary fixed-variable asymptotic, including zero}
We first derive a local asymptotic rather than invoke a large-variable
formula excluding the origin. For positive $A,B$ put $s=A+B$ and
$W(z)=|\Gamma(A+iz)\Gamma(B+iz)|^2$.
The standard continuous-Hahn polynomial $p_j(z;A,B,A,B)$ has positive
leading coefficient and squared norm in $W(z)\,dz$
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Hahnnorm}
 h_j^{A,B}=\frac{2\pi\Gamma(j+2A)\Gamma(j+s)^2\Gamma(j+2B)}
              {(2j+2s-1)\Gamma(j+2s-1)j!}.
\end{equation}
The removable expression at $j=0,s=1/2$ is interpreted by
$(2s-1)\Gamma(2s-1)=\Gamma(2s)$. Neither parameter pair below
requires that convention. The classical generating function
\cite[DLMF 18.23.6]{dlmf} is
\[
 {}_1F_1(A+iz;2A;-it)\,{}_1F_1(B-iz;2B;it)
       =\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{p_j(z)t^j}{(2A)_j(2B)_j}.
\]
Euler's beta integral for ordinary ${}_1F_1$ (DLMF 13.4.1 together
with the normalization conversion 13.2.4) gives exactly
\begin{align}
 p_j(z)&=\frac{i^j\Gamma(j+2A)\Gamma(j+2B)}{j!W(z)}I_j(z),
 \label{dsasm:Hahnbeta}\\
 I_j(z)&=\int_0^1\!\int_0^1(v-u)^j
 u^{A+iz-1}(1-u)^{A-iz-1}
 v^{B-iz-1}(1-v)^{B+iz-1}\,du\,dv.\nonumber
\end{align}
Absolute convergence and boundedness of the entire exponential on
the integration square justify coefficient extraction. The norm
and polynomial conventions agree with \cite[Equations (1.1)--(1.4)]{cll}.

Uniformly for real $z$ in a compact set, the corners $(u,v)=(0,1)$
and $(1,0)$ yield
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:corners}
 I_j(z)=\Gamma(A+iz)\Gamma(B+iz)j^{-s-2iz}
  +(-1)^j\Gamma(A-iz)\Gamma(B-iz)j^{-s+2iz}+O_K(j^{-s-1}).
\end{equation}
To verify the error, at the first corner set $q=1-v$. The smooth
remaining factor equals $1+O_K(u+q)$. On the full triangle
$u,q\ge0$, $u+q\le1$ the leading Dirichlet integral is
\[
 \int u^{A+iz-1}q^{B+iz-1}(1-u-q)^j\,du\,dq
 =\frac{\Gamma(A+iz)\Gamma(B+iz)\Gamma(j+1)}
        {\Gamma(j+s+2iz+1)}.
\]
The $O(u+q)$ term has absolute integral $O_K(j^{-s-1})$.
Removing the portion outside a fixed corner neighborhood costs an
exponentially small error. The second corner is the conjugate version
with $(-1)^j$. Away from these corners $|v-u|\le1-\eps$; the beta
powers have integrable absolute values independent of real $z$.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:corners}, uniformly through $z=0$.

Let $F_j=\sqrt Wp_j/\sqrt{h_j^{A,B}}$ and
$\eta(z)=\Gamma(A+iz)\Gamma(B+iz)/|\Gamma(A+iz)\Gamma(B+iz)|$.
The gamma-ratio estimate in \eqref{dsasm:Hahnnorm} gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Hahnlocal}
 F_j(z)=\frac1{\sqrt{\pi j}}
 \left\{i^j\eta(z)j^{-2iz}+(-i)^j\overline{\eta(z)}j^{2iz}\right\}
          +O_K(j^{-3/2}).
\end{equation}
This additive uniform error remains valid at zeros and for odd
polynomials at zero. For compactly supported integrable $H$, squaring
and using Riemann--Lebesgue gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:localmeasure}
 \int H(z)jF_j(z)^2\,dz\longrightarrow\frac2\pi\int H(z)\,dz.
\end{equation}
For compactly supported $L^2$ tests, \eqref{dsasm:Hahnlocal} similarly
implies $\int H\sqrt jF_j\to0$. The oscillatory frequencies are
$2\log j$ and $4\log j$, respectively.

\subsection{A positive exact norm identity prevents escape of mass}
For the desired pair $(A,B)=(5/6,7/6)$ put $d_0=1/6$ and
$V(z)=|\Gamma(1/6+iz)\Gamma(5/6+iz)|^2$. Gamma recurrence gives
$W(z)=(z^2+d_0^2)V(z)$ exactly. Let $P_j^V,Q_j^W$ be the monic
orthogonal polynomials for $V,W$, with squared norms $H_j^V,H_j^W$.
Both have their degree parity. At $id_0$, the terminating
hypergeometric formula has upper parameter zero, so
\[
 P_j^V(id_0)=\frac{i^j(1/3)_j}{\binom{2j}{j}},\qquad
 r_j=-\frac{P_{j+2}^V(id_0)}{P_j^V(id_0)}
 =\frac{(j+1/3)(j+4/3)(j+1)(j+2)}{4(2j+1)(2j+3)}>0.
\]
The standard leading coefficient in this parameter pair is
$\binom{2j}{j}$. Direct divisibility and orthogonality prove the
quadratic Christoffel relations
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Christoffel}
 Q_j^W(z)=\frac{P_{j+2}^V(z)+r_jP_j^V(z)}{z^2+d_0^2},\qquad
 H_j^W=r_jH_j^V.
\end{equation}
Indeed parity gives the two zeros in the numerator; multiplying by
$z^2+d_0^2$ proves orthogonality against all lower degrees, and the
norm identity follows by pairing with the numerator.

Put $a_j^V=|P_j^V(id_0)|^2/H_j^V$. For $k\le j$ of the same parity,
$P_k^V-[P_k^V(id_0)/P_j^V(id_0)]P_j^V$ is divisible by $z^2+d_0^2$
with quotient of degree less than $j$. Orthogonality and monicity
therefore give
\[
 \langle Q_j^W,P_k^V\rangle_V
       =H_j^V P_k^V(id_0)/P_j^V(id_0).
\]
Opposite-parity pairings vanish. Expand $Q_j^W$ in the $V$ basis
and use \eqref{dsasm:Christoffel}. For the same normalized function $F_j$
as above this gives the positive exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:normidentity}
 \int\frac{F_j(z)^2}{z^2+1/36}\,dz
       =\frac1{r_ja_j^V}\sum_{\substack{0\le k\le j\\k\equiv j\ (2)}}a_k^V.
\end{equation}
The lowered norm formula \eqref{dsasm:Hahnnorm} is
$h_j^{1/6,5/6}=2\pi\Gamma(j+1/3)\Gamma(j+5/3)/(2j+1)$,
so
\[
 a_j^V=\frac{(2j+1)\Gamma(j+1/3)}
                  {2\pi\Gamma(1/3)^2\Gamma(j+5/3)}
      \sim\frac{j^{-1/3}}{\pi\Gamma(1/3)^2}.
\]
For either parity, elementary power-sum comparison yields
\[
 \sum_{\substack{k\le j\\k\equiv j\ (2)}}a_k^V
       \sim\frac{3j^{2/3}}{4\pi\Gamma(1/3)^2},\qquad r_j\sim j^2/16.
\]
Thus \eqref{dsasm:normidentity} proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:totalmassz}
 j\int\frac{F_j(z)^2}{z^2+1/36}\,dz\longrightarrow12.
\end{equation}
This uses a positive sum and no cancellation of asymptotic terms.

Let $k_0=3\sqrt3$, $y=k_0z$. Up to the real leading sign,
$f_j(y)=k_0^{-1/2}F_j(y/k_0)$ and
$p(y)=k_0^2(z^2+1/36)$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:totalmass}
 \|\sqrt j f_j/b_y\|_2^2\longrightarrow4/9.
\end{equation}
On each bounded interval \eqref{dsasm:localmeasure} gives the limiting
weighted mass $(2/(\pi k_0))\int dy/p(y)$, whose total over the real
line is also $4/9$. Subtracting the compact-interval limit from
\eqref{dsasm:totalmass} and then enlarging the interval proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:tightness}
 \lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{j\to\infty}
      j\int_{|y|>R}\frac{|f_j(y)|^2}{p(y)}\,dy=0.
\end{equation}
The sequence $u_j=\sqrt j f_j/b_y$ is bounded by \eqref{dsasm:totalmass}
and weakly null by the compactly supported test conclusion of
\eqref{dsasm:Hahnlocal}, followed by density. Multiplication by a fixed
bounded unimodular function preserves weak nullity and tightness.

\subsection{A local compactness principle and the original kernel}
If $A$ is bounded and $A\one_{[-R,R]}$ is compact for every $R$,
then a bounded, weakly null, $L^2$-tight sequence $u_j$ satisfies
$\|Au_j\|\to0$. Indeed the compact part tends to zero for fixed
$R$, and the other part is at most $\|A\|\|\one_{|y|>R}u_j\|$.
Taking limsup and then $R\to\infty$ proves the assertion.

Split off the rank-one positive-tail kernel
\[
 R_0(y,x)=\tfrac13G_0(y)r_0(x),\quad
 G_0(y)=g_0(y)e^{2ay},\quad
 r_0(x)=h_0(x)e^{-2ax}\one_{x\ge0}.
\]
Both factors lie in $L^2$. We claim that
$A_0=(\mathcal E-R_0)M_{b_y}$, with the multiplier absorbed in
the input kernel, is bounded and locally compact.
For $x,y<0$ set $r_x=e^{2ax}$ and $d_x=\sqrt{1-r_x+r_x^2}$.
The exact formulas in \eqref{dsasm:kernelreal} are
$g_0(y)=b_y/[\sqrt2(1+r_y)d_y]$ and
$h_0(x)=\sqrt2d_x/b_x$. The negative-quadrant kernel of $A_0$ is
\begin{align*}
 &\one_{x,y<0}(b_y-b_x)\varphi(x-y)\\
 &\quad+\one_{x,y<0}b_y\varphi(x-y)
                  \left[\frac{d_x}{(1+r_y)d_y}-1\right].
\end{align*}
The first is bounded by $|x-y|\varphi(x-y)$ since $b_y$ is
1-Lipschitz, so Schur's test applies. Restricting its input to a
bounded interval makes it square integrable. The second bracket is
$O(e^{2ax}+e^{2ay})$; exponential off-diagonal decay and these half-line
tails make that whole kernel square integrable.

For $x\ge0$ the kernel is
\[
 g_0(y)\left[\varphi(x-y)-\tfrac13e^{-2a(x-y)}\right]h_0(x)b_x.
\]
Here $h_0(x)b_x=O(e^{2ax})$. The bracket is $O(e^{-4a(x-y)})$
for $x\ge y$ and $O(e^{2a(y-x)})$ for $x<y$.
Use $g_0(y)=O(1+|y|)$ at $-\infty$ and
$O((1+y)e^{-4ay})$ at $+\infty$. The resulting bounds on the three
regions $y<0\le x$, $0\le y\le x$, and $0\le x<y$ are respectively
\[
 C(1+|y|)e^{4ay}e^{-2ax},\qquad
 C(1+y)e^{-2ax},\qquad C(1+y)e^{-2ay}.
\]
Each is square integrable, including the triangular regions.
For $x<0\le y$, the reference and rank-one kernels vanish;
the remaining bound $C(1+y)e^{-6ay}e^{2ax}$ is square integrable.
This proves the claim. The local compactness principle and
\eqref{dsasm:tightness} now show
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:minusRrate}
 \sqrt j\|(\mathcal E-R_0)f_j\|_2=\|A_0u_j\|_2\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}

The exceptional rank-one term uses the calibrated coefficient,
not a noncalibrated asymptotic. At $t=3$, \eqref{dsasm:qsource} simplifies to
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:q3simple}
 q_3(y)=\frac{\sqrt2[\sqrt3e^{ay}-y-3/2]}{p(y)}.
\end{equation}
Put $F=\sqrt{w_y}q_3$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:subtraction}
 b_y[r_0-\sqrt{2/3}F]\in L^2(\R).
\end{equation}
For $y\ge0$, set $\rho=e^{-2ay}$, $d=\sqrt{1-\rho+\rho^2}$.
Exactly $r_0=\sqrt2d/b_y$ and
$F=[\sqrt3-(y+3/2)e^{-ay}]/(b_yd)$, so the left expression in
\eqref{dsasm:subtraction} is
$\sqrt2(d-1/d)+\sqrt{2/3}(y+3/2)e^{-ay}/d$, which decays
exponentially. On the negative tail $r_0=0$ and $b_yF$ decays
exponentially up to a polynomial; all factors are bounded near zero.
Writing the function in \eqref{dsasm:subtraction} as $h_0^{\rm sub}$,
weak nullity and \eqref{dsasm:calcoefficient} give
\[
 \sqrt j\langle r_0,f_j\rangle
 =\sqrt{2/3}\sqrt j\langle F,f_j\rangle
      +\langle h_0^{\rm sub},u_j\rangle\longrightarrow0.
\]
The rank-one norm therefore tends to zero at the required scale.
Together with \eqref{dsasm:minusRrate} this proves the first assertion of
\eqref{dsasm:smoothingrates}.

\subsection{The phase correction has a compact weighted kernel}
The exact gamma phase in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:jacobiconversion}, satisfies, on $x,y<0$,
\[
 |v(y)/v(x)-1|\le
     \frac{C|x-y|}{(1+|x|)(1+|y|)}.
\]
Consequently for $D_v=M_vC_0M_v^*-C_0$,
\[
 |D_v(y,x)b_x|\le
       \frac{C\one_{x,y<0}|x-y|\varphi(x-y)}{1+|y|}.
\]
The right side is square integrable, by integrating in the difference
variable and then in $y$. Thus $D_vM_{b_y}$ is Hilbert--Schmidt.
Compactness applied to $u_j\rightharpoonup0$ proves
$\sqrt jD_vf_j\to0$. Apply the identical argument to $M_vu_j$ and
to the conjugate phase difference to obtain all the stated variants.
This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:smoothing}.
\section{A quantitative Jacobi--Hardy column comparison}\label{dsasm:sec:columns}
Continue with the angular notation \eqref{dsasm:wang}. Put $\gamma=2/3$,
$N_j=j+3/2$, $c_0=11\pi/12$, and
\[
 v_j^\pm(\theta)=e^{\pm i(N_j\theta-c_0)}/\sqrt{2\pi},\quad
 v_j=v_j^-+v_j^+,\quad Ue_j=u_j,\quad V_\pm e_j=v_j^\pm,\quad V=V_-+V_+.
\]
These synthesis maps are bounded (the wave maps are restricted circle
Fourier synthesis). Set $X=M_{s_0}HU$, $X_0=M_{s_0}V_-$,
$A=X^*X$, $T_0=X_0^*X_0$.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:thm:columns}
One has $\sqrt j\|(A-T_0)e_j\|_2\to0$.
After restoring the exact basis phase $i^j$, the Toeplitz model has
real symmetric entries $b_{i-k}$ where
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Toeplitzsymbol}
 b_r=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}w(x+\pi/2)e^{irx}\,dx,
 \qquad |b_r|\le C(1+|r|)^{-5/3}.
\end{equation}
For the original $\psi$-coordinate matrix of $\mathcal B$, denoted
$B$, and this Toeplitz matrix $T_b$, one therefore has
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:originalcolumns}
 \|(B-T_b)e_j\|=o((j+1)^{-1/2}).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\subsection{An endpoint Fourier lemma}
Suppose $k(\theta,\phi)$ is locally absolutely continuous in $\phi$,
with envelope $a_0(\theta)$ such that
\[
 |k|\le Ca_0(\theta)d(\phi)^{-1/2},\qquad
 |\partial_\phi k|\le Ca_0(\theta)d(\phi)^{-3/2}.
\]
Suppose that for each fixed interior $\theta$, the two scaled
expressions $d(\phi)^{1/2}k$ and $d(\phi)^{3/2}\partial_\phi k$
tend to zero at both endpoints. Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Fourierlittleo}
 \sqrt N\int_0^\pi k(\theta,\phi)e^{\pm iN\phi}\,d\phi\longrightarrow0
\end{equation}
pointwise, bounded in absolute value by $Ca_0(\theta)$.
Split at $1/N$ and $\pi-1/N$. The endpoint pieces are
$O(a_0/\sqrt N)$; one integration by parts on the middle gives
the same order from its boundary and derivative terms. For little-o,
first choose fixed small endpoint neighborhoods where the scaled
bounds are arbitrarily small for this $\theta$, and integrate by
parts on the remaining compact interval, giving $O(1/N)$ there.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:Fourierlittleo} for real $N\to\infty$.
Dominated convergence gives its $L^2(q(\theta)d\theta)$ version
whenever $\int q a_0^2<\infty$.

\subsection{Two explicit Hardy kernels}
Let $f(\theta)=\log\tan(\theta/2)$, so $f'=1/\sin\theta$.
The difference between $H$ and the compressed ordinary
negative-frequency Hardy operator $C_I$ has, apart from a fixed
factor $-i/(2\pi)$, the kernel
\[
 d_H(\theta,\phi)=
 \frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}
                         -\frac1{\theta-\phi}.
\]
The canceled diagonal is filled continuously. The explicit bounds are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Hardyest}
 |d_H|\le\frac C{\sqrt{d(\theta)d(\phi)}},\qquad
 |\partial_\phi d_H|\le\frac C{\sqrt{d(\theta)}d(\phi)^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
For completeness, in comparable left-endpoint scales set $\phi=\theta r$,
$1/2\le r\le2$. After extracting the scale, the canceled quotient
is smooth through $r=1$, and its required derivatives are uniformly
bounded; hence the bounds are $C/\theta$ and $C/\theta^2$.
In noncomparable scales use
$|f(\theta)-f(\phi)|\ge c|\log(\theta/\phi)|$, whose logarithm
is bounded away from zero. The ordinary Cauchy terms obey
\eqref{dsasm:Hardyest} separately there. At opposite endpoints the denominator
