is bounded below by
$c[1+|\log d(\theta)|+|\log d(\phi)|]$; mixed compact/endpoint
regions obey the same estimates. Direct differentiation uses
\[
 \partial_\phi\frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}
 =\frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}
       \left[-\frac{\cot\phi}{2}
                    +\frac{\csc\phi}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}\right].
\]
For fixed interior $\theta$, the logarithmic denominator gives the
two endpoint little-o conditions. Since $\int w/d$ and
$\int w^2/d$ are finite, the Fourier lemma proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Hardywaves}
 \sqrt j\|s_0(H-C_I)v_j^\pm\|_2\to0,
 \qquad \sqrt j\|w(H-C_I)v_j^\pm\|_2\to0.
\end{equation}

The commutator $[H,w]$ has, up to the same fixed factor, kernel
\[
 k_w(\theta,\phi)=(w(\phi)-w(\theta))
       \frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}.
\]
It obeys the Fourier lemma with square-integrable envelope
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:commutenvelope}
 a_0(\theta)=C\left\{d(\theta)^{\gamma-1/2}
       +\frac1{\sqrt{d(\theta)}(1+|\log d(\theta)|)}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Here $|w^{(r)}|\le C_rd^{\gamma-r}$ for $r=0,1,2$.
At comparable endpoint scales the canceled quotient is
$O(\theta^{\gamma-1})$ and its derivative is
$O(\theta^{\gamma-2})$. For $\phi\ge2\theta$ near the same
endpoint, use
\[
 \sup_{2\theta\le\phi\le\eps}
 \frac{\phi^\gamma+\theta^\gamma}{\log(\phi/\theta)}
 \le C\left\{\theta^\gamma+(1+|\log\theta|)^{-1}\right\}.
\]
This follows by putting $v=\log(\phi/\theta)$; the term
$\theta^\gamma e^{\gamma v}/v$ has no interior maximum.
The case $\phi\le\theta/2$ follows directly from
$|w(\phi)-w(\theta)|\le C\theta^\gamma$. Opposite endpoints use
the logarithmic sum denominator from \eqref{dsasm:Hardyest}; mixed
regions give the second summand in \eqref{dsasm:commutenvelope}.
Differentiation adds $d(\phi)^{-1}$, using the displayed derivative
formula and the derivative bounds for $w$. For fixed $\theta$ the
scaled expressions still tend to zero, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:commuterate}
 \sqrt j\|[H,w]v_j^-\|_2\to0.
\end{equation}
The borderline envelope square $d^{-1}/(1+|\log d|)^2$ is integrable.

\subsection{The direct wave defect}
The normalized Jacobi expansion proved from the uniform Bessel
formula in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:mean}, implies, for $R_j=u_j-v_j$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Rbounds}
 |R_j(\theta)|\le C\min\{1,[(j+1)d(\theta)]^{-1}\},\quad
 \|R_j\|_2=O(j^{-1/2}),\quad
 \|s_0R_j\|_2=O(j^{-(1+\gamma)/2}).
\end{equation}
On every compact interior interval $\sqrt jR_j$ tends uniformly
to zero; its global $L^2$ norm is bounded. It is therefore weakly
null. The earlier proof shows $[s_0,H]$ compact, so
\[
 \sqrt j\|s_0HR_j\|_2\to0
\]
by $s_0HR_j=H(s_0R_j)+[s_0,H]R_j$.
This uses compactness on a bounded weakly null sequence with the
scale already included.
The ordinary compressed Hardy tail estimate is
\[
 |C_Iv_j-v_j^-|\le C\{L(N_j\theta)+L(N_j(\pi-\theta))\},\qquad
 L(r)=\begin{cases}1+|\log r|,&r\le1,\\1/r,&r\ge1.\end{cases}
\]
Integration against $w=O(d^\gamma)$ makes its weighted norm
$O(j^{-(1+\gamma)/2})$. Together with \eqref{dsasm:Hardywaves} and
\eqref{dsasm:Rbounds}, this proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:directdefect}
 \sqrt j\|(X-X_0)e_j\|_2\to0.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The adjoint defect must be controlled separately}
We prove
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:dualdefect}
 \sqrt j\|(U-V)^*(wv_j^-)\|_{\ell^2}\to0.
\end{equation}
First, uniformly in $k$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:variation}
 \Var(wR_k)\le C,\qquad (wR_k)(0)=(wR_k)(\pi)=0.
\end{equation}
Here no uncontrolled remainder is differentiated. The exact angular
Jacobi derivative identity for parameters $\alpha_J,\beta_J$ is
\[
 u_k'=q_J(\theta)u_k-
       \sqrt{k(k+\alpha_J+\beta_J+1)}\,
                      u_{k-1}^{(\alpha_J+1,\beta_J+1)},
\]
where $q_J=[(2\alpha_J+1)\cot(\theta/2)
-(2\beta_J+1)\tan(\theta/2)]/4$.
Apply the separate uniform first-order expansions to the two families.
The shifted family has the same $N_k$ and phase $\Phi_k-\pi/2$;
the leading derivative terms cancel, while
$\sqrt{k(k+3)}-N_k=O(1/N_k)$. Consequently
\[
 |R_k'|\le C[d^{-1}+(N_kd^2)^{-1}]\quad(N_kd\ge1),\qquad
 |R_k'|\le CN_k\quad(N_kd<1).
\]
The latter uses the exact identity and endpoint powers $11/6,7/6$,
both greater than one. Integrating $w'R_k+wR_k'$ with
\eqref{dsasm:Rbounds} proves \eqref{dsasm:variation}.

The first Jacobi correction is
\[
 A_J(\theta)=\frac{(1/4-\alpha_J^2)\cot(\theta/2)
                  -(1/4-\beta_J^2)\tan(\theta/2)}4,
 \quad (\alpha_J,\beta_J)=(4/3,2/3),\quad\Phi_k=N_k\theta-c_0.
\]
The normalized expansion, with no $1/k$ amplitude term, gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:L1first}
 \|w[kR_k-\sqrt{2/\pi}A_J\sin\Phi_k]\|_1\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Indeed $wA_J\in L^1$. On $d<1/k$ both terms have integral
$O(k^{-\gamma})$; on the complement the uniform
$O((N_kd)^{-2})$ remainder, multiplied by $kw$, has the same bound.
Replacing $k/N_k$ by one adds $O(k^{-1})$.

Set $\beta_{kj}=\langle R_k,wv_j^-\rangle$. Integration by parts
in \eqref{dsasm:variation} gives $|\beta_{kj}|\le C/(j+1)$ uniformly
in $k$. By \eqref{dsasm:L1first}, $k\beta_{kj}$ is, uniformly in $j$,
a fixed linear combination of Fourier coefficients of $wA_J$ at
frequencies $k-j$ and $-(k+j+3)$, plus an error tending to zero
as $k\to\infty$. Fix $\eps>0$ and $M$. The contribution to
$j\sum_k|\beta_{kj}|^2$ from $k<\eps j$ is at most $C\eps$.
The window $|k-j|\le M$ contributes $O(M/j)$.
On $k\ge\eps j$ outside that window, Riemann--Lebesgue and the
uniform error in \eqref{dsasm:L1first}, together with
$\sum_{k\ge\eps j}k^{-2}=O((\eps j)^{-1})$, give a limsup
bounded by $C\eps^{-1}\sup_{|r|>M}|\widehat{wA_J}(r)|^2$.
Take $j\to\infty$, then $M\to\infty$, then $\eps\downarrow0$.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:dualdefect}.

Similarly $w$ vanishes at both endpoints and $w'\in L^1$.
Integration by parts and Riemann--Lebesgue give Fourier coefficients
$o(1/|r|)$ uniformly in the tail. The entries of $V_+^*(wv_j^-)$
have frequencies $k+j+3$, so summing their squares gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:plusdefect}
 \sqrt j\|V_+^*(wv_j^-)\|_{\ell^2}\to0.
\end{equation}
The exact decomposition
\[
 (H-I)wv_j^-=[H,w]v_j^-+w(H-C_I)v_j^-+w(C_I-I)v_j^-
\]
and \eqref{dsasm:Hardywaves}--\eqref{dsasm:commuterate} yield
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:weightedprojection}
 \sqrt j\|(H-I)(wv_j^-)\|_2\to0.
\end{equation}
The last ordinary Hardy term is $O(1/j)$ in $L^2$ because $2\gamma>1$.

Now put $D=X-X_0$. Exactly
\[
 A-T_0=X^*D+D^*X_0,
\]
\[
 D^*X_0e_j=U^*(H-I)(wv_j^-)
       +(U-V)^*(wv_j^-)+V_+^*(wv_j^-).
\]
Equations \eqref{dsasm:directdefect}, \eqref{dsasm:dualdefect},
\eqref{dsasm:plusdefect}, and \eqref{dsasm:weightedprojection} prove the first
claim of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:columns}.

\subsection{Returning to the original operator and restoring phase}
Let $S$ be unitary scaling to $z=y/(3\sqrt3)$, $\mathcal F$ the
unitary Fourier transform with kernel $e^{-iz\xi}$, and $J$ the
Fourier-to-angular coordinate unitary. With the scaled interpretation
of $v$, the exact transform $L=J\mathcal FS M_v$ satisfies
\[
 Lf_j=i^ju_j,\qquad
 J\mathcal FS C_0S^*\mathcal F^*J^*=HM_wH.
\]
The latter identity has no gamma phase. Therefore
\[
 L\widetilde{\mathcal B}f_j-(HM_wH)Lf_j
   =L\mathcal Ef_j+J\mathcal FS(M_vC_0-C_0M_v)f_j.
\]
The second term is the unitary image of
$(M_vC_0M_v^*-C_0)M_vf_j$. The two precise smoothing variants
in Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:smoothing} prove an $o(j^{-1/2})$ error.
Let $Q=\diag(i^j)$. The original coordinate matrix is
$Q^*U^*L\widetilde{\mathcal B}L^*UQ$, and the Hardy model is
$Q^*AQ$. Column norms are unchanged by the diagonal phases.
The raw wave Gram entry is
$(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^\pi w(\theta)e^{i(i-k)\theta}\,d\theta$.
Conjugating by $Q$ multiplies it by $i^{k-i}$; setting
$x=\theta-\pi/2$ proves exactly \eqref{dsasm:Toeplitzsymbol} and
\eqref{dsasm:originalcolumns}.

The corresponding circle symbol is $w(x+\pi/2)$ on
$|x|\le\pi/2$ and zero elsewhere. It is endpoint $C^{2/3}$,
with first and second derivative bounds $d^{-1/3},d^{-4/3}$.
Integrate once by parts, split at distance $1/|r|$ from each
support endpoint, and integrate the interior part once more by
parts. This proves $b_r=O(|r|^{-5/3})$.
The symmetry $w(\pi-\theta)=w(\theta)$ makes $b_r$ real and even.
The absolutely convergent Fourier series vanishes at $x=\pi$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:alternatingsum}
 \sum_{r\ge1}(-1)^rb_r=-b_0/2=\sqrt3/4-1/2,
 \qquad b_0=1-\sqrt3/2.
\end{equation}
The last value is the exact scalar mean already evaluated in
Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:mean}. All parts of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:columns}
are now proved.
\section{Fixed-source closure and the finite-section edge theorem}\label{dsasm:sec:edge}
\subsection{An invariant density class}
Choose $R$ with $2h<R<5h/2$. Let $\mathcal C$ consist of functions
$g$ whose density $\rho_g=w_yg$ is holomorphic on $|\Im y|<R$ and,
on every closed narrower strip, satisfies for some integer $M\ge0$
\begin{align}
 \rho_g(y)&=c_ge^{-ay}+O((1+\Re y)^Me^{-2a\Re y})
                                  &&(\Re y\to+\infty),\label{dsasm:densityclass}\\
 \rho_g(y)&=O((1+|\Re y|)^Me^{2a\Re y})
                                  &&(\Re y\to-\infty).\nonumber
\end{align}
Such functions lie in $\mathcal H$ by the same tail calculation
used for \eqref{dsasm:qsource}. Cauchy's formula supplies the corresponding
fixed-derivative estimates on narrower strips.

Both $q_3$ and $q^{(1)}$ belong to this class. In
\eqref{dsasm:rhosource} the affine numerator vanishes identically in $t$
at $y=\pm ih/2,\pm3ih/2$, as direct substitution shows. These
remove the first four zeros of $2\cosh(3ay)$; the next possible
poles are $\pm5ih/2$. The tails give
$c_{q_3}=\sqrt{3/2}$ and $c_{q^{(1)}}=c_{q_3}/4$.

The exact adjoint density formula is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:adjointdensity}
 \rho_{\mathcal B^*g}(y)
   =e^{ay}\int_\R\varphi(y-x)e^{-ax}m(x)\rho_g(x)\,dx.
\end{equation}
It follows directly from the unweighted kernel, first on the real
axis by absolute convergence. Write $F(x)=e^{-ax}m(x)\rho_g(x)$.
It is holomorphic on $|\Im x|<3h/2$, the nearest poles of $m$,
and its horizontal-strip tails are
\[
 F(x)=O(\operatorname{poly}(x)e^{-4a\Re x})\quad(+\infty),\qquad
 F(x)=O(\operatorname{poly}(|x|)e^{a\Re x})\quad(-\infty).
\]
The kernel is holomorphic for $|\Im u|<h$ and has tails
$O(e^{-2a|\Re u|})$ on narrower strips. To extend
\eqref{dsasm:adjointdensity} to $|\Im y|<5h/2$, choose a contour height
$v$ with $|v|<3h/2$ and $|\Im y-v|<h$. Overlaps agree by contour
shifting; the vertical sides vanish by the displayed tails.
A finite cover gives uniform estimates on each narrower closed strip.

The positive leading coefficient is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:leadingmoment}
 c_{\mathcal B^*g}=\tfrac13\int_\R e^{ax}m(x)\rho_g(x)\,dx.
\end{equation}
To see the error rigorously, subtract $e^{-2a(y-x)}/3$ from the
kernel over the entire chosen contour. For $\Re x\le\Re y$ its
remainder is $O(e^{-4a(\Re y-\Re x)})$; for $\Re x>\Re y$
bound the kernel and subtracted exponential separately. Combining
with the tails of $F$ gives a polynomial times $e^{-4a\Re y}$
before multiplication by $e^{ay}$. The borderline integration over
$0<\Re x<\Re y$ only adds one polynomial power. The final remainder
is $O(\operatorname{poly}(y)e^{-3a\Re y})$, stronger than needed.
The moment integrand in \eqref{dsasm:leadingmoment} is analytic in the
contour strip and decays at both ends, so its value is contour
independent. On the negative tail split at $\Re x=\Re y$ and
$\Re x=0$. The convolution is
$O(\operatorname{poly}(|y|)e^{a\Re y})$ before multiplication,
giving the required $e^{2a\Re y}$ bound. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:classclosure}
 \mathcal B^*\mathcal C\subset\mathcal C.
\end{equation}

Every member has the same leading coefficient shape:
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:shape}
 \langle\psi_j,g\rangle
   =\frac{c_g}{c_{q_3}}a_j^{\rm src}+o(1/(j+1)).
\end{equation}
Here is a proof of the little-o claim, including the endpoint domain.
Subtract $(c_g/c_{q_3})q_3$ and call the result $d_g$. Its density
has two-sided $\operatorname{poly}(y)e^{-2a|\Re y|}$ tails in the
strip. In $z=y/(3h)$ put
$G^\sharp(z)=\Gamma(5/6-iz)\Gamma(7/6+iz)$.
The exactly normalized Fourier input in the Jacobi transform is
$\sqrt{c_W}G(z)d_g(3hz)$, $c_W=81\sqrt3/(8\pi^2)$.
Since $w_y(3hz)$ is a constant times $G(z)G^\sharp(z)$, this input
is a fixed constant times $\rho_{d_g}(3hz)/G^\sharp(z)$.
The reciprocal gamma product is entire. Stirling on horizontal strips
makes this quotient, and all its fixed polynomial multiples, exponentially
decaying. Its holomorphy strip contains $\Im z=\pm2/3$, because
$R>2h$. Contour shifting for its Fourier transform $H_g$ yields
\[
 |H_g(\xi)|+|H_g'(\xi)|\le Ce^{-2|\xi|/3}.
\]
The angular image
$g_\theta(\theta)=\sqrt{2/\sin\theta}\,H_g(\xi(\theta))$
therefore satisfies
$g_\theta=O(d^{5/6})$ and $g_\theta'=O(d^{-1/6})$.
It belongs to $H_0^1(0,\pi)$ and
$\int |g_\theta|^2/d^2<\infty$. Hence it is in the quadratic-form
domain of the Friedrichs Jacobi angular operator, with potential
\[
 \frac{\alpha_J^2-1/4}{4\sin^2(\theta/2)}
 +\frac{\beta_J^2-1/4}{4\cos^2(\theta/2)},
 \qquad (\alpha_J,\beta_J)=(4/3,2/3).
\]
This is the realization with eigenfunctions $u_j$ and eigenvalues
$(j+3/2)^2$, also at the limit-circle endpoint.
Its form expansion gives
$\sum_j(j+3/2)^2|\langle\psi_j,d_g\rangle|^2<\infty$;
the unitary changes coefficients only by phases $i^j$.
Thus $j\langle\psi_j,d_g\rangle\to0$, proving \eqref{dsasm:shape}.
In particular these coefficients are $O((j+1)^{-1})$ and their
projection-tail norms are $O(n^{-1/2})$.
By \eqref{dsasm:classclosure}, all fixed iterates of both $q_3$ and
$q^{(1)}$ have this shape. The constants in \eqref{dsasm:leadingmoment}
retain the full operator, including its compact and rank-one pieces.

\subsection{An abstract edge theorem with nonlocal tails retained}
\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:thm:edge}
Let $B$ be bounded on $\ell^2(\N_0)$ and $T_b=T_b^*$ a Toeplitz
operator with entries $b_{i-k}$, where for some $\eps>0$
\[
 |b_r|\le C(1+|r|)^{-1-\eps},\qquad
 \|(B-T_b)e_i\|=o((i+1)^{-1/2}).
\]
Let $q$ be fixed and $u_\ell=(B^*)^\ell q$. Suppose for each fixed
$\ell\ge0$ that
\[
 (u_\ell)_i=\kappa_\ell a_i+o((i+1)^{-1}),\qquad
 a_i\sim c_*( -1)^i/(i+1),\quad c_*\ne0.
\]
Then for every fixed $\ell\ge0$ the full-sequence limit
\[
 \frac{[(P_nB^*P_n)^\ell P_nq]_{n-1}}{a_{n-1}}
\]
exists. Every fixed right-edge profile coordinate has a limit as well.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $Q_n=I-P_n$, $A_n=P_nB^*P_n$, and
$e_{n,\ell}=A_n^\ell P_nq-P_nu_\ell$. Exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:defectrecurrence}
 e_{n,0}=0,\qquad
 e_{n,\ell+1}=P_nB^*e_{n,\ell}-P_nB^*Q_nu_\ell.
\end{equation}
The coefficient hypothesis gives $\|Q_nu_\ell\|=O(n^{-1/2})$,
and boundedness of $B$ then gives
$\|e_{n,\ell}\|=O(n^{-1/2})$ for each fixed $\ell$.
Set $K=B-T_b$. If $\|v_n\|=O(n^{-1/2})$, the column hypothesis
implies, uniformly for $i\ge\eta n$, $\eta>0$ fixed,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:compacttail}
 |(K^*v_n)_i|\le\|Ke_i\|\|v_n\|=o(1/n).
\end{equation}
Uniformity follows because
$\sup_{i\ge\eta n}\sqrt{i+1}\|Ke_i\|\to0$.
This uses columns of $K$ to control coordinates of $K^*$, without
assuming an adjoint-column estimate.

Induction in \eqref{dsasm:defectrecurrence} gives the stronger pointwise bound
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pointwisedefect}
 \sup_{\eta n\le i<n}n|(e_{n,\ell})_i|\le C_{\ell,\eta}.
\end{equation}
For its Toeplitz part split the input at $\eta n/2$. On the upper
part use the preceding inductive bound and $\sum|b_r|<\infty$.
On the lower part the distance is at least $\eta n/2$;
Cauchy--Schwarz bounds the Toeplitz row tail by
$O(n^{-1/2-\eps})$, which times $\|e_{n,\ell}\|=O(n^{-1/2})$
is $O(n^{-1-\eps})$. The source tail $Q_nu_\ell$ has coordinates
$O(1/n)$ for all $i\ge n$, so its Toeplitz image is $O(1/n)$.
The two $K^*$ terms satisfy \eqref{dsasm:compacttail}. This proves
\eqref{dsasm:pointwisedefect}, starting from zero.

For each fixed $r\ge0$ let
$E_\ell(r)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(e_{n,\ell})_{n-1-r}/a_{n-1}$
when it exists. It is zero for $\ell=0$. Assuming it exists at
level $\ell$, the exact recurrence gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:edgeprofile}
 E_{\ell+1}(r)=\sum_{s\ge0}b_{s-r}E_\ell(s)
       -\kappa_\ell\sum_{m\ge0}b_{-r-1-m}(-1)^{m+1}.
\end{equation}
To justify all tails, on input indices $k\ge n/2$ the normalized
defect coordinates are uniformly bounded by \eqref{dsasm:pointwisedefect};
absolute summability permits a fixed-distance cutoff followed by its
removal. On $k<n/2$, the preceding row-tail estimate is
$O(n^{-1-\eps})=o(a_{n-1})$. On the exterior tail $k=n+m$, the
normalized source coordinates are uniformly bounded and converge,
for each fixed $m$, to $\kappa_\ell(-1)^{m+1}$. Dominated summation
therefore applies to the second series. The $K^*$ terms are
$o(a_{n-1})$ by \eqref{dsasm:compacttail}. Each limiting profile is bounded
by \eqref{dsasm:pointwisedefect} with $\eta=1/2$.
This proves existence inductively and gives
\[
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{[A_n^\ell P_nq]_{n-1}}{a_{n-1}}
       =\kappa_\ell+E_\ell(0).
\]
There is no infinite-power interchange in this argument.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Closing the analytic boundary limit}
Apply Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:edge} to the original $B$ in the $\psi$ basis,
using Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:columns} with $\eps=2/3$ and the source shape
\eqref{dsasm:shape}. Apply it separately to $q_3$ and $q^{(1)}$.
Every coefficient in \eqref{dsasm:Taylorcoef} therefore converges along
the full sequence. The normal-family argument following that formula
proves $F_n\to F$ locally uniformly on $\HH_+$.
The companion identity gives exactly
\[
 F_n(s)F_{n+1}(s)
  =(s/c)^2\frac{D_n(s^2)/D_n(3)}{D_{n+1}(s^2)/D_{n+1}(3)}.
\]
Since $E_n\to E$ locally uniformly, the right side tends to
$(s/c)^2/P(s)$. Thus $F(s)^2=(s/c)^2/P(s)$.
It is nonzero on $\HH_+$, and $F(c)=1$ selects the branch
$F(s)=(s/c)/\sqrt{P(s)}$. This proves \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget} without a
residual parity ambiguity.

\subsection{A nonzero leading Galerkin contribution as a check}
There is a useful independent check on the sign and scale.
Define
\[
 \tau_j=\frac{\langle B e_j,Q_{j+1}q_3\rangle}{a_j^{\rm src}},
\]
with source and basis now in coordinate space. The $B-T_b$ part is
$o(1)$ after division by $a_j^{\rm src}$, by
\eqref{dsasm:originalcolumns} and $\|Q_{j+1}q_3\|=O(j^{-1/2})$.
For the Toeplitz part, the calibration asymptotic makes
$a_{j+r}^{\rm src}/a_j^{\rm src}$ uniformly bounded for $r\ge1$
and gives its fixed-$r$ limit $(-1)^r$. Absolute summability yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:taulimit}
 \tau_j\longrightarrow\sum_{r\ge1}(-1)^rb_r=\sqrt3/4-1/2.
\end{equation}
Thus the original finite-projection tail is genuinely leading order.
It was retained in \eqref{dsasm:edgeprofile}; compactness alone would not
have justified discarding it.
\section{Individual amplitudes, inverse thresholds and cumulants}\label{dsasm:sec:consequences}
\subsection{Proof of the full equivalent}
Write $R_n(s)=\Log[Z_n(s)/Z_n(c)]$ on $\HH_+$, normalized at $c$.
The exact adjacent identity and the now-proved companion limit give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:adjacentsum}
 R_n(s)+R_{n+1}(s)=nf(s^2)+E_n(s^2)+\Log(s/c).
\end{equation}
The exact boundary definition gives
$R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)=\Log(s/c)-\Log F_n(s)$.
Because $F_n\to(s/c)e^{-f(s^2)/2}$ locally uniformly and all functions
are nonzero with the same normalization, their analytic logarithms
converge locally uniformly. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:adjacentdifference}
 R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)\longrightarrow\tfrac12f(s^2).
\end{equation}
Subtract \eqref{dsasm:adjacentdifference} from \eqref{dsasm:adjacentsum} and divide
by two. This yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:Rlimit}
 R_n(s)-\tfrac n2f(s^2)
 \longrightarrow\tfrac12E(s^2)+\tfrac12\Log(s/c)-\tfrac14f(s^2)
\end{equation}
locally uniformly. Adding the real full-sequence calibration
\eqref{dsasm:calibrationequiv} proves Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}, including its
complex local uniformity. All factors in \eqref{dsasm:amplitude} are positive
on the positive axis and nonzero on $\HH_+$. There are no zeros or
poles of $P$ there; the definitions at $c$ fix all roots uniquely.
At $s=1$, $P(1)=2/3$, so
$(1/c)^{1/2}P(1)^{-1/4}=2^{-1/4}$. Also
$b+\tfrac12\log(2/3)=\beta$. This verifies \eqref{dsasm:aequiv}.

\subsection{Amplitude-aware inverse count thresholds}
Let
\[
 C_*=C(1)=C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}e^{E(1)/2},\qquad
 N(T)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge T\},\quad y=\log T,\quad r=\sqrt{y/\alpha}.
\]
The minimum exists because \eqref{dsasm:aequiv} tends to infinity.
The counts are nondecreasing: $A\mapsto\diag(A,1)$ is an injection.
Only large $T$ is considered below.

\begin{corollary}\label{dsasm:cor:inverse}
Define the real center
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:zinverse}
 z_C(T)=r-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}
       +\frac{\kappa\log r+\beta^2/(4\alpha)-\log C_*}{2\alpha r}.
\end{equation}
There exists a nonnegative function $\eps(T)\to0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:inversebracket}
 \ceil{z_C(T)-\eps(T)/r}\ \le N(T)\le\ \ceil{z_C(T)+\eps(T)/r}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently the displayed center is
\begin{align}\label{dsasm:inverseexpanded}
 z_C(T)={}&\sqrt{\frac{\log T}{\alpha}}-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}
 +\frac{\kappa\log\log T}{4\sqrt{\alpha\log T}}\nonumber\\
 &+\frac{\beta^2/(4\alpha)-\log C_*-(\kappa/2)\log\alpha}
             {2\sqrt{\alpha\log T}}.
\end{align}
Thus the amplitude enters at the $1/\sqrt{\log T}$ scale, and the
remaining error is little-o at that scale before ceilings.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Set $g(x)=\alpha x^2+\beta x-\kappa\log x+\log C_*$ for $x>0$.
The full equivalent says $\log a_n=g(n)+\eta_n$ with $\eta_n\to0$.
On $[r/2,2r]$, $g'(x)=2\alpha x+\beta-\kappa/x\asymp r$.
Substitution of \eqref{dsasm:zinverse}, writing its last numerator as
$L_r=\kappa\log r+\beta^2/(4\alpha)-\log C_*$, gives
\[
 g(z_C(T))-y=O((\log r)/r)=o(1).
\]
Indeed the quadratic and linear contributions are
$y-\beta^2/(4\alpha)+L_r+O((\log r)^2/r^2)$,
while $-\kappa\log z_C=-\kappa\log r+O(1/r)$.
The leading terms cancel exactly.

The growth theorem first gives $N(T)\in[r/2,2r]$ for all sufficiently
large $T$ (the endpoints have different fixed quadratic multiples
of $y$). On this interval the tail supremum
$\sup_{n\ge r/2}|\eta_n|$ tends to zero.
Choose $\eps(T)>0$, tending to zero, larger by a fixed sufficient
factor than this supremum plus $|g(z_C)-y|$ and $1/r$.
The lower derivative bound then shows that every integer
$n<z_C-\eps/r$ has $\log a_n<y$, while the integer
$n=\ceil{z_C+\eps/r}$ has $\log a_n\ge y$.
Integers outside $[r/2,2r]$ are handled by the preliminary growth
comparison and monotonicity. These two statements are exactly
\eqref{dsasm:inversebracket}. Substituting $r=\sqrt{y/\alpha}$ and
$\log r=(\log y-\log\alpha)/2$ proves \eqref{dsasm:inverseexpanded}.
\end{proof}

The ceilings are essential: even a vanishing real error can decide
which adjacent integer is the threshold when the center is near an
integer. The theorem does not give a fixed explicit decay rate for
$\eps(T)$, nor does it justify discarding it inside a ceiling.
\begin{writenote}[Superseded inverses, 5~October 2026]
Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse} sharpens Part~II's Corollary~\ref{dsasm:log:inverseintro}: the centre \eqref{dsasm:zinverse} is Part~II's \eqref{dsasm:log:zdef} with the amplitude term $-\log C_*/(2\alpha r)$ added, and the error $C/r$ becomes $\eps(T)/r$ with $\eps(T)\to0$. Part~III's bracket \eqref{dsasm:pr:inversebound} (error $\eta(T)$ without the $\log r/r$ term) and Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{dsasm:env:inverse} (limiting width $(q-p)/(2\alpha)\approx0.1254$) are weaker. None of the four brackets is effective (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~4).
\end{writenote}


\subsection{Constant terms of every fixed diagonal cumulant}
For fixed $s>0$ assign probability $s^{S(A)}/Z_n(s)$ to a DSASM,
and let $S_n$ denote its number of nonzero diagonal entries.
Put $\mathcal D=s\,d/ds$ and $L(s)=\Log C(s)$, real on the
positive axis. Define
\[
 m(s)=\tfrac12-\frac1{(s+1)(s+2)},\qquad
 v(s)=\frac{s(2s+3)}{(s+1)^2(s+2)^2}>0.
\]
Then $\mathcal DB=m$ and $\mathcal Dm=v$.

\begin{corollary}\label{dsasm:cor:cumulants}
For each fixed $k\ge1$, locally uniformly on positive fugacity intervals,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:cumulantconstant}
 \operatorname{cum}_k(S_n)=n\mathcal D^{k-1}m(s)
                              +\mathcal D^kL(s)+o(1).
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{align*}
 \mathbb E_sS_n&=nm(s)+s^2E'(s^2)+\tfrac12-\tfrac12m(s)+o(1),\\
 \Var_s(S_n)&=nv(s)+2s^2E'(s^2)+2s^4E''(s^2)-\tfrac12v(s)+o(1).
\end{align*}
At $s=1$ the linear coefficients are $m(1)=1/3$ and $v(1)=5/36$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The locally holomorphic limit \eqref{dsasm:Rlimit} implies, on a small
complex disk about $z=0$,
\[
 \log\mathbb E_se^{zS_n}
 =n[B(se^z)-B(s)]+L(se^z)-L(s)+o(1)
\]
locally uniformly. Cauchy's formula makes every fixed derivative
of the error $o(1)$. Differentiating proves
\eqref{dsasm:cumulantconstant}. Inserting
$L(s)=\log C_{\rm cal}+E(s^2)/2+\tfrac12\Log(s/c)
-\tfrac14\Log P(s)$ gives the displayed first two constants.
\end{proof}
This strengthens the bounded cumulant errors in the earlier article.
It relies on complex-local convergence, rather than differentiating
pointwise real asymptotics.
\begin{writenote}[Answered questions, 5~October 2026]
Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants} strengthens Part~II's bounded errors \eqref{dsasm:log:cumulants} (that is the ``earlier article'' meant here) and Part~III's limits \eqref{dsasm:pr:cumulants}. It answers Part~III's question~2 (Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}), already answered by Part~II in the form $M_n(s)-nm(s)=O(1)$: the difference converges. The constants $E'(1)$ and $E''(1)$ are not evaluated; an uncertified estimate of $E'(1)$ is in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~6.
\end{writenote}


\section{Scope and reproducibility}\label{dsasm:sec:scope}
The result is a full leading equivalent at each fixed positive
fugacity, with a positive constant characterized by convergent
operators and a Fourier series. Convergence is locally uniform on
the right half-plane. It is not asserted uniformly as $s\to0$ or
$s\to\infty$, nor uniformly in an unbounded derivative order.
No numerical digits for $C(1)$, explicit rate for the little-o terms,
$n^{-2}$ correction, or all-orders conjecture are established here.
\begin{writenote}[Numerical amplitude, 5~October 2026]
Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan give a numerical estimate of $C=C(1)$ from the exact counts for $n\le1000$; an independent uncertified estimate from exact counts to $n=177$, consistent with it, and the evidence about the conjectured $n^{-2}$ and parity-dependent corrections are recorded in Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}. Neither is part of Report~128's claims.
\end{writenote}


The source bundle contains this article's modular TeX, its PDF,
the unchanged earlier-input article's PDF and TeX, a closed SHA-256
inventory, deterministic clean-build and repack scripts, and exact
finite checks with an adversarial input campaign. The finite checks
corroborate identities and normalization; they are not proofs of any
infinite-dimensional estimate, asymptotic convergence or numerical
amplitude. The analytical arguments above establish those limits.
The replay checks package immutability, normal and optimized Python
behavior, independent exact data, two byte-identical clean PDF builds,
and a closed deterministic ZIP round trip. Rebuilding needs only the
locally installed TeX distribution and Python; the scripts make no
network requests and install no dependencies.
\begin{writenote}[The source bundle here, 5~October 2026]
The bundle described in this paragraph was delivered as the archive named in the Part's heading. In this report the article's TeX is \file{article.tex}; the PDFs, the closed SHA-256 inventory and the earlier-input copy of Report~124 are not shipped (Report~124 is Part~II), and the scripts and data are shipped under prefixed names (README). The delivered replay cannot run in this directory.
\end{writenote}

\section{Further questions and research}\label{dsasm:sec:further}
\begin{writenote}[Added in the write, 5~October 2026]
This section is not part of Report~128. Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026 it collects the claims of Part~I, and the questions of Parts~II--IV that remain open after Part~I, that are stated without proof, with their source, the sketch given and what is missing. Questions that Part~I answers are recorded as answered at their places in Parts~II--IV (Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}, \ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions} and~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:questions}).
\end{writenote}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{The value of the amplitude.} Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main} proves that $C_*=C(1)=C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}e^{E(1)/2}$ exists and characterizes it by \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}, ``without a numerical fit''; Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:scope} states that no digits of $C(1)$ are established. Open: a certified numerical value, and a closed form. Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan estimate $C\approx0.72352852136732419487$ from the exact counts for $n\le1000$ and report no conjectured closed expression \cite[Conjecture~11.1]{bfk}. \emph{Uncertified evidence (intake dossier and write, 5~October 2026):} from exact counts to $n=177$ (independent Pfaffian and leading-minor computation, agreeing with the OEIS terms checked), the residual $r_n=\log a_n-\alpha n^2-\beta n+\kappa\log n$ is $-0.3236158$ at $n=100$ and $-0.3236155$, $-0.3236162$ at $n=176,177$; averages over four consecutive $n$ and Richardson extrapolation with $1/n$ or $1/n^2$ drift give $\log C_*=-0.323615\pm10^{-6}$, that is
\[
 C_*\approx0.7235287\pm10^{-6}\qquad\text{(uncertified)},
\]
\[
 E(1)=2\log\bigl(C_*/(C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4})\bigr)\approx-0.248070\pm2\cdot10^{-6},
\]
with $C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}=0.81907484878612193750\ldots$ from \eqref{dsasm:Ccal}. This agrees with the estimate of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan ($\log C=-0.32361531\ldots$) to within the stated error. (Dated note, 5~October 2026, after the independent check recorded at the end of this section: the centre of the display first read $0.7235288$, but $\exp(-0.323615)=0.72352875\ldots$. An extrapolation with $1/n$ drift has no basis in the form of Conjecture~11.1, which has no $1/n$ term. Least-squares fits of $r_n$ to that form, with a constant, $n^{-2}$, the two parity terms in $n^{-5/2}$, $n^{-3}$, $(-1)^nn^{-3}$ and $n^{-4}$, over $80\le n\le177$, $100\le n\le177$, $80\le n\le200$ and $120\le n\le200$, give $\log C_*$ between $-0.3236153120$ and $-0.3236153117$, within $7\cdot10^{-10}$ of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan's $-0.3236153123$. This is also uncertified.) What is missing: a certified evaluation of \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}, or rigorous error bounds for an extrapolation, which would need an explicit rate (item~2).
\item \textbf{Rate and the finer corrections of Conjecture~11.1.} Part~I gives $(1+o(1))$ with no explicit rate (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:scope}). Conjecture~11.1 of \cite{bfk} predicts the factor $1-385/(31104n^2)+(-1)^{n/2}a\,n^{-5/2}+O(n^{-3})$ for even $n$ and $1-385/(31104n^2)+(-1)^{(n+1)/2}b\,n^{-5/2}+O(n^{-3})$ for odd $n$, with $b=(1+\sqrt2)a$; none of this is proved here. \emph{Uncertified evidence:} the increments $r_n-r_{n-1}$ show a decaying oscillation of period~$4$ (about $\pm3\cdot10^{-7}$ and $\pm7\cdot10^{-7}$ near $n=175$, signs $+,+,-,-$), which is the period of the signs $(-1)^{n/2}$ ($n$ even) and $(-1)^{(n+1)/2}$ ($n$ odd) of the conjectured $n^{-5/2}$ terms; and the exact $r_n$ for $170\le n\le177$ differ from the logarithm of the conjectured expansion, with the published estimates of $C$, $a$, $b$ inserted, by at most $2\cdot10^{-10}$. What is missing: any quantitative form of the companion limit $E_n\to E$ and of the boundary limit \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget}; Part~I's proofs (Vitali, normal families, strong resolvent convergence) are qualitative.
\item \textbf{An all-orders expansion.} Neither an $n^{-2}$ correction nor an all-orders or transseries expansion is established (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:scope}; Part~II, question~3 of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}). Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan remark that, unlike the product-formula classes, the DSASM expansion appears to contain fractional exponents and irrational coefficients. Open.
\item \textbf{Effective constants and a certified inverse.} The function $\eps(T)$ of Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse} has no explicit decay and cannot be dropped inside the ceilings; Part~II (question~4 of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}), Part~III (question~5 of Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}) and Part~IV (question~5 of Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:questions}) ask for effective constants and onsets. Open: explicit Jacobi, kernel-ideal, overlap and Fredholm constants, a rate as in item~2, and hence a certified finite-target threshold algorithm. For a specific finite $T$ the exact counts decide $N(T)$ directly.
\item \textbf{Uniformity.} Convergence is locally uniform on $\Re s>0$ and for each fixed derivative order (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:scope}); it is not asserted as $s\to0$, $s\to\infty$, or for derivative orders growing with $n$. Open: joint regimes in $(n,s)$; Part~III, questions~3 and~4 of Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}, ask related questions about root escape and prefactors away from the central window.
\item \textbf{Evaluating $E(t)$.} The operator/Fourier expression \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit} has not been evaluated at any $t\ne3$. \emph{Uncertified evidence (dossier):} the exact means give $M_n(1)-n/3\to0.4060$ approximately, on both parities; with Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants} at $s=1$, $\mathbb E_1S_n-n/3\to E'(1)+\frac12-\frac16$, this suggests $E'(1)\approx0.0727$. The boundary ratios satisfy $F_n(s)-(s/c)/\sqrt{P(s)}=1.2\cdot10^{-6}$, $4.3\cdot10^{-6}$, $-1.5\cdot10^{-5}$ at $s=\frac12,1,3$ and $n=100$. They do not decrease monotonically in $n$: each varies with period~$4$, without changing sign, and its maximum over $4k\le n\le4k+3$ decreases with $k$ for $20\le4k\le196$, roughly like $n^{-2}$ (about $4\cdot10^{-7}$, $10^{-6}$ and $1.6\cdot10^{-5}$ for $192\le n\le195$). (Dated note, 5~October 2026: corrected after the independent check recorded at the end of this section; the sentence first ended ``and $n=100$, decreasing''.) Open: a convergent numerical scheme with error bounds for the determinants $\mathcal A$, $\Xi$, $\Psi$ and the series in \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Independent check of the write (dated note, 5~October 2026).}
An independent adversarial check of the write, made by the intake after the write (\texttt{ed4d0a12f}), reread its deductions in the dated notes: the recentring of Part~III's local CLT at $nm(s)$; the replacement of Part~II's bounds by any $c_-<C_*<c_+$ and $E_n(1)\to E(1)$; the answered questions of Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}, \ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions} and~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:questions}; $p<B(1)=\beta<q$; and the identification of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main} with the leading term of Conjecture~11.1 of \cite{bfk}. It reread the merge: the eight conversions of \verb|\cite{r124}|, the list (I1)--(I4) against Report~124's text, the printing of shared sections once, and the statements about Conjecture~11.1; and it recomputed every number of the notes and of this section. It found every mathematical claim valid, with no counterexample and no gap in any of the deductions, and the merge faithful. Five corrections were made where the text stands, each dated; where wording was replaced, a note keeps the first wording. The centre of the uncertified estimate of $C_*$ in item~1 is $0.7235287$, not $0.7235288$. The notes in Sections~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:stability} and~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:questions} said that cross-size interlacing is proved; Part~II proves weak interlacing \eqref{dsasm:log:weakinterlace}, with multiplicities, and says that strict interlacing is not asserted. The note in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions} compared the root fractions of $P_{60}$ with values of $\nu([0,x])$ but called them values of the normalized finite part of $\nu$. Item~6 called the boundary-ratio errors decreasing; they vary with period~$4$, and only their maximum over four consecutive $n$ decreases. And item~2 of ``Where the merge had to choose'' omitted one of the three differences of Report~121's interlacing section, which the note in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing} records. Two additions, both uncertified or finite: the least-squares fits of item~1, and the simplicity of the roots and strict interlacing for $n\le64$ (note in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}). The note there also says now that Part~II's proof of \eqref{dsasm:log:cumulants} is locally uniform in~$s$. No other statement or number changed.

The check used neither the delivered programs nor the write's. It computed $a_n$ exactly for $n\le200$ from the Pfaffian formula of \cite[Corollary~4.2]{bfk} by skew elimination modulo $280$ primes near $2^{31}$ and the Chinese remainder theorem, stable when $15$ primes are dropped; the terms equal the OEIS b-file ($n\le131$) and the write's exact terms ($n\le177$), and a brute-force enumeration of all alternating sign matrices for $n\le7$ gives the counts and $Z_4=3+12s^2+s^4$, $Z_5=32s+34s^3+s^5$. From these terms it recomputed $r_{100}$, $r_{176}$, $r_{177}$, the period-$4$ increments of $r_n$ near $n=175$, the residual against the expansion \cite[(11.8)]{bfk} with the published $C$, $a$, $b$ ($1.895\cdot10^{-10}$ at most for $170\le n\le177$, $1.56\cdot10^{-10}$ for $178\le n\le200$), and $C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}$. From the exact polynomials $P_n$, $n\le64$, interpolated from the same Pfaffian, and the exact $P_n$ and $P_n'$ at $t=1,3,\frac14,9$ for $n\le200$, it confirmed $P_n(1)=a_n$, $Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}$ for $n\le63$, $Z_n(c)Z_{n+1}(c)=c2^nA_{n+1}$ for $n\le199$, the Newton inequalities, the root data of $P_{60}$, $M_n(1)-n/3\to0.4060$ on both parities and the boundary ratios. A normalized \texttt{diff} of Part~I against the delivered sections of Report~128 shows exactly the thirteen changed lines that the conversions of \verb|\cite{r124}| and the item list (I1)--(I4) explain, and diffs of the delivered sources confirm the shared sections and the equation-number mappings of the pointer notes. Against the HTML text of arXiv:2309.08446v3 it confirmed (11.8) as the front matter prints it, the published constants and that no closed form of $C$ is conjectured. This was a careful reading with numerical tests, not a formal verification.
\clearpage
\part{Logarithmic asymptotics: bounded determinant remainders and inverse thresholds}\label{dsasm:log:part}
\parteqs{II.}
\partsource{Bundle Report~124, archive \file{A005163_Exact_Logarithmic_Scale_and_Inverse_Thresholds_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{report124.tex} (34 pages), dated 2~October 2026; Report~128 ships the same TeX and PDF byte for byte as its earlier input. Delivered title block: ``REPORT 124 / Logarithmic asymptotics of diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices / Bounded determinant remainders and inverse thresholds / 2 October 2026 / Standalone proof and reproducibility companion''. Printed in full, with \textbf{[write]} notes. Report~124's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+9$ here, its statement $k.j$ is $(k+9).j$, and its equation $(k)$ is $(\mathrm{II}.k)$.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~II & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$P_n(t)$ the parity-reduced polynomial (Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:main}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:complex}); $P_n$ the degree-$<n$ projection (Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}); $P_n^0$ the coordinate projection (Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap}) & Part~I: $P_n$ only a projection, $P(s)$ the pressure\\
$E_n=\log[D_n(1)/D_n(3)]-n\log(2/3)$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scope}) & Part~I: $E_n(t)$, with $E_n=E_n(1)$\\
$z(T)$ inverse centre; $r=\sqrt{y/\alpha}$ & Part~I: $z_C(T)$ adds $-\log C_*/(2\alpha r)$; Parts~III, IV: $n_0=r$\\
$C_{\DS}$ conjectured amplitude & Part~I: $C_*$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $a_n$ count diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices of order
$n$, the sequence A005163. We prove
\[
 \log a_n=\frac12\log\!\left(\frac{3\sqrt3}{4}\right)n^2
       +\frac14(\log3)n-\frac5{72}\log n+O(1).
\]
Thus the counts have positive finite upper and lower bounds at the
full exponential and logarithmic shape in the conjecture of
Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan. The proof retains the exact finite
orthogonal-polynomial projections throughout. A regularized Fredholm
comparison reduces the original determinant to a Jacobi projection
of a Hardy-compressed scalar multiplier. Endpoint-controlled Jacobi
means, a finite tilted-projection variance bound, and a weighted
Hardy overlap estimate give the bounded remainder. Exact stability,
cross-size interlacing, and product calibration transfer it to
individual sizes. We also identify the constant correction to the
first calibration trace and the logarithmic term in inverse count
thresholds, with integer rounding retained. No convergence of the
centered count or determinant, no limiting DSASM amplitude, and no
full asymptotic equivalent are asserted. A complex-local extension gives
bounded errors for every fixed diagonal cumulant and an explicitly
centered central limit theorem at each positive fugacity.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[Status of Part~II, 5~October 2026]
Part~I uses this Part's results (I1)--(I4) (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:main}) and strengthens its bounded statements to limits: the sentence ``No convergence of the centered count or determinant, no limiting DSASM amplitude, and no full asymptotic equivalent are asserted'' describes this Part, and is now historical, since Part~I proves all three (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}). Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration} are also Sections~2, 4 and~5 of Reports~121 and~120 (Parts~III and~IV), and Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing} is also Report~121's Section~9; they are printed only here, and the variant sentences of the other two manuscripts are printed at Sections~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing}, \ref{dsasm:env:sec:stability} and~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:logcalibration}.
\end{writenote}
\section{Definitions and main conclusions}\label{dsasm:log:sec:main}

An alternating sign matrix is a square matrix with entries in
$\{-1,0,1\}$ whose nonzero entries alternate in sign in every row and
column, and whose row and column sums equal one. It is diagonally
symmetric if it equals its transpose. Let
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:definitions}
\begin{split}
 a_n&=|\DS(n)|,\qquad S(A)=|\{i:A_{ii}\ne0\}|,\\
 Q_n(s_1,\ldots,s_n)&=\sum_{A\in\DS(n)}\prod_{i:A_{ii}\ne0}s_i,
 \qquad Z_n(s)=Q_n(s,\ldots,s).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Use $a_0=Z_0=Q_0=1$ and natural logarithms. In the notation of
Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan~\cite{bfk}, $Z_n(s)=X_n(1,s,1)$.
The sequence $a_n$ is OEIS A005163~\cite{oeis}. Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:constants}
 g=\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4},\quad \alpha=\frac g2,\quad
 \beta=\frac14\log3,\quad \kappa=\frac5{72},\quad
 b=\frac{g+\log2}{2}=\frac34\log3-\frac12\log2.
\end{equation}
All asymptotic statements are along the full sequence of positive
integers, unless specified otherwise.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:log:main}
There are constants $0<c_-\le c_+<\infty$ such that for every $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:bounds}
 c_- e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-\kappa}
 \le a_n\le c_+ e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-\kappa}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:logmain}
 \log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1).
\end{equation}
For the polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Ddef}
 D_n(t)=\frac{Z_n(\sqrt t)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt t)}{\sqrt t},
\end{equation}
interpreted polynomially, the determinant remainder is bounded:
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Dmain}
 \log\frac{D_n(1)}{D_n(3)}=n\log(2/3)+O(1).
\end{equation}
Its first logarithmic derivative at the solvable calibration satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:firstmain}
 \frac{D_n'(3)}{D_n(3)}=(n+1)\left(1-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\right)+o(1).
\end{equation}
The constants in \eqref{dsasm:log:bounds} are not made numerically effective here.
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Superseded bounds, 5~October 2026]
Part~I, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}, proves that $a_ne^{-\alpha n^2-\beta n}n^\kappa\to C_*>0$, so \eqref{dsasm:log:bounds} holds with every $c_-<C_*<c_+$ for all large $n$, and $\log(D_n(1)/D_n(3))-n\log(2/3)\to E(1)$. The trace limit \eqref{dsasm:log:firstmain} is not restated in Part~I and stands here; Part~III, \eqref{dsasm:pr:maintarget}, proves the weaker $\ell_n/n\to1-\sqrt3/2$ by a different route.
\end{writenote}


Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan's Conjecture 11.1 in the version dated
1 October 2026 predicts a full equivalent
$a_n\sim C_{\DS}e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}$ and further
parity-sensitive corrections. Their Theorem 11.2 gives the leading
quadratic growth. Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main} proves two-sided bounds at the
conjectured shape; it does not establish the limit of the normalized
counts or determine $C_{\DS}$.
\begin{writenote}[Historical, 5~October 2026]
Part~I proves the limit of the normalized counts; $C_{\DS}$ is Part~I's $C_*$, characterized by \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit} but not evaluated (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}). The parity-sensitive corrections remain unproved.
\end{writenote}


\begin{corollary}\label{dsasm:log:inverseintro}
Let $N(T)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge T\}$, $y=\log T$ and
$r=\sqrt{y/\alpha}$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:zdef}
 z(T)=r-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}
       +\frac{\kappa\log r+\beta^2/(4\alpha)}{2\alpha r}.
\end{equation}
For some $C,T_0>0$ and every $T\ge T_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:inversebracket}
 \ceil{z(T)-C/r}\le N(T)\le\ceil{z(T)+C/r}.
\end{equation}
In particular, the coefficient of
$(\log\log T)/\sqrt{\log T}$ in this real-valued center is
$\kappa/(4\sqrt\alpha)$. The bracket, rather than an unrounded
additive $o(1)$ assertion, is the integer inverse statement.
\end{corollary}
\begin{writenote}[Sharpened, 5~October 2026]
Part~I, Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse}, adds the amplitude term $-\log C_*/(2\alpha r)$ to the centre \eqref{dsasm:log:zdef} and replaces $C/r$ by $\eps(T)/r$ with $\eps(T)\to0$.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{Proof architecture and published inputs}
Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration} establish the
exact finite prerequisites. The negative-root property makes the
regularized relative determinant nonvanishing, and cross-size
interlacing controls the possible parity imbalance. The calibration
is derived from a shifted determinant evaluation and the ordinary
ASM product; no calibration amplitude is required.
Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:jacobiconversion} construct
the fixed bounded-operator comparison, distinguish Hilbert--Schmidt
from trace-class errors, and identify the exact Jacobi projection.
The endpoint mean theorem in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:mean}, the scalar
determinant bound in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:scalar}, and the weighted Hardy
overlap in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:overlap} close the analytic argument.
Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:transfer} transfers it to the count and its inverse.

The published combinatorial inputs are the triangular six-vertex
bijection and determinant of~\cite{bfk}. Stability closure and the
MOD operator are classical~\cite{bb1,bb2}; the degree-two operator and
the interlacing implication are proved explicitly below. The shifted
determinant and Meixner--Pollaczek Gram formulas are classical
inputs~\cite{kratt,ros}. The only orthogonal-polynomial asymptotic
input is the fixed-parameter uniform Jacobi Bessel expansion of
Frenzen--Wong~\cite{fw}, with its explicit DLMF normalization and
remainder~\cite{dlmf}. Standard Fredholm determinant continuity and
regularization are used only after the relevant ideal convergence
has been proved~\cite{simon}. No general strong Szeg\H{o} theorem is
invoked, and no global bibliographic priority claim is made.

For later reference, stability and parity will give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:paritypoly}
 Z_n(s)=s^{\eps_n}P_n(s^2),\qquad
 \eps_n=n\bmod2,\qquad P_n(0)>0,
\end{equation}
with $P_n$ monic of degree $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ and all its roots
strictly negative. Consequently $D_n=P_nP_{n+1}$ is monic of degree
$n$ and has only strictly negative roots, with multiplicities retained.

\section{An orientation polynomial and diagonal stability}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:stability}
Let $\HH=\{z\in\C:\Re z>0\}$. A polynomial is
$\HH$-stable if it is nonvanishing whenever all its variables belong
to $\HH$; the zero polynomial is not called stable here.

\subsection{The exact triangular graph}
The model from \cite[Section 5.1, Equations (5.1)--(5.2)]{bfk} has
vertices
\[
 V=\{(i,j):1\le i\le j\le n\},\quad
 B=\{(i,j):i<j\},\quad D=\{(i,i):1\le i\le n\}.
\]
Its internal undirected edges are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:edges}
\begin{split}
 E_{\rm h}&=\{\{(i,j),(i,j+1)\}:1\le i\le j<n\},\\
 E_{\rm v}&=\{\{(i,j),(i+1,j)\}:1\le i<j\le n\},
 \qquad E_{\rm int}=E_{\rm h}\cup E_{\rm v}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
There are $n(n-1)$ internal edges. The $n$ top boundary arrows point
out of the triangle; the $n$ right boundary arrows point into it.
Each bulk vertex has degree four and must have indegree two. Each
diagonal vertex has degree two and no indegree restriction.

In the local correspondence with matrix entries, the diagonal
possibilities are as follows. ``In'' and ``out'' refer to the diagonal
vertex, not to the whole triangle.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
Diagonal entry & North arrow & East arrow & Indegree\\
\midrule
$+1$ & out & in & $1$\\
$-1$ & in & out & $1$\\
$0$ & in & in & $2$\\
$0$ & out & out & $0$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Thus a nonzero diagonal entry is exactly a diagonal vertex of indegree
one. The published correspondence is a bijection, so no extra
multiplicity factor is needed.

Assign a variable $x_v$ to every vertex $v\in V$ and form
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:orientation}
 H_n(\mathbf x)=\left(\prod_{i=1}^n x_{(i,n)}\right)
             \prod_{\{v,w\}\in E_{\rm int}}(x_v+x_w).
\end{equation}
Choosing $x_v$ in the factor for $\{v,w\}$ directs that edge into $v$.
The prefactor records the fixed incoming arrows on the right;
fixed outgoing top arrows contribute one. A monomial exponent is
therefore the corresponding indegree, including boundary arrows.
Different orientations may give the same monomial, and the polynomial
counts each of them with its proper multiplicity.

Each edge factor is nonzero when its variables lie in $\HH$, and so
is the prefactor. Hence $H_n$ is $\HH$-stable. Enforce all the bulk ice
rules by coefficient extraction:
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:extract}
 F_n(\mathbf x_D)=\left[\prod_{v\in B}x_v^2\right]H_n(\mathbf x).
\end{equation}
Differentiation and specialization to a boundary point of $\HH$
preserve stability, with a possible identically zero result; these
standard closure properties are given in
\cite[Theorem 1.6 and Lemma 1.7]{bb1} and
\cite[Remark 3.1]{bb2}. For coefficient extraction, differentiate
twice, set that variable to zero, and divide by $2!$. Boundary
specialization also follows by taking positive real specializations
tending to zero and applying Hurwitz's theorem. A configuration
corresponding to the identity matrix survives each extraction.
All coefficients are nonnegative, so there is no cancellation and
no zero intermediate result. Consequently $F_n$ is stable.

The remaining variable $x_{(i,i)}$ has degree at most two. At the first
corner it has degree at most one because the top arrow is fixed
outward; at the last corner it has exponent at least one because the
right arrow is fixed inward. When $n=1$, \eqref{dsasm:log:orientation} and
\eqref{dsasm:log:extract} give $H_1=F_1=x_{(1,1)}$, as required.
The polynomial $H_n$ has total degree $n^2$. Extraction removes
$n(n-1)$, so $F_n$ is homogeneous of degree $n$.

\subsection{The boundary operator with all degree cases}
For a variable $x$ of degree at most two, define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:operator}
 \mathcal T_s(1)=1,\qquad \mathcal T_s(x)=s,\qquad
 \mathcal T_s(x^2)=1.
\end{equation}
This is the degree-two restriction of the MOD operator from
\cite[Section 8.4]{bb2}, which reduces monomial exponents modulo two
and preserves weak Hurwitz stability. The following direct argument
is included so that the exact case needed here is explicit.

\begin{lemma}\label{dsasm:log:boundarylemma}
Applying \eqref{dsasm:log:operator} to one variable of a stable multivariate
polynomial whose degree in that variable is at most two produces a
stable polynomial in the new variable $s$ and all the other variables.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix all the other variables at arbitrary points of $\HH$. The resulting
polynomial $f(x)$ is nonzero on $\HH$, so it is not identically zero.
Its actual degree is zero, one or two, even if a leading coefficient
vanishes under the chosen specialization.

If $f(x)=c\ne0$, the image is $c$. If $f(x)=c(x+a)$, then
$\Re a\ge0$ and the image $c(s+a)$ is nonzero for $s\in\HH$.
In degree two, write $f(x)=c(x+a)(x+d)$ with
$c\ne0$ and $\Re a,\Re d\ge0$. Its image is
\[
 c\bigl(1+ad+s(a+d)\bigr).
\]
If $a+d\ne0$, its only possible zero is
$s=-(1+ad)/(a+d)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:boundaryidentity}
 \Re\frac{1+ad}{a+d}
 =\frac{\Re(a)(1+|d|^2)+\Re(d)(1+|a|^2)}{|a+d|^2}\ge0.
\end{equation}
That zero is outside $\HH$. If $a+d=0$, the nonnegative real parts
imply $a=iy$, $d=-iy$ for some $y\in\R$; then $1+ad=1+y^2>0$.
The image is again nonzero. Since every allowed assignment of the
other variables was covered, the full multivariate assertion follows.
\end{proof}

Apply this operator to every diagonal variable in $F_n$. The local
indegree table shows exactly that
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Qoperator}
 Q_n(s_1,\ldots,s_n)=
 \left(\prod_{i=1}^n\mathcal T_{s_i}\right)F_n(\mathbf x_D).
\end{equation}
Iterating Lemma~\ref{dsasm:log:boundarylemma} proves the joint stability of $Q_n$.
Setting every $s_i=s$ proves that $Z_n$ has no zero with $\Re s>0$.

\subsection{Parity and strictly negative roots}
Each monomial of $F_n$ has exponents $d_i\in\{0,1,2\}$ with
$\sum_i d_i=n$. Replacing these exponents by their parities changes
total degree by an even number. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:parity}
 Z_n(-s)=(-1)^n Z_n(s),
\end{equation}
which gives \eqref{dsasm:log:paritypoly} with nonnegative integer coefficients.
One can also check parity directly from symmetry:
$n=\sum_i A_{ii}+2\sum_{i<j}A_{ij}$, while each nonzero $A_{ii}$ is
congruent to one modulo two.

If a zero of $Z_n$ had negative real part, its negative would have
positive real part by \eqref{dsasm:log:parity}, contradicting stability.
Every zero of $Z_n$ therefore lies on the imaginary axis. The
antidiagonal permutation matrix is symmetric and has exactly
$\eps_n$ nonzero diagonal entries; hence $P_n(0)>0$. The identity matrix
has $n$ nonzero diagonal entries, giving degree $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$.
For any root $t$ of $P_n$, choose $s$ with $s^2=t$. Then $s\ne0$,
$Z_n(s)=0$, and $s$ is purely imaginary. It follows that $t=s^2$ is
strictly negative and real. This proves the asserted root structure. For $n=1$, $P_1=1$ and the root statement is vacuous.

The root theorem by itself does not prove simplicity. Cross-size weak
interlacing will be established separately in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}.
Stability for arbitrary further six-vertex weights is not asserted.


\section{Cross size interlacing and parity control}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}
The joint stability established in Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability} gives
more than real-rootedness of each separate polynomial. We now derive
a stable pencil and its consequences with multiplicities retained.

\subsection{Corner deletion and the stable pencil}
If $A_{11}\ne0$ in an ASM, the topmost nonzero entry in its first
column shows $A_{11}=1$. Every entry of the first row is zero or one,
so its row sum forces all other entries in that row to vanish.
Symmetry gives the same conclusion in the first column. Deleting
that row and column is a bijection to $\DS(n-1)$, whose inverse
adjoins an isolated $+1$ corner. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:corner}
 [s_1]Q_n(s_1,\ldots,s_n)=Q_{n-1}(s_2,\ldots,s_n).
\end{equation}
This also proves inductively that the unique DSASM with all diagonal
entries nonzero is the identity. Thus $Z_n$ is monic of degree $n$,
and $P_n$ is monic as well.

Define
\[
 \mathcal H_n(\mathbf x)=i^{-n}Q_n(i\mathbf x),\qquad
 q_n(x)=i^{-n}Z_n(ix).
\]
Every monomial of $Q_n$ has degree congruent to $n$ modulo two, by
the symmetry argument used in \eqref{dsasm:log:parity}; thus $\mathcal H_n$
has real coefficients and $Q_n(-\mathbf s)=(-1)^nQ_n(\mathbf s)$.
If every $\Im x_j>0$, then every $\Re(-ix_j)>0$, and parity and
right-half-plane stability give
$Q_n(i\mathbf x)=(-1)^nQ_n(-i\mathbf x)\ne0$.
Therefore $\mathcal H_n$ is upper-half-plane stable. In particular
$q_n$ is monic, real-rooted, and has symmetric roots about zero.

Since $Q_n$ is multiaffine, \eqref{dsasm:log:corner} gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:pencil}
 \mathcal H_n(x+t,x,\ldots,x)=q_n(x)+tq_{n-1}(x).
\end{equation}
For $\Im x>0$ and $\Im t>0$, all arguments on the left lie in the
upper half-plane. This proves two-variable stability of the pencil.
The general stable-pencil/interlacing criterion is classical
\cite[Definition 1.1 and Lemma 1.8]{bb1}; the direct proof below fixes
all residue signs and handles cancellation explicitly.

\subsection{The rational function argument with common roots}
Take the monic greatest common divisor $d_n=\gcd(q_n,q_{n-1})$ and
write $f=q_n/d_n$, $g_0=q_{n-1}/d_n$. These are coprime monic
real-rooted polynomials of degrees differing by one. For $\Im z>0$,
stability in \eqref{dsasm:log:pencil} means $-f(z)/g_0(z)$ cannot have positive
imaginary part. Thus $R_0=f/g_0$ maps the upper half-plane into its
closure.

Every pole of $R_0$ is real. At a pole $p$ of order $r$ with real
leading coefficient $a\ne0$, the leading imaginary part at
$z=p+\rho e^{i\theta}$ is $-a\rho^{-r}\sin(r\theta)$.
If $r\ge2$, this has both signs for $0<\theta<\pi$, a contradiction
for small $\rho$. Thus all actual poles are simple, and their residues
are negative. Partial fractions and the monic degree difference give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:partialfractions}
 R_0(z)=z+\beta-\sum_{j=1}^r\frac{c_j}{z-p_j},
 \qquad p_1<\cdots<p_r,\quad c_j>0,\quad\beta\in\R.
\end{equation}
On every real interval between consecutive poles,
$R_0'(x)=1+\sum_jc_j/(x-p_j)^2>0$. The one-sided limits show that
$R_0$ runs from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$ on each such interval and
on both exterior intervals. It has exactly one simple zero in each,
so $f$ and $g_0$ strictly interlace. If $g_0=1$, the same conclusion
is the elementary statement for $R_0(z)=z+\beta$.

Let $N_p(x)$ count the roots of a polynomial in $(-\infty,x]$ with
multiplicity. Reduced interlacing gives
$N_f(x)-N_{g_0}(x)\in\{0,1\}$. Restoring the common roots leaves this
difference unchanged. Therefore, if the original root lists are
$a_1\le\cdots\le a_n$ and $b_1\le\cdots\le b_{n-1}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:weakinterlace}
 a_j\le b_j\le a_{j+1}\qquad(1\le j<n).
\end{equation}
The difference of counts in any interval, with a fixed convention at
its endpoints, has absolute value at most one. This follows by
subtracting the two half-line discrepancies, each in $\{0,1\}$.
No simplicity or strict interlacing of the original polynomials is
asserted.

For the reverse ratio there is a useful positive-residue statement.
If $\alpha_k$ are the simple roots of $f$, then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:positiveresidues}
 \frac{q_{n-1}(z)}{q_n(z)}
 =\sum_k\frac{w_k}{z-\alpha_k},\qquad
 w_k=\frac1{R_0'(\alpha_k)}>0,\qquad\sum_kw_k=1.
\end{equation}
Only uncancelled poles appear. Writing a residue as
$q_{n-1}(r)/q_n'(r)$ at an original multiple or common root could give
$0/0$ and is not justified; \eqref{dsasm:log:positiveresidues} uses the reduced
polynomials instead.

\subsection{The adjacent mean bound}
Let $r_{n,j}$ be all the real roots of $q_n$, with multiplicity.
The roots of $Z_n$ are $ir_{n,j}$. Pairing roots $r,-r$ in its
logarithmic derivative cancels imaginary parts and gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:rootmean}
 M_n(s):=sZ_n'(s)/Z_n(s)=\sum_{j=1}^n\frac{s^2}{s^2+r_{n,j}^2}\qquad(s>0).
\end{equation}
Zero roots are included. For $0<u<1$, the superlevel set of the
summand as a function of $r$ is the interval
$(-s\sqrt{u^{-1}-1},s\sqrt{u^{-1}-1})$. Layer cake and
\eqref{dsasm:log:weakinterlace} therefore prove
\[
 |M_n(s)-M_{n-1}(s)|
 \le\int_0^1 1\,du=1.
\]
This holds at every positive fugacity and includes $n=1$ under the
empty-size conventions. The constant cannot be improved uniformly,
since $M_1-M_0=1$.


Integrating the adjacent bound against $ds/s$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ratioadjacent}
 \left|\log\frac{Z_{n+1}(s)}{Z_{n+1}(c)}
       -\log\frac{Z_n(s)}{Z_n(c)}\right|
 \le|\log(s/c)|\qquad(s,c>0).
\end{equation}
This is the finite-size estimate needed below. It avoids alternating
sums of asymptotic errors, which need not be controlled.

\section{The exact solvable calibration}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:calibration}
Let $A_N=|\ASM(N)|$ denote the ordinary ASM count, with $A_0=1$.
We will prove
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:calibration}
 Z_n(\sqrt3)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt3)=\sqrt3\,2^n A_{n+1}
 \qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
This is derived from the published adjacent determinant by exact
finite transformations, followed by a classical determinant evaluation.

\subsection{From the source determinant to an adjacent minor}
Put $t=s^2$ and interpret
$D_n(t)=Z_n(s)Z_{n+1}(s)/s$ polynomially, including at $s=0$.
Define the skew-symmetric formal kernel
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:kernel}
 K_t(u,v)=\frac{v-u}{1-uv}
 \left(t+\frac{(1+u)(1+v)}{1-u-v}\right).
\end{equation}
Equation (4.11) of \cite{bfk}, specialized to $r=1$, states
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:sourcedet}
 D_n(t)=\det_{0\le i,j\le n}
 \begin{cases}
 [u^iv^j]\dfrac{K_t(u,v)}{(1+u)(1+v)},&0\le j<n,\\[5pt]
 (-1)^i,&j=n.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
All coefficient extractions here are at $u=v=0$.

On the first $n$ columns, multiply coefficient series by $1+v$.
On all $n+1$ rows, multiply by $1+u$. Both operations are finite
unitriangular operations, so preserve the determinant. The last
column becomes $e_0=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf T}$. Expansion along it
gives $(-1)^n$ times the minor with entries
$[u^{i+1}v^j]K_t$, $0\le i,j<n$. Transposition and
$K_t(v,u)=-K_t(u,v)$ contribute another $(-1)^n$. The signs cancel,
and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:minordet}
 D_n(t)=\det_{0\le i,j<n}\bigl([u^iv^{j+1}]K_t(u,v)\bigr).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite Pascal and Toeplitz transformations}
For an $n\times n$ matrix use indices $0,\ldots,n-1$. Let $S_n$ be
the lower shift and set
\[
 T_n(f)=f(S_n),\quad
 \Pi_{ij}=\binom{i+j}{i},\quad L_{ij}=\binom ij,\quad
 E=\operatorname{diag}((-1)^i),
\]
where $\binom ij=0$ for $j>i$. Because $S_n^n=0$, only the first $n$
coefficients of a formal series are used. In particular,
$T_n(fh)=T_n(f)T_n(h)$ is an exact finite identity.

Let $Q(z)=z^2-z+1$ and write
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Hdecomp}
\begin{split}
 H_t(u,v)&=\frac{K_t(u,v)-K_t(u,0)}{v}
       =\frac{\mathcal A_t(u)}{1-uv}
           +\frac{\mathcal B(u)}{1-u-v},\\
 \mathcal A_t(u)&=(1-u^2)\left(t-1-\frac{3u}{Q(u)}\right),\\
 \mathcal B(u)&=\frac{(u-2)(u+1)(2u-1)}{(1-u)Q(u)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
This identity follows by putting both sides over their common
denominator. The coefficient matrix of $H_t$ is
$T_n(\mathcal A_t)+T_n(\mathcal B)\Pi$. Since
$\mathcal B(0)=2$, factoring the second Toeplitz matrix gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:pascaldet}
\begin{split}
 D_n(t)&=2^n\det\bigl(\Pi+T_n(C_t)\bigr),\\
 C_t(z)&=\frac{\mathcal A_t(z)}{\mathcal B(z)}
 =\frac{(1-z)^2\bigl((t-1)(z^2-z+1)-3z\bigr)}{(z-2)(2z-1)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}

The finite binomial identities needed next are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:binomialidentities}
 \Pi=LL^{\mathsf T},\qquad
 L^{-1}T_n(f)L=T_n\!\left(f\!\left(\frac z{1+z}\right)\right),
 \qquad L^{-1}L^{-\mathsf T}=E\Pi E.
\end{equation}
For clarity, these do not involve an infinite-matrix determinant.
Vandermonde's identity gives the first equality.
The inverse of $L$ has entries $(-1)^{i-j}\binom ij$, which gives the
third equality by the first one. The second can be verified on the
monomials $f(z)=z^k$, or from the finite truncation of the binomial
transform $h(z)\mapsto(1-z)^{-1}h(z/(1-z))$. Every coefficient of
degree less than $n$ is unchanged by this truncation.

Multiply $\Pi+T_n(C_t)$ on the left by $L^{-1}$ and on the right by
$L^{-\mathsf T}$. Both determinants are one. Then conjugate by $E$.
Using $E T_n(f)E=T_n(f(-z))$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Gdet}
\begin{split}
 D_n(t)&=2^n\det\bigl(I+T_n(G_t)\Pi\bigr),\\
 G_t(z)&=C_t\!\left(\frac{-z}{1-z}\right)
 =\frac{(t-4)z^2+(4-t)z+t-1}{(2-z)(1-z)^2(1+z)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
All transformations are finite, and preserve every boundary row and
column of the indicated sections.

\subsection{The shifted determinant and its indexing}
At $t=3$, the numerator of $G_t$ is $(2-z)(1+z)$, and hence
$G_3(z)=(1-z)^{-2}$. The hockey-stick identity gives
\[
 \bigl(T_n((1-z)^{-2})\Pi\bigr)_{ij}
 =\sum_{k=0}^i(i-k+1)\binom{k+j}{k}
 =\binom{i+j+2}{i}.
\]
The $q\to1$ specialization of the shifted determinant in
\cite[Theorem 34, Equation (3.24)]{kratt}, transposed if necessary, is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Andrews}
 \det_{0\le i,j<n}\left(\delta_{ij}+\binom{i+j+2}{i}\right)
 =\prod_{1\le i\le j\le n+1}\frac{n+i+j}{2i+j-1}
 =A_{n+1}.
\end{equation}
Here the final equality has the indicated index $n+1$, rather than $n$.
To verify it directly, write the double product with $N=n+1$ as $R_N$.
Cancellation between successive sizes gives
\begin{align*}
 \frac{R_N}{R_{N-1}}
 &=\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{N-1+2j}{N-1+j}
   \prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{2N-1+i}{N-1+2i}\\
 &=\frac{(N-1)!(3N-2)!}{(2N-1)!(2N-2)!}.
\end{align*}
The standard ASM product formula~\cite[Equation (1.1)]{bfk},
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ASMproduct}
 A_N=\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{(3i+1)!}{(N+i)!},
\end{equation}
has the same successive ratio, and $R_1=A_1=1$. This proves the final
equality in \eqref{dsasm:log:Andrews} without finite-term recognition. Substituting
\eqref{dsasm:log:Andrews} into \eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet} proves \eqref{dsasm:log:calibration}.

\section{Logarithmic calibration without matching parity amplitudes}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration}
We require the bounded-remainder estimate
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:W}
 W_n:=\log Z_n(\sqrt3)
 =\alpha n^2+bn-\kappa\log n+O(1).
\end{equation}
The same coefficients will hold on both parities. No common limiting
constant in the $O(1)$ remainder is needed.

Put $d_{\rm ASM}=5/36=2\kappa$. The ratio from \eqref{dsasm:log:ASMproduct} is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ASMratio}
 \frac{A_N}{A_{N-1}}
 =\frac{(N-1)!(3N-2)!}{(2N-1)!(2N-2)!}
 =\frac{\Gamma(N)\Gamma(3N-1)}{\Gamma(2N)\Gamma(2N-1)}.
\end{equation}
Stirling's expansion, with a uniform remainder for these four fixed
shifts, gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ratiostirling}
 \log\frac{A_N}{A_{N-1}}=g(2N-1)-\frac{d_{\rm ASM}}N+O(N^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Here is an explicit check of the only inverse-power coefficient needed.
For $a>0$ and fixed $c$, the coefficient of $N^{-1}$ in
$\log\Gamma(aN+c)$ is $(c^2-c+1/6)/(2a)$.
For the four factors of \eqref{dsasm:log:ASMratio}, their signed contributions
are
\[
 \frac1{12}+\frac{13}{36}-\frac1{24}-\frac{13}{24}
 =-\frac5{36}.
\]
The coefficient of $N$ is $3\log3-4\log2=2g$, the constant term is
$-g$, and the $\log N$ terms cancel. Summing \eqref{dsasm:log:ratiostirling},
including its summable remainder, gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ASMlog}
 \log A_N=gN^2-d_{\rm ASM}\log N+O(1).
\end{equation}

Taking ratios between neighboring instances of \eqref{dsasm:log:calibration},
and using $Z_1(\sqrt3)=\sqrt3$, gives the exact formula
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:evenproduct}
 Z_{2m}(\sqrt3)=2^m\prod_{k=1}^m\frac{A_{2k}}{A_{2k-1}}.
\end{equation}
For example, $Z_2(\sqrt3)=4=2A_2/A_1$ is the starting case.
Substitute \eqref{dsasm:log:ratiostirling} with $N=2k$ and sum:
\begin{align*}
 W_{2m}
 &=m\log2+\sum_{k=1}^m\left(g(4k-1)-\frac{d_{\rm ASM}}{2k}+O(k^{-2})\right)\\
 &=2gm^2+(g+\log2)m-\frac5{72}\log m+O(1).
\end{align*}
Replacing $2m$ by $n$ proves \eqref{dsasm:log:W} on even $n$.

For odd $n\ge3$, \eqref{dsasm:log:calibration} at $n-1$ says
\[
 W_n=\frac12\log3+(n-1)\log2+\log A_n-W_{n-1}.
\]
Insert \eqref{dsasm:log:ASMlog} and the even formula. Since $2\alpha=g$,
$2b=g+\log2$ and $d_{\rm ASM}=2\kappa$, the quadratic, linear and logarithmic
terms are $\alpha n^2$, $bn$ and $-\kappa\log n$ respectively.
The remaining terms are bounded. This proves \eqref{dsasm:log:W} uniformly
over both parities. In particular, separate bounded parity constants
are fully compatible with everything used below.


\section{From the finite determinant to an exact polynomial operator}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator}
The next construction gives a fixed bounded operator whose finite
compressions are the determinant ratio. The order of multiplication
and the exact polynomial space are essential.

\subsection{The finite matrix identity}
In the notation of \eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet}, let
\[
 V_n(i,j)=\binom{i+j+2}{i},\quad d_i=\sqrt{(i+1)(i+2)},\quad
 D=\diag(d_i),\quad C_n=D^{-1}V_nD.
\]
Thus
\[
 C_n(i,j)=\frac{(i+j+2)!}{\sqrt{i!(i+2)!j!(j+2)!}}.
\]
Write $A_n=D^{-1}S_nD$ for the weighted lower shift; its entry in
position $(i,i-1)$ is $\sqrt{i/(i+2)}$. In this subsection only,
$A_n$ denotes this shift, not the Hardy Gram matrix introduced later.
Set
\[
 q(z)=2+z-z^2,\qquad r(z)=1-z+z^2,\qquad R(z)=r(z)/q(z).
\]
Since $G_t=G_3[1+(t-3)R]$, equation \eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet} gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:finitequotient}
 \frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}
 =\frac{\det\{q(A_n)(I+C_n)+(t-3)r(A_n)C_n\}}
        {2^n\det(I+C_n)}.
\end{equation}
All functions of the nilpotent shift are finite polynomial expressions.
In particular, $r(A_n)$ is invertible with determinant one, and
$\det q(A_n)=2^n$. No infinite inverse is used.

\subsection{The classical Gram forms and a degree lowering operator}
Let $h=\sqrt3$, $a=\pi/(3h)$ and define monic polynomials by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:MPrec}
 u_0(y)=1,\quad u_1(y)=\tfrac32-y,\quad
 u_{k+1}(y)=(k+\tfrac32-y)u_k(y)-k(k+2)u_{k-1}(y).
\end{equation}
Here ``monic'' refers to the variable $-y$; the leading coefficient
in $y$ is $(-1)^k$. Put $h_k=k!(3)_k$ and $\phi_k=u_k/\sqrt{h_k}$.
The rescaled Meixner--Pollaczek Gram identities~\cite[Equations
(2.4)--(2.6), (3.4a), (3.6)--(3.7)]{ros} are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:MPgrams}
\begin{gathered}
 w_\pm(y)=\frac3{4\pi}e^{\pm ay}
       |\Gamma(3/2+iy/h)|^2,\\
 \int_{\R}u_i u_j w_+\,dy=h_i\delta_{ij},\qquad
 \int_{\R}u_i u_j w_-\,dy=(3)_{i+j}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
They give $C_n(i,j)=\int\phi_i\phi_jw_-$. The stated constants can
also be checked from the $k=0$ integral and the recurrence
\eqref{dsasm:log:MPrec}; thus the Gram matrix for $w=w_++w_-$ is $I+C_n$.

On polynomials define $\mathcal L u_k=ku_{k-1}$. Its row-action
matrix in the $\phi_k$ basis is $A_n$, because
$k\sqrt{h_{k-1}/h_k}=\sqrt{k/(k+2)}$. Direct substitution in the
recurrence proves the exponential generating function
\[
 \sum_{k\ge0}\frac{u_k(y)z^k}{k!}
 =(1-z+z^2)^{-3/2}\exp(y\eta(z)),\qquad
 \eta(z)=-\int_0^z\frac{dv}{1-v+v^2}.
\]
In finite polynomial functional calculus, therefore,
\[
 \mathcal L=\tau(\partial_y),\qquad
 \tau(w)=\frac{-2\sin(hw/2)}{\sqrt3\cos(hw/2)-\sin(hw/2)}.
\]
Indeed $\tau$ is the local inverse of $\eta$, which proves this
identity on the generating function and then coefficientwise.
The elementary trigonometric identity
\[
 \frac{q(\tau(w))}{r(\tau(w))}=1+2\cos(hw+\pi/3)
\]
shows that $\mathcal K=q(\mathcal L)/r(\mathcal L)$ acts exactly as
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Kaction}
 (\mathcal K f)(y)=f(y)+e^{i\pi/3}f(y+ih)+e^{-i\pi/3}f(y-ih).
\end{equation}
It preserves every degree space, is real on real polynomials, and
has diagonal coefficient two in any degree-ordered basis.

Let $\psi_j$ be real orthonormal polynomials for $w(y)dy$.
Multiplying the numerator in \eqref{dsasm:log:finitequotient} on the left by
$r(A_n)^{-1}$, then applying \eqref{dsasm:log:MPgrams}, gives the bilinear form
\[
 (f,g)\longmapsto\int(\mathcal Kf)g\,w\,dy
                    +(t-3)\int fg\,w_-\,dy.
\]
Changing both polynomial bases from $\phi_j$ to $\psi_j$ divides
its determinant by $\det(I+C_n)$. We obtain, with row-action indexing,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:WD}
 \frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}=2^{-n}\det_{0\le i,j<n}
 \left\{\int (\mathcal K\psi_i)\psi_jw
                  +(t-3)\int\psi_i\psi_jw_-\right\}.
\end{equation}
Every integral here is absolutely convergent. The imaginary
translations act only on polynomials; no contour shift or domain
assertion for unbounded operators is hidden in this formula.

\subsection{An exact positive convolution inverse}
The elementary identity for $|\Gamma(3/2+iy/h)|^2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:wexplicit}
 w(y)=\frac{p(y)}{2(2\cosh(2ay)-1)},\quad p(y)=y^2+\tfrac34,
 \qquad m(y)=\frac{w_-(y)}{w(y)}=\frac1{1+e^{2ay}}.
\end{equation}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:phi}
 \varphi(u)=\frac1{3(1+2\cosh(2au))}.
\end{equation}
The bilateral Laplace integral is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:laplace}
 \int_{\R}e^{zu}\varphi(u)\,du=\frac1{1+2\cos(hz)},
 \qquad |\Re z|<2a.
\end{equation}
For completeness, $v=e^{2au}$ reduces the integral to
$(6a)^{-1}\int_0^\infty v^{z/(2a)}/(v^2+v+1)\,dv$.
Partial fractions at $e^{\pm2\pi i/3}$ and the elementary Mellin
integral $\int_0^\infty v^{\rho-1}/(v+c)\,dv
=\pi c^{\rho-1}/\sin(\pi\rho)$, first in its strip of convergence
and then by continuation within the convergent difference, give
\eqref{dsasm:log:laplace}. In particular, $\int\varphi=1/3$.
Define on polynomials
\[
 (Tf)(y)=\int_{\R}e^{au}\varphi(u)f(y+u)\,du.
\]
Its kernel is positive, has mass $1/2$, and has moment generating
function $[1+2\cos(hw+\pi/3)]^{-1}$. Differentiating
\eqref{dsasm:log:laplace} is justified on compact substrips. The Taylor series
on a polynomial terminates, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Tinverse}
 T=[1+2\cos(h\partial_y+\pi/3)]^{-1}=\mathcal K^{-1}
\end{equation}
exactly on each finite degree space.

Write $P_n$ for the orthogonal projection onto degree-$<n$
polynomials in $L^2(wdy)$. Transpose the row-action matrix in
\eqref{dsasm:log:WD} before factoring. With $\delta=t-3$ its determinant is
$\det[I+\delta M_n(\mathcal K_n^{\rm col})^{-1}]$.
Since $T$ preserves the degree space, this equals
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Bcompression}
 \frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}=\det_{\Ran P_n}(I+\delta P_n\mathcal B P_n),
 \qquad \mathcal B=mT.
\end{equation}
Boundedness of this fixed operator is proved next. This exact ordering
is $m\mathcal K^{-1}$; interchanging these factors or compressing
an unrelated infinite inverse would not prove \eqref{dsasm:log:Bcompression}.

\section{A controlled relative Fredholm comparison}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:fredholm}
Conjugate $L^2(wdy)$ to $L^2(dy)$ by multiplication by $\sqrt w$.
Let $\Pi_n$ project onto the span of $\sqrt w\psi_j$, $j<n$.
The transformed kernel of $\mathcal B$ is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Bkernel}
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}(y,x)=g_0(y)\varphi(x-y)h_0(x),\quad
 g_0(y)=\sqrt{w(y)}m(y)e^{-ay},\quad
 h_0(x)=e^{ax}/\sqrt{w(x)}.
\end{equation}
Our reference is
\[
 C(y,x)=\one_{y<0}\varphi(x-y)\one_{x<0}.
\]
The Fourier multiplier of convolution by $\varphi$ is
$[1+2\cosh(h\xi)]^{-1}$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Corder}
 0\le C\le I/3.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{dsasm:log:ideallemma}
For $E=\widetilde{\mathcal B}-C$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:idealclaim}
 E\in\Stwo,\qquad S=(E+E^*)/2\in\Sone,\qquad \tr S=0.
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathcal B$ is bounded and \eqref{dsasm:log:Bcompression}
is a bounded-operator compression identity.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We give the kernel estimates, including a trace-class criterion.
Partition $\R$ into $I_j=[j,j+1]$. If a kernel $k$ is $C^{2,2}$
on each closed square $I_j\times I_l$, put
\[
 M_{jl}=\max_{0\le r,s\le2}\sup_{I_j\times I_l}
                |\partial_y^r\partial_x^s k(y,x)|.
\]
If $\sum_{j,l}M_{jl}<\infty$, then the operator is trace class.
Indeed, expanding each block in the orthonormal cosine bases, two
integrations by parts in each variable, with the boundary derivative
terms retained, bound the coefficient with indices $r,s$ by
$CM_{jl}/[(1+r^2)(1+s^2)]$. The absolute coefficient sum bounds its
trace norm, and the block trace norms are summable. A jump at zero
is permitted because zero is an interval boundary. The same proof
justifies tracing a diagonal block by integrating its kernel on the
diagonal, followed by the absolutely convergent block sum.

For $y<0$ write $r_y=e^{2ay}$, $d_y=\sqrt{1-r_y+r_y^2}$ and
$b_y=\sqrt{p(y)}$. Directly from \eqref{dsasm:log:wexplicit},
\[
 g_0(y)=\frac{b_y}{\sqrt2(1+r_y)d_y},\qquad
 h_0(y)=\frac{\sqrt2d_y}{b_y}.
\]
On the negative quadrant this gives
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}(y,x)=\frac{b_y}{b_x}\varphi(x-y)+k_e(y,x),
\]
where $k_e$ and its derivatives through order two in each variable
are bounded, for suitable fixed $M,c>0$, by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:expkernel}
 C(1+|x-y|)^M e^{-c|x-y|}(e^{ay}+e^{ax}).
\end{equation}
To verify it, note that $d_y\ge\sqrt3/2$, the smooth factors in
$r_y,r_x$ differ from one by $O(r_y+r_x)$, and
$b_y/b_x\le C(1+|x-y|)$. Differentiation preserves these types of
bounds. The unit-block sum in \eqref{dsasm:log:expkernel} is finite.

The remaining difference from $C$ has kernel
$(b_y/b_x-1)\varphi(x-y)$. Since $b$ is 1-Lipschitz and
$b_x\ge c(1+|x|)$, its modulus is at most
$C|x-y|\varphi(x-y)/(1+|x|)$. Its square is integrable.
Its symmetric part is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:symkernel}
 \frac{(b_y-b_x)^2}{2b_yb_x}\varphi(x-y).
\end{equation}
The same finite collection of derivatives is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:symbound}
 \frac{C(1+|x-y|)^M e^{-c|x-y|}}{(1+|y|)(1+|x|)}.
\end{equation}
This follows by differentiating \eqref{dsasm:log:symkernel}, using bounded
derivatives of $b$, inverse powers of $b$, and
$|b_y-b_x|\le|y-x|$. For fixed block offset the denominators sum
like $\sum_{j\ge1}j^{-2}$; the offset sum has exponential decay.
The criterion proves trace class of the symmetric part.

For $x\ge0$, first subtract the rank-one kernel
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:rankone}
 \frac13[g_0(y)e^{2ay}]
       [h_0(x)e^{-2ax}\one_{x\ge0}].
\end{equation}
Both factors are in $L^2$: the first decays exponentially at both
ends, and the second is $O((1+x)^{-1})$ at $+\infty$ and bounded
at zero. For $u\ge0$, $\varphi(u)-e^{-2au}/3=O(e^{-4au})$,
with the same derivative bounds; for $u<0$ the difference is
$O(e^{-2au})$. The elementary tails needed to estimate the remainder
are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ghbounds}
\begin{array}{ll}
 g_0(y)=O(1+|y|),&y\to-\infty,\\
 g_0(y)=O((1+y)e^{-4ay}),&y\to+\infty,\\
 h_0(x)=O((1+|x|)^{-1}),&x\to-\infty,\\
 h_0(x)=O(e^{2ax}/(1+x)),&x\to+\infty.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
On $x\ge0$, $x\ge y$, the remainder after \eqref{dsasm:log:rankone} has
