summable differentiated block bounds: it decays exponentially in
both tails when $y<0$, and at least exponentially in $x$ up to
polynomial factors when $y\ge0$. On $0\le x<y$ it is bounded by a
polynomial times $e^{-2ay}/(1+x)$, with only $O(1+y)$ blocks for
each $y$-block. The formula is smooth across $x=y$; the split is
only for estimates. Finally, on $y\ge0,x<0$, \eqref{dsasm:log:ghbounds}
and $\varphi(x-y)=O(e^{2a(x-y)})$ give exponential decay in both
tails, including derivatives. The criterion applies on every block,
including those touching zero. This proves the two ideal assertions.

The diagonal formula is legitimate by the absolutely summable cosine
expansions above and the usual rank-one formula. Since
$g_0(y)h_0(y)=m(y)$ and $\varphi(0)=1/9$,
\[
 \tr S=\varphi(0)\int_{\R}[m(y)-\one_{y<0}]\,dy=0,
\]
because $m(y)+m(-y)=1$ and the difference is integrable.
The first-order negative-tail error was only Hilbert--Schmidt;
it is its symmetric part that has the extra decay needed for trace
class. These two classes cannot be conflated.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{dsasm:log:relativeprop}
For $t>0$, set
\[
 Q_n^C(t)=\det_{\Ran\Pi_n}(I+(t-3)\Pi_n C\Pi_n).
\]
There is a positive finite factor $\mathcal A(t)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:relative}
 \frac{D_n(t)/D_n(3)}{Q_n^C(t)}\longrightarrow\mathcal A(t).
\end{equation}
The convergence and normalized analytic logarithms are locally
uniform for $t\in\C\setminus(-\infty,0]$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial functions are complete in $L^2(wdy)$. In fact, $w$
has exponential moments; if $f$ is orthogonal to every polynomial,
Cauchy--Schwarz shows that $fwdy$ has an exponentially integrable
absolute value in a smaller strip. Its Fourier transform is analytic
there with every derivative zero at the origin. Analytic uniqueness
and Fourier uniqueness imply $f=0$. Thus $\Pi_n\to I$ strongly.

Write $C_n=\Pi_n C\Pi_n$, $E_n=\Pi_n E\Pi_n$ on the full Hilbert
space, extended by zero, and similarly $S_n=\Pi_n S\Pi_n$.
Then $E_n\to E$ in $\Stwo$, $S_n\to S$ in $\Sone$ and $C_n\to C$
strongly. For $\delta\in\Omega:=\C\setminus(-\infty,-3]$,
\[
 R_n(\delta)=(I+\delta C_n)^{-1},\quad R(\delta)=(I+\delta C)^{-1}
\]
are uniformly bounded on compact subsets of $\Omega$, and $R_n$ and
their adjoints converge strongly. This follows from
$0\le C_n,C\le I/3$ and continuous functional calculus on that fixed
spectral interval. Therefore $R_nE_n\to RE$ in $\Stwo$, locally
uniformly. Strong convergence combined with compact ideal
approximations justifies each of these ideal products.

The exact finite factorization is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:det2finite}
 \frac{\det[I+\delta(C_n+E_n)]}{\det(I+\delta C_n)}
 =\det{}_2(I+\delta R_nE_n)
       \exp\{\delta\tr(R_nE_n)\}.
\end{equation}
Here $\det_2(I+F)=\det(I+F)e^{-\tr F}$ in finite dimensions, and its
continuous Hilbert--Schmidt extension is used for the limit.
All kernels and projections are real. Write $E=S+K$ with $K$ real
and skew-adjoint. In a real basis $R_n(\delta)$ is transpose-symmetric,
even for complex $\delta$, so
\[
 \tr(R_n\Pi_nK\Pi_n)=0
\]
exactly. Hence $\tr(R_nE_n)\to\tr(RS)$ by trace-norm convergence
of $S_n$. Standard regularized determinant continuity~\cite{simon}
gives the locally uniform limit
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Aformula}
 \mathcal A(t)=\det{}_2\!\left(I+(t-3)(I+(t-3)C)^{-1}E\right)
 \exp\!\left\{(t-3)\tr[(I+(t-3)C)^{-1}S]\right\}.
\end{equation}

Nonvanishing requires a separate argument. Each ratio in
\eqref{dsasm:log:det2finite} is nonzero on $\Omega$: the numerator equals
$D_n(3+\delta)/D_n(3)$ and has only negative real $t$-roots; the
denominator is nonzero by \eqref{dsasm:log:Corder}. The limit equals one
at $\delta=0$. Hurwitz's theorem on the connected slit domain
shows that it is nowhere zero. For real $t>0$ the finite ratios are
positive, so the limit is positive and finite. Normalized analytic
logarithms converge as well. Finally
$\mathcal A(3)=1$ and $(\log\mathcal A)'(3)=\tr S=0$.
\end{proof}

\section{The exact Jacobi projection and the phase determinant}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:jacobiconversion}
Rescale $y=3\sqrt3z$ and put $a_0=5/6$, $b_0=7/6$. Let
\[
 G(z)=\Gamma(a_0+iz)\Gamma(b_0-iz),\qquad v(z)=G(z)/|G(z)|.
\]
Gamma reflection and recurrence give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Gexplicit}
 w(3\sqrt3z)=\frac{27}{8\pi^2}|G(z)|^2,\qquad
 G(z)=\frac{2\pi(1/6-iz)}{\cosh(\pi z)-i\sqrt3\sinh(\pi z)}.
\end{equation}
Thus the polynomial subspace, after unitary rescaling, is $|G|$
times polynomials in $z$. Use the unitary Fourier transform
\[
 (\mathcal Ff)(\xi)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{\R}e^{-iz\xi}f(z)\,dz.
\]
The beta integral, since $a_0+b_0=2$, gives
\[
 G(z)=\int_{\R} e^{iz\xi}\frac{e^{5\xi/6}}{(1+e^\xi)^2}\,d\xi,
 \qquad \mathcal FG=F_0(\xi)=\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{e^{5\xi/6}}{(1+e^\xi)^2}.
\]
Multiplication by a degree-$k$ polynomial in $z$ becomes a differential
operator of degree $k$. For $x=\tanh(\xi/2)$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:derivspace}
 \frac{d}{d\xi}[F_0(\xi)P(x)]
 =F_0(\xi)\left\{(-\tfrac16-x)P(x)+\frac{1-x^2}{2}P'(x)\right\}.
\end{equation}
The degree-raising coefficient on degree $k$ is $-(k+2)/2\ne0$.
Consequently the first $n$ derivatives span exactly $F_0$ times
polynomials of degree $<n$ in $x$, with no asymptotic substitution.
Since
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Jacobiweight}
 |F_0(\xi)|^2d\xi=\frac\pi4(1-x)^{4/3}(1+x)^{2/3}\,dx,
\end{equation}
the rescaling, multiplication by $v$, and Fourier transform carry
$\Pi_n$ exactly to the Jacobi projection $J_n$ with parameters
$(\alpha_J,\beta_J)=(4/3,2/3)$.

Under the rescaling the reference is
\[
 C_z=\one_{z<0}L\one_{z<0},\qquad
 Lf=\varphi_z*f,\quad
 \varphi_z(u)=\frac{\sqrt3}{1+2\cosh(2\pi u)}.
\]
Its multiplier is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:sigmadef}
 \sigma(\xi)=\frac1{1+2\cosh(\xi/3)}.
\end{equation}
Let $H=\mathcal F\one_{z<0}\mathcal F^*$ and $A=HM_\sigma H$.
Then $\mathcal FC_z\mathcal F^*=A$ and $0\le A\le I/3$.
The actual transformed reference includes $v$, so it is necessary
to account for this phase rather than discard it.

\begin{lemma}\label{dsasm:log:phaselemma}
For every $\delta\in\Omega$,
\[
 C_v:=M_vC_zM_v^*,\qquad C_v-C_z\in\Sone,\qquad
 \det[(I+\delta C_v)(I+\delta C_z)^{-1}]=1.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous phase from \eqref{dsasm:log:Gexplicit} is
\[
 \vartheta(z)=-\arctan(6z)+\arctan(\sqrt3\tanh\pi z).
\]
Its limiting values are $\vartheta(\pm\infty)=\mp\pi/6$, and
\[
 \vartheta'(z)=\frac{-6}{1+36z^2}
       +\frac{\sqrt3\pi\,\operatorname{sech}^2(\pi z)}
                    {1+3\tanh^2(\pi z)}.
\]
For each fixed $j\ge1$, $|\vartheta^{(j)}(z)|\le C_j(1+|z|)^{-j-1}$.
On the negative half-line, integration gives
\[
 |\vartheta(z)-\vartheta(s)|
 \le\frac{C|z-s|}{(1+|z|)(1+|s|)}.
\]
The same paired denominator, up to a polynomial in $|z-s|$, bounds
the derivatives needed for the kernel
$[e^{i(\vartheta(z)-\vartheta(s))}-1]\varphi_z(z-s)$.
The trace-class unit-square criterion of Lemma~\ref{dsasm:log:ideallemma}
applies. The bounds hold up to zero.

For each fixed $k\ge1$, the kernel of $C_z^k$ and its fixed-order
derivatives are bounded by a polynomial in $|z-s|$ times
$e^{-c|z-s|}$, by domination of the intermediate half-line
integrals with full-line convolutions. Thus
$C_v^k-C_z^k=M_vC_z^kM_v^*-C_z^k$ satisfies the same trace-class
criterion and has identically zero diagonal. The justified trace
formula yields $\tr(C_v^k-C_z^k)=0$. For $|\delta|<3$,
\[
 \log\det[(I+\delta C_v)(I+\delta C_z)^{-1}]
 =\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}\delta^k}{k}\tr(C_v^k-C_z^k)=0.
\]
Absolute convergence follows from
$\|C_v^k-C_z^k\|_1\le k3^{1-k}\|C_v-C_z\|_1$.
Analytic continuation on $\Omega$ proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

Strong convergence $J_n\to I$, trace-norm convergence of the
compressed difference, and uniform resolvent bounds give
\[
 \frac{\det[I+\delta J_n\mathcal FC_v\mathcal F^*J_n]}
      {\det[I+\delta J_n A J_n]}\longrightarrow1.
\]
All determinants are on $\Ran J_n$. Combining with
Proposition~\ref{dsasm:log:relativeprop} proves the exact reference reduction
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:jacobirelative}
 \frac{D_n(t)/D_n(3)}{Q_n^J(t)}\longrightarrow\mathcal A(t)>0,
 \qquad Q_n^J(t)=\det(I+(t-3)J_nHM_\sigma HJ_n).
\end{equation}
The phase has limiting relative determinant one, not just a bounded
unspecified error. The remaining Hardy projections will be handled
by a finite overlap bound; they cannot yet be replaced by a scalar
multiplier.

\section{Endpoint controlled Jacobi means}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:mean}
We now prove the mean estimate required for the determinant. The
symbol has angular endpoint exponent $2/3$; a smooth-test theorem
cannot be used without checking this endpoint behavior.
Use temporarily general fixed Jacobi parameters $a_J,b_J$, set
$s_J=a_J+b_J$, and write
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Jacobinorm}
 h_k^{J}=\frac{2^{s_J+1}\Gamma(k+a_J+1)\Gamma(k+b_J+1)}
 {(2k+s_J+1)\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(k+s_J+1)}.
\end{equation}
The real orthonormal angular functions on $I=(0,\pi)$ are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:angularfunctions}
 u_k(\theta)=\sqrt{\frac{(1-\cos\theta)^{a_J}(1+\cos\theta)^{b_J}
                                  \sin\theta}{h_k^{J}}}
                  P_k^{(a_J,b_J)}(\cos\theta),\qquad
 \rho_n(\theta)=\sum_{k<n}u_k(\theta)^2.
\end{equation}
The constant $\pi/4$ in \eqref{dsasm:log:Jacobiweight} cancels in the normalized
projection density. In the application $(a_J,b_J)=(4/3,2/3)$.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:log:meantheorem}
For these parameters, suppose $F(\theta)=f(\cos\theta)$ is real
measurable and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:meancondition}
 \int_0^\pi\frac{|F(\theta)|}{\delta(\theta)}\,d\theta<\infty,
 \qquad \delta(\theta)=\min(\theta,\pi-\theta).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:meanformula}
 \tr(J_nM_fJ_n)=(n+1)\mu_f+o(1),\qquad
 \mu_f=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi F(\theta)\,d\theta.
\end{equation}
For an unbounded test the left side means the finite quadratic-form
matrix with entries $\int F u_i u_j$; it does not assert that
$M_fu_i$ lies in $L^2$. The actual tests below are bounded.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The normalized uniform Bessel input}
Set
\[
 N_k=k+(s_J+1)/2,\quad
 \Phi_k=N_k\theta-\pi(a_J/2+1/4),
\]
\[
 a_1(\theta)=\frac{(1/4-a_J^2)\cot(\theta/2)
                         -(1/4-b_J^2)\tan(\theta/2)}4.
\]
The uniform Frenzen--Wong Bessel expansion~\cite{fw}, in the form
DLMF 18.15.6--18.15.9~\cite{dlmf}, implies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:angularasymp}
 u_k(\theta)=\sqrt{2/\pi}
 \left\{\cos\Phi_k+\frac{a_1(\theta)}{N_k}\sin\Phi_k
                     +O((N_k\delta(\theta))^{-2})\right\}
\end{equation}
uniformly where $N_k\delta\ge1$, with $|a_1(\theta)|\le C/\delta$.
Here and below finitely many small degrees can be absorbed in the
constant. We give the normalization details because a spurious
$1/k$ amplitude term would change the mean.

On $0<\theta\le\pi/2$, angular normalization of the source formula
produces a coefficient $\varkappa_k$ satisfying
\[
 \varkappa_k^2=
 \frac{\Gamma(k+a_J+1)\Gamma(k+s_J+1)}
      {N_k^{2a_J}\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(k+b_J+1)}.
\]
The first relative coefficient of the two gamma ratios is
$a_J(s_J+1)/k$, exactly that of $N_k^{2a_J}/k^{2a_J}$.
DLMF 5.11.13 therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:kappacancel}
 \varkappa_k=1+O((k+1)^{-2}).
\end{equation}
There is no $1/k$ cosine-amplitude correction.
With
\[
 g_J(\theta)=(1/4-a_J^2)\bigl(\cot(\theta/2)-2/\theta\bigr)
                       -(1/4-b_J^2)\tan(\theta/2),
 \qquad B_0=g_J/(4\theta),
\]
the first normalized Bessel terms are
\begin{align*}
 \varkappa_k\biggl[&\sqrt{N_k\theta}J_{a_J}(N_k\theta)
 +\frac{\theta B_0(\theta)}{N_k}
                     \sqrt{N_k\theta}J_{a_J+1}(N_k\theta)\\
 &+\frac{A_1(\theta)}{N_k^2}
                     \sqrt{N_k\theta}J_{a_J}(N_k\theta)\biggr].
\end{align*}
The source coefficients $B_0,A_1$ are bounded on this half interval.
For truncation order $M=1$, the normalized remainder on
$N_k\theta\ge1$ is $\theta O(N_k^{-3})$. The two-term fixed-order
Bessel expansion (DLMF 10.17.3) gives the sine coefficient
\[
 -\frac{4a_J^2-1}{8\theta}+\theta B_0(\theta)=a_1(\theta),
\]
and all remaining terms are $O(N_k^{-2}\theta^{-2})$.
Equation \eqref{dsasm:log:kappacancel} preserves that error. Reflection and
interchange of parameters prove \eqref{dsasm:log:angularasymp} on the other
half interval.

The same source with $M=0$ gives, uniformly in $k,\theta$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:endpointbounds}
 |u_k(\theta)|\le C,\qquad
 |u_k(\theta)|\le C((k+1)\theta)^{a_J+1/2}
       \quad((k+1)\theta\le1),
\end{equation}
and the reflected bound with $b_J$ at $\pi$. This uses the boundedness
of $\sqrt rJ_{a_J}(r)$ for $r\ge1$ and its small-argument power.
The normalized remainder in the small-argument region is
$\theta^{a_J+2}O(N_k^{a_J-2M+1/2})$, which is dominated by the stated
endpoint bound. The families required below are the base pair,
its reflection, the simultaneous shift $(7/3,5/3)$ and its reflection,
and the single shifts $(7/3,2/3)$ and $(5/3,4/3)$.
All parameters exceed $-1/2$, their sums exceed $-1$, and their
differences exceed $-2$ in each orientation needed. Thus the
Frenzen--Wong Main Theorem condition $a_J-b_J>-2m$ holds already
with truncation order $m=1$. No shifted family is silently
identified with the base family.

\subsection{Global density bounds and the interior constant}
We first show
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:densitybounds}
 \rho_n(\theta)\le Cn,\qquad
 |\rho_n(\theta)-n/\pi|\le C/\delta(\theta).
\end{equation}
The first follows from \eqref{dsasm:log:endpointbounds}. For the second,
choose $m\asymp1/\delta$ so that \eqref{dsasm:log:angularasymp} applies to
$k\ge m$. The first $m$ terms contribute $O(1/\delta)$, or contain
all terms. Squaring the expansion on the remaining terms gives
\[
 u_k^2=\frac1\pi+\frac{\cos(2\Phi_k)}\pi
       +\frac{2a_1(\theta)}{\pi N_k}\sin(2\Phi_k)
       +O(N_k^{-2}\delta^{-2}).
\]
The cosine sum is $O(1/|\sin\theta|)=O(1/\delta)$.
Abel summation with the same geometric bound gives
\[
 \left|\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}\frac{e^{2ik\theta}}{N_k}\right|
 \le\frac{C}{N_m|\sin\theta|}.
\]
After multiplication by $a_1=O(1/\delta)$ this is $O(1/\delta)$;
the squared-error sum is also $O(1/(m\delta^2))=O(1/\delta)$.
If $\delta<1/n$, the first inequality of \eqref{dsasm:log:densitybounds}
suffices. Summing the sine correction absolutely instead would
lose its cancellation and introduce an unjustified logarithm.

For the constant term restrict now to a fixed
$[\epsilon,\pi-\epsilon]$. The exact derivative identity is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Jacderiv}
 u_n'(\theta)=q_J(\theta)u_n(\theta)
       -\sqrt{n(n+s_J+1)}\,u_{n-1}^{(a_J+1,b_J+1)}(\theta),
\end{equation}
where
\[
 q_J(\theta)=\frac{(2a_J+1)\cot(\theta/2)
                           -(2b_J+1)\tan(\theta/2)}4.
\]
This follows from DLMF 18.9.15 and the exact norm ratio in
\eqref{dsasm:log:Jacobinorm}. The shifted function has frequency $N_n$,
phase $\Phi_n-\pi/2$, and correction
$a_1^{\rm shifted}+q_J=a_1$. Applying \eqref{dsasm:log:angularasymp} to the
two families in \eqref{dsasm:log:Jacderiv} gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:angularDeriv}
 u_n'=\sqrt{2/\pi}
       [-N_n\sin\Phi_n+a_1(\theta)\cos\Phi_n+O_\epsilon(n^{-1})].
\end{equation}
We have used an exact derivative identity, rather than differentiated
an uncontrolled asymptotic remainder.
The orthonormal recurrence coefficient is
\[
 r_n^J=\frac2{2n+s_J}
 \sqrt{\frac{n(n+a_J)(n+b_J)(n+s_J)}
 {(2n+s_J-1)(2n+s_J+1)}}=\frac12+O(n^{-2}).
\]
The confluent Christoffel--Darboux formula is
\[
 \rho_n(\theta)=\frac{r_n^J}{\sin\theta}
                    (u_nu_{n-1}'-u_n'u_{n-1}).
\]
Write $N=N_n$, $\Phi=\Phi_n$. Substitution of
\eqref{dsasm:log:angularasymp} and \eqref{dsasm:log:angularDeriv} yields
\[
 u_nu_{n-1}'-u_n'u_{n-1}
 =\frac2\pi\left[(N-\tfrac12)\sin\theta
                   +\tfrac12\sin(2\Phi-\theta)\right]
                   +O_\epsilon(n^{-1}).
\]
The cosine-cosine first corrections cancel; the sine-sine correction
has factor $N/(N-1)-(N-1)/N=O(n^{-1})$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:densityconstant}
 \rho_n(\theta)=\frac{n+s_J/2}{\pi}
 +\frac{\sin((2n+s_J)\theta-\pi(a_J+1/2))}{2\pi\sin\theta}
 +O_\epsilon(n^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Here $s_J=2$, so the nonoscillatory constant is $(n+1)/\pi$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:meantheorem}]
The trace is exactly $\int F\rho_n$. For fixed $\epsilon>0$,
\eqref{dsasm:log:densityconstant} and the Riemann--Lebesgue lemma show
\[
 \int_\epsilon^{\pi-\epsilon}F(\theta)
          [\rho_n(\theta)-(n+1)/\pi]\,d\theta\longrightarrow0.
\]
The oscillatory multiplier $F/\sin\theta$ is integrable on that
compact interval. By \eqref{dsasm:log:densitybounds}, the absolute contribution
of the two omitted endpoints is at most
\[
 C\int_{\delta<\epsilon}|F(\theta)|/\delta(\theta)\,d\theta
\]
uniformly in $n$. Condition \eqref{dsasm:log:meancondition} makes this tend
to zero. First taking $n\to\infty$, then $\epsilon\to0$, proves
the result. The bounded angular basis also proves that the finite
quadratic-form entries are well defined.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact scalar means}
Under $x=\cos\theta=\tanh(\xi/2)$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:angularsymbol}
 w(\theta):=\sigma(\xi(\theta))
 =\frac1{1+\cot^{2/3}(\theta/2)+\tan^{2/3}(\theta/2)},
 \qquad \xi(\theta)=2\log\cot(\theta/2).
\end{equation}
Thus $0\le w\le1/3$ and $w=O(\delta^{2/3})$ at both endpoints.
The notation $w(\theta)$ here is the angular symbol, not the earlier
real-line polynomial weight $w(y)$. Let
\[
 V(\theta)=\log(1-2w(\theta)),\qquad -\log3\le V\le0.
\]
Since $|V|\le6w$, both tests satisfy \eqref{dsasm:log:meancondition}.
Their means are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:scalarmeans}
 \mu_w=2-\sqrt3,\qquad \mu_V=2\log(2/3).
\end{equation}
Here is an elementary integral verification independent of any DSASM
pressure theorem. Define, only as a scalar integral,
\[
 J(t)=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi\log[1+(t-3)w(\theta)]\,d\theta,
 \qquad t>0.
\]
With $v=\tan^{1/3}(\theta/2)$, differentiation gives
\[
 J'(t)=\frac6\pi\int_0^\infty
 \frac{v^4\,dv}{(1+v^6)(v^4+(t-2)v^2+1)}.
\]
For $t\ne1,4$ the rational integrand equals
\begin{align*}
 &-\frac1{3(t-4)(v^2+1)}
 +\frac{v^2+1}{3(t-1)(v^4-v^2+1)}\\
 &\hspace{35mm}
 +\frac{v^2+1}{(t-1)(t-4)(v^4+(t-2)v^2+1)}.
\end{align*}
Using $\int_0^\infty (1+v^2)/(v^4+cv^2+1)\,dv
=\pi/\sqrt{c+2}$ for $c>-2$, which follows by the change
$v-1/v$ or by elementary partial fractions, we obtain
\[
 J'(t)=\frac{\sqrt t+3}{\sqrt t(\sqrt t+1)(\sqrt t+2)}.
\]
The removable cases $t=1,4$ follow by continuity of the original
integral. Since $J(3)=0$, integration yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Jintegral}
 J(t)=2\log\frac{(\sqrt t+1)^2}{2(\sqrt t+2)}.
\end{equation}
Evaluating $J'(3)$ and $J(1)$ proves \eqref{dsasm:log:scalarmeans}. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:appliedmean}
 \tr(J_nM_\sigma J_n)=(n+1)(2-\sqrt3)+o(1),\qquad
 \tr(J_nM_VJ_n)=2(n+1)\log(2/3)+o(1).
\end{equation}

\section{The scalar determinant has a bounded remainder}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:scalar}
We prove
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:scalarresult}
 \log T_n=2n\log(2/3)+O(1),\qquad
 T_n=\det_{\Ran J_n}(J_ne^{M_V}J_n)
     =\det(I-2J_nM_\sigma J_n).
\end{equation}
The proof combines a uniform endpoint kernel estimate with an exact
finite-dimensional exponential-tilt identity. It does not require
asymptotics for the tilted Jacobi weight.

\subsection{Adjacent cancellation and a global kernel bound}
For the base Jacobi pair, the reflected and reindexed contiguous
identity DLMF 18.9.6 is
\begin{align*}
 &(2n+s_J)(1-x)P_{n-1}^{(a_J+1,b_J)}(x)\\
 &\hspace{15mm}=2(n+a_J)P_{n-1}^{(a_J,b_J)}(x)-2nP_n^{(a_J,b_J)}(x).
\end{align*}
After angular normalization,
\[
 u_n=a_n^*u_{n-1}-b_n^*\sqrt{1-\cos\theta}\,
                 u_{n-1}^{(a_J+1,b_J)},
\]
where direct gamma cancellation gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:adjacentcoeff}
 (a_n^*)^2=\frac{(n+a_J)(n+s_J)(2n+s_J+1)}
                   {n(n+b_J)(2n+s_J-1)},\qquad
 (b_n^*)^2=\frac{(2n+s_J)(2n+s_J+1)}{2n(n+b_J)}.
\end{equation}
Hence $a_n^*-1=O(1/n)$ and $b_n^*=O(1)$. Since $a_J+1/2\ge1$,
\eqref{dsasm:log:endpointbounds} gives $|u_{n-1}(\theta)|/n\le C\theta$
on the left half interval: use boundedness for $\theta\ge1/n$
and the endpoint power for $\theta<1/n$. The shifted family is
uniformly bounded. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:endpointcancel}
 |u_n(\theta)-u_{n-1}(\theta)|\le C\theta,\qquad
 |u_n(\theta)+u_{n-1}(\theta)|\le C(\pi-\theta).
\end{equation}
The second follows by Jacobi reflection and $b_J+1/2\ge1$.
Uniform boundedness extends both inequalities over all of $I$.

The angular Christoffel--Darboux kernel is
\[
 K_n(\theta,\phi)=\sum_{k<n}u_k(\theta)u_k(\phi)
 =r_n^J\frac{u_n(\theta)u_{n-1}(\phi)-u_{n-1}(\theta)u_n(\phi)}
                  {\cos\theta-\cos\phi}.
\]
Using first the difference, then the sum in \eqref{dsasm:log:endpointcancel},
the numerator is bounded by both $C(\theta+\phi)$ and
$C(2\pi-\theta-\phi)$. Meanwhile
\[
 |\cos\theta-\cos\phi|
 \ge c\min(\theta+\phi,2\pi-\theta-\phi)|\theta-\phi|.
\]
The recurrence coefficient is bounded, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:kernelglobal}
 |K_n(\theta,\phi)|\le C/|\theta-\phi|\qquad(\theta\ne\phi),
\end{equation}
uniformly in $n$ on the entire square. Endpoint cancellation, rather
than extrapolation of a bulk estimate, proves this bound.

\subsection{Uniform variance and the exact tilted projection}
The symbol $V$ is globally H\"older of exponent $\gamma=2/3$.
Indeed on the left half interval $w=r/(1+r+r^2)$ with
$r=\tan^{2/3}(\theta/2)$; the derivative in $r\in[0,1]$ is bounded
and $r'=O(\theta^{-1/3})$. Reflection and
$|d\log(1-2w)/dw|\le6$ give
$|V(\theta)-V(\phi)|\le C|\theta-\phi|^{2/3}$.
For the angular projection $P=J_n$ the exact variance identity is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:varianceidentity}
\begin{split}
 \mathcal V_n&=\|(I-P)VP\|_\HS^2
 =\tr(PV^2P)-\tr((PVP)^2)\\
 &=\frac12\int_I\int_I
  (V(\theta)-V(\phi))^2|K_n(\theta,\phi)|^2\,d\theta\,d\phi.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{dsasm:log:kernelglobal}, the integrand is at most
$C|\theta-\phi|^{-2/3}$, which is integrable. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:variancebound}
 \sup_n\mathcal V_n<\infty.
\end{equation}

For $0\le u\le1$, set on $\Ran P$
\[
 T(u)=Pe^{uV}P,\quad F(u)=\log\det T(u),\quad
 E_u=e^{uV/2},\quad R_u=E_uP T(u)^{-1/2},\quad Q_u=R_uR_u^*.
\]
All inverses are finite-dimensional. Since $R_u^*R_u=I$, $Q_u$
is the orthogonal projection onto $E_u\Ran P$. Differentiating the
finite determinant, and using cyclicity, gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:tiltidentity}
 F'(u)=\tr(Q_uV),\qquad
 F''(u)=\tr(Q_uV^2)-\tr(Q_uVQ_uV)
       =\|(I-Q_u)VR_u\|_\HS^2\ge0.
\end{equation}
The crucial ordered identity is
\[
 (I-Q_u)VR_u=(I-Q_u)E_u(I-P)VP T(u)^{-1/2}.
\]
It uses only $VE_u=E_uV$ and $(I-Q_u)E_uP=0$.
If $m\le V\le M$, the operator norms therefore yield
\[
 F''(u)\le e^{u(M-m)}\mathcal V_n.
\]
Here $m=-\log3$, $M=0$, so $F''(u)\le3^u\mathcal V_n$.
Taylor's integral remainder, $F(0)=0$, and $F'(0)=\tr(PVP)$ give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:jensengap}
 0\le\log\det(Pe^VP)-\tr(PVP)
 \le\frac{2-\log3}{(\log3)^2}\mathcal V_n=O(1).
\end{equation}
No commutation of $P$ with the multiplier or its exponential has
been assumed. Combining \eqref{dsasm:log:jensengap} with \eqref{dsasm:log:appliedmean}
proves \eqref{dsasm:log:scalarresult}.

\section{A uniform weighted Hardy overlap bound}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:overlap}
Return to the exact projection $J_n$ in $L^2(d\xi)$ and put
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:ABdefs}
 A=HM_\sigma H,\qquad B=(I-H)M_\sigma(I-H),\qquad D=A+B,
\end{equation}
\[
 A_n=J_nAJ_n,\qquad B_n=J_nBJ_n
\]
on $\Ran J_n$. These are now the Hardy Gram matrices; the weighted
shift of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:finiteoperator} is no longer used.
Although $AB=0$ in the ambient space, the compressed matrices need
not commute or have zero product. We prove the estimate which
retains precisely this finite-projection interaction.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:log:overlaptheorem}
The exact compressed matrices satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:overlapbound}
 \sup_{n\ge1}\tr(A_nB_n)<\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Angular Hardy coordinates and weighted wave comparison}
The map
\[
 (U_{\rm ang}f)(\theta)=\sqrt{2/\sin\theta}\,
           f(2\log\cot(\theta/2))
\]
is unitary from $L^2(d\xi)$ to $L^2(I,d\theta)$.
Write $H_\theta=U_{\rm ang}HU_{\rm ang}^*$,
$w(\theta)=\sigma(\xi(\theta))$, and $s(\theta)=\sqrt{w(\theta)}$.
By \eqref{dsasm:log:angularsymbol},
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:weightdecay}
 0\le w\le1/3,\qquad w(\theta)\le C\delta(\theta)^\gamma,
 \qquad \gamma=2/3.
\end{equation}
Let $U:\ell^2(\N_0)\to L^2(I)$ synthesize the real Jacobi basis:
$Ue_k=u_k$. Completeness makes it unitary. Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:waves}
\begin{split}
 N_k&=k+3/2,\quad c=11\pi/12,\quad
 v_k=\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos(N_k\theta-c),\\
 v_k^-&=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-iN_k\theta+ic},\qquad
 v_k^+=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{iN_k\theta-ic}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Let $V_-,V_+$ be the corresponding synthesis maps and $V=V_-+V_+$.
Each one-sided family is the restriction of an orthogonal Fourier
family on an interval of length $2\pi$, multiplied by one fixed
unimodular factor. Consequently $\|V_\pm\|\le1$ and $\|V\|\le2$.
Orthogonality on $I$ itself is neither asserted nor required.

Equations \eqref{dsasm:log:angularasymp} and \eqref{dsasm:log:endpointbounds} give
\[
 |u_k-v_k|\le C\min\{1,((k+1)\delta)^{-1}\}.
\]
With $\gamma=2/3$, integration using \eqref{dsasm:log:weightdecay}
therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \|s(u_k-v_k)\|_2^2
 &\le C\left\{\int_0^{1/(k+1)}r^\gamma\,dr
 +(k+1)^{-2}\int_{1/(k+1)}^{\pi/2}r^{\gamma-2}\,dr\right\}\\
 &\le C(k+1)^{-1-\gamma}.
\end{align*}
The column squares sum, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:weightedUV}
 M_s(U-V)\in\Stwo.
\end{equation}
The unweighted map $U-V$ is separately bounded.

\subsection{The coordinate defect has a weighted square integrable kernel}
Let $C_I$ be the compression to $I$ of the negative-frequency
Hardy projection on $L^2(\R,d\theta)$. It is a positive contraction,
not generally a projection on $L^2(I)$. Set
$f(\theta)=\log\tan(\theta/2)$, so $f'=1/\sin\theta$ and
$\xi=-2f$. The Fourier convention gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:Hardykernels}
\begin{split}
 C_I&=\tfrac12 I-\frac{i}{2\pi}\PV\frac1{\theta-\phi},\\
 H_\theta&=\tfrac12 I-\frac{i}{2\pi}\PV
 \frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
These are kernel formulas with respect to $d\phi$. To check the
sign, the original $H$ is
$\tfrac12I+(i/2\pi)\PV(\xi-\eta)^{-1}$; the decreasing coordinate
map contributes the displayed minus sign and cancels its factor two
against the square-root Jacobians. Locally $H_\theta$ thus selects
the negative-frequency branch $v_k^-$.
The transformed principal value is the ordinary angular principal
value on compactly supported smooth inputs: the ratio of the two
angular exclusion radii induced by a symmetric $\xi$ exclusion tends
to one. Its possible diagonal residual is proportional to the
logarithm of that ratio, hence zero.

Define the diagonal-subtracted kernel
\[
 d(\theta,\phi)=
 \frac{\sqrt{f'(\theta)f'(\phi)}}{f(\theta)-f(\phi)}
             -\frac1{\theta-\phi}.
\]
We claim the global row-square estimate
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:rowbound}
 \int_I|d(\theta,\phi)|^2\,d\phi\le C/\delta(\theta).
\end{equation}
Here is a direct proof covering same and opposite endpoints at once.
Fix $a=\delta(\theta)$ and split at $|\phi-\theta|<a/2$.
On this inner interval, $f'\asymp1/a$, $|f''|\le C/a^2$, and
Taylor comparison of the endpoint geometric mean of $f'$ with its
interval average bounds the difference of the two Cauchy kernels by
$C/a$. Its row-square contribution is $O(1/a)$; the singularity on
the diagonal cancels.
Outside this interval, the ordinary Cauchy kernel has squared integral
$O(1/a)$. For the transformed kernel, substitute $\eta=f(\phi)$:
its squared row integral is
\[
 f'(\theta)\int_{\text{outside image}}
                \frac{d\eta}{|f(\theta)-\eta|^2}.
\]
The excluded inner interval maps to an interval containing a fixed
radius neighborhood of $f(\theta)$, because $f'\asymp1/a$ there.
The last integral is uniformly bounded, and $f'(\theta)\le C/a$.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:log:rowbound}, including opposite endpoint interactions
and all transition regions.
Since $\int_I w/\delta<\infty$, it follows that
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:coordHS}
 M_s(H_\theta-C_I)\in\Stwo.
\end{equation}
The singular-integral identities first hold on smooth compactly
supported inputs; the actual weighted Hilbert--Schmidt kernel and
boundedness of the operators extend the identity to all $L^2$ inputs.

\subsection{The noncommuting multiplier and the interval tails}
We also need
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:commutator}
 [M_s,H_\theta]\in\Stwo.
\end{equation}
Undoing angular coordinates, the multiplier is
$s_0(\xi)=(1+2\cosh(\xi/3))^{-1/2}$, with $s_0,s_0'\in L^2(\R)$.
Its Hardy commutator has, up to a constant, kernel
$(s_0(\xi)-s_0(\eta))/(\xi-\eta)$. For $|r|<1$,
\[
 \|s_0(\cdot+r)-s_0\|_2^2\le r^2\|s_0'\|_2^2,
\]
and for $|r|\ge1$ it is at most $4\|s_0\|_2^2$. Integrating after
division by $r^2$ proves \eqref{dsasm:log:commutator}. Hence the ordered identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:orderedcommutator}
 M_sH_\theta(U-V)
 =H_\theta[M_s(U-V)]+[M_s,H_\theta](U-V)\in\Stwo
\end{equation}
is valid. A weighted bound on $U-V$ alone would not permit the weight
to pass through the Hardy projection.

The full-line negative-frequency projection selects
$e^{-iN\theta}$ and kills $e^{iN\theta}$ for $N>0$.
After compression to $I$, subtraction of the omitted tails gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:tailbound}
 |C_Ie^{-iN\theta}-e^{-iN\theta}|+|C_Ie^{iN\theta}|
 \le C\{L(N\theta)+L(N(\pi-\theta))\},
\end{equation}
where $L(r)=1+|\log r|$ for $0<r\le1$, and $L(r)=1/r$ for $r\ge1$.
The tails are multiples of $\int_r^\infty e^{\pm iu}\,du/u$.
Integration by parts bounds them by $2/r$ for $r\ge1$; splitting
at one gives $|\log r|+2$ otherwise. The full-line wave identity
can be justified by Abel damping before this subtraction.
For $0<\gamma<1$, $\int_0^\infty r^\gamma L(r)^2\,dr<\infty$;
using \eqref{dsasm:log:weightdecay} in \eqref{dsasm:log:tailbound} gives
\[
 \|s(C_Iv_k-v_k^-)\|_2^2\le C(k+1)^{-1-\gamma}.
\]
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:waveHS}
 M_s(C_IV-V_-)\in\Stwo.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fixed Hilbert Schmidt errors and the finite cutoff}
Define bounded maps from the coefficient space by
\[
 X=M_sH_\theta U,\quad Y=M_s(I-H_\theta)U,\quad
 X_0=M_sV_-,\quad Y_0=M_sV_+.
\]
Combining \eqref{dsasm:log:coordHS}, \eqref{dsasm:log:orderedcommutator}, and
\eqref{dsasm:log:waveHS},
\[
 X-X_0=M_sH_\theta(U-V)+M_s(H_\theta-C_I)V
                           +M_s(C_IV-V_-)\in\Stwo.
\]
Also $Y-Y_0=M_s(U-V)-(X-X_0)\in\Stwo$.
These are fixed operators on the full coefficient space, with finite
norms independent of $n$. Let $P_n^0$ be the first-$n$ coordinate
projection in $\ell^2(\N_0)$. The exact finite matrices are
\[
 A_n=P_n^0X^*XP_n^0,\qquad B_n=P_n^0Y^*YP_n^0.
\]
Finite-rank cyclicity gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:overlapidentity}
 \tr(A_nB_n)=\|XP_n^0Y^*\|_\HS^2.
\end{equation}
Every occurrence of the finite cutoff is retained.

The model operator $X_0P_n^0Y_0^*$ has kernel
\[
 \frac{s(\theta)s(\phi)}{2\pi}
 e^{2ic-(3i/2)(\theta+\phi)}
             \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}e^{-ik(\theta+\phi)}.
\]
The geometric sum is at most $1/|\sin((\theta+\phi)/2)|$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:modelHS}
 \|X_0P_n^0Y_0^*\|_\HS^2
 \le\frac1{4\pi^2}\int_I\int_I
       \frac{w(\theta)w(\phi)}{\sin^2((\theta+\phi)/2)}\,d\theta\,d\phi
 <\infty
\end{equation}
uniformly in $n$. The only singular corners are $(0,0)$ and
$(\pi,\pi)$. At the first the integrand is at most
$C\theta^\gamma\phi^\gamma/(\theta+\phi)^2$.
The change $\theta=ru$, $\phi=r(1-u)$ leaves the factors
$r^{2\gamma-1}dr$ and $u^\gamma(1-u)^\gamma du$, both integrable.
Reflection handles the second corner. Opposite corners are
nonsingular because their sum is near $\pi$.
Finally,
\[
 XP_n^0Y^*=X_0P_n^0Y_0^*+(X-X_0)P_n^0Y^*
                          +X_0P_n^0(Y-Y_0)^*.
\]
The last two terms have uniformly bounded Hilbert--Schmidt norms by
the ideal property. Equation \eqref{dsasm:log:modelHS} then proves a uniform
bound on \eqref{dsasm:log:overlapidentity}, completing
Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:overlaptheorem}.

\section{The determinant transfer and the first calibration trace}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:transfer}
We now combine the estimates, keeping the finite matrices
noncommuting throughout.

\subsection{Complementary Hardy pieces and their cross term}
Because the two summands of $D$ in \eqref{dsasm:log:ABdefs} have orthogonal
ambient support,
\[
 0\le D\le I/3,\qquad A_n+B_n\le I/3.
\]
The difference $M_\sigma-D$ is trace class. Undoing the Fourier
transform, it consists of the two opposite-half-line blocks of
convolution by $\varphi_z$. There $|z-s|=|z|+|s|$, so its kernel
and all fixed derivatives decay exponentially in both variables.
The unit-square criterion applies including the squares touching
zero. Put
\[
 S_n^J=\det(I-2(A_n+B_n)),\qquad T_n=\det(I-2J_nM_\sigma J_n).
\]
Strong convergence $J_n\to I$, trace-norm convergence of the
compressed cross term and the uniform resolvent bound give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:crossdet}
 \frac{S_n^J}{T_n}\longrightarrow
 \Xi=\det[(I-2D)(I-2M_\sigma)^{-1}]>0.
\end{equation}
The limiting relative operator is invertible, so its determinant is
nonzero; positivity follows from the positive finite ratios. The
cross kernel has zero diagonal, but that alone does not make the
relative determinant equal to one.

Complex conjugation in the real Jacobi basis interchanges $H$ and
$I-H$, and commutes with $J_n$ and $M_\sigma$. Thus $A_n,B_n$ have
the same eigenvalues and
\[
 \det(I-2A_n)=\det(I-2B_n)=Q_n^J(1).
\]
For $0\le s,u\le1$ define
\[
 F(s,u)=\log\det[I-2sA_n-2uB_n],\quad
 R_{su}=(I-2sA_n-2uB_n)^{-1}.
\]
The matrices are positive and $\|R_{su}\|\le3$. Direct finite
matrix differentiation gives
\[
 \partial_s\partial_u F
 =-4\tr(R_{su}A_nR_{su}B_n)
 =-4\|A_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}\|_\HS^2.
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:mixedgap}
 \Delta_n:=2\log Q_n^J(1)-\log S_n^J
 =4\int_0^1\int_0^1
           \|A_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}\|_\HS^2\,ds\,du\ge0.
\end{equation}
To bound this without an invalid noncommutative trace estimate,
put $C_s=I-2sA_n$ and use the resolvent equation. It gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:resfactor}
 A_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}
 =C_s^{-1}A_n^{1/2}B_n^{1/2}
             [I+2uB_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}].
\end{equation}
The two outside factors have norm at most three. The squared
Hilbert--Schmidt norm is thus at most $81\tr(A_nB_n)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:324}
 0\le\Delta_n\le324\tr(A_nB_n)=O(1).
\end{equation}
It would in general be unjustified simply to bound
$\tr(RARA')$ by $\|R\|^2\tr(AA')$ for arbitrary positive
noncommuting matrices; \eqref{dsasm:log:resfactor} supplies the needed
ordered factorization.

By \eqref{dsasm:log:scalarresult}, \eqref{dsasm:log:crossdet}, and \eqref{dsasm:log:324},
\[
 \log Q_n^J(1)=n\log(2/3)+O(1).
\]
The positive relative limit \eqref{dsasm:log:jacobirelative} now proves
\eqref{dsasm:log:Dmain}. Both the lower and upper bound have been established;
no infinite-rank substitution was made in the overlap estimate.

\subsection{The constant correction in the first trace}
Let $L$ be convolution by $\varphi_z$ and
$C_-=\one_{z<0}L\one_{z<0}$, $C_+=\one_{z>0}L\one_{z>0}$.
In the positive $|G|$ gauge the polynomial projection, denoted here
$\widehat\Pi_n$, is invariant under $z\mapsto-z$, since its weight
is even. The kernel of $L$ is even, so
\[
 \tr(\widehat\Pi_nC_-\widehat\Pi_n)
 =\tr(\widehat\Pi_nC_+\widehat\Pi_n).
\]
The cross operator $L-C_--C_+$ is trace class by the same opposite
half-line argument and has trace zero. Strong projection convergence
gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:halftraceJacobi}
 \tr(\widehat\Pi_nC_-\widehat\Pi_n)
          -\tfrac12\tr(\widehat\Pi_nL\widehat\Pi_n)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The full-line phase difference $L_v-L=M_vLM_v^*-L$ is also trace
class with trace zero. On the two same-sign quadrants this follows
from the phase bounds of Lemma~\ref{dsasm:log:phaselemma}; on opposite-sign
quadrants exponential decay controls both tails. Its kernel has
zero diagonal, and the same criterion justifies the trace formula.
The Fourier-phase transformation of the exact projection therefore
gives
\[
 \tr(\widehat\Pi_nL\widehat\Pi_n)
             -\tr(J_nM_\sigma J_n)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Finally \eqref{dsasm:log:Bcompression} implies
$D_n'(3)/D_n(3)=\tr(\Pi_n\widetilde{\mathcal B}\Pi_n)$.
The skew part of $E$ has zero finite compressed trace in the real
basis, while $\tr(\Pi_n S\Pi_n)\to\tr S=0$. Combining this with
\eqref{dsasm:log:halftraceJacobi} yields
\[
 \frac{D_n'(3)}{D_n(3)}-\frac12\tr(J_nM_\sigma J_n)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Equation \eqref{dsasm:log:appliedmean} proves \eqref{dsasm:log:firstmain}. This exact
first-trace centering does not by itself control all trace powers
or imply a determinant amplitude.

\subsection{From adjacent products to individual counts}
Let $c=\sqrt3$, $f_1=\log(2/3)$ and
$R_n=\log[Z_n(1)/Z_n(c)]$. The definition \eqref{dsasm:log:Ddef} and
\eqref{dsasm:log:Dmain} give
\[
 R_n+R_{n+1}=nf_1+O(1)-\tfrac12\log3.
\]
By \eqref{dsasm:log:ratioadjacent}, $|R_{n+1}-R_n|\le\tfrac12\log3$.
Subtracting this bounded difference from the sum proves
\[
 R_n=\tfrac n2\log(2/3)+O(1).
\]
Adding the all-size calibration \eqref{dsasm:log:W} and using
$b+\tfrac12\log(2/3)=\beta$ proves \eqref{dsasm:log:logmain}. Exponentiation
gives \eqref{dsasm:log:bounds} for all sufficiently large $n$; positivity of
finitely many smaller counts permits enlargement of the constants
to cover every $n\ge1$. In particular,
\[
 0<\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}}
 \le\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{e^{\alpha n^2+\beta n}n^{-5/72}}
 <\infty.
\]

\section{Integer inverse thresholds and the logarithmic correction}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:inverse}
The identity $Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}$ from~\cite[Proposition 5.1]{bfk},
with nonnegative fugacity coefficients, implies
$a_{n+1}=Z_n(2)\ge Z_n(1)=a_n$. Alternatively, adjoining an isolated
$+1$ corner gives a direct injection. Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main} implies
$a_n\to\infty$, so $N(T)$ is well defined.

Put
\[
 F(x)=\alpha x^2+\beta x-\kappa\log x,\quad
 d(r)=\frac{\kappa\log r+\beta^2/(4\alpha)}{2\alpha},\quad
 z=r-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}+\frac{d(r)}r.
\]
The polynomial terms cancel exactly:
\[
 \alpha z^2+\beta z
 =y+\kappa\log r+\alpha d(r)^2/r^2.
\]
Since $\log z=\log r-\beta/(2\alpha r)+O(\log r/r^2)$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:inverseF}
 F(z)=y+O(1/r),\qquad
 F'(z+O(1/r))=2\alpha r+O(\log r/r).
\end{equation}
Thus for fixed $C$,
\[
 F(z\pm C/r)=y\pm2\alpha C+o(1).
\]
Choose $M,n_0$ such that $|\log a_n-F(n)|\le M$ for $n\ge n_0$.
Taking $C>M/(2\alpha)+1$ and enlarging the onset gives
\[
 F(z-C/r)+M<y,\qquad F(z+C/r)-M>y.
\]
The function $F$ is eventually strictly increasing. Every integer
$n<z-C/r$ then satisfies $a_n<T$, while $n=\ceil{z+C/r}$ satisfies
$a_n\ge T$. Finitely many $n<n_0$ are excluded by enlarging $T_0$.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:log:inversebracket}.

Expressed in $y$ alone, the center is
\begin{align}\label{dsasm:log:inverseexpanded}
 z(T)={}&\sqrt{y/\alpha}-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}
       +\frac{\kappa}{4\sqrt\alpha}\frac{\log y}{\sqrt y}\notag\\
 &+\left(\frac{\beta^2}{8\alpha^{3/2}}
          -\frac{\kappa\log\alpha}{4\sqrt\alpha}\right)\frac1{\sqrt y}.
\end{align}
The unrounded bracket has width $O(y^{-1/2})$ and eventually yields
at most two adjacent integer candidates. When both ceilings agree,
they determine $N(T)$. Thresholds arbitrarily close to a count are
covered by retaining both ceilings. The displayed fixed $y^{-1/2}$
term is a choice of center: an unknown bounded remainder in
\eqref{dsasm:log:logmain} allows an additional unknown correction of this same
order in a continuous inverse. A limiting amplitude would be needed
to identify the full constant at that order. The constants $C,T_0$
are not effective here, so this is an asymptotic localization theorem,
not a finite numerical certification procedure.
\begin{writenote}[The amplitude at order $y^{-1/2}$, 5~October 2026]
The limiting amplitude that this paragraph says would be needed is supplied by Part~I: the full constant at order $y^{-1/2}$ is that of \eqref{dsasm:inverseexpanded}. The constants remain non-effective.
\end{writenote}


\section{Bounded holomorphic pressure and diagonal cumulants}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:complex}
The preceding real determinant proof admits a useful stronger form.
Boundedness on a real interval alone would not justify differentiating
an error term. We supply the extra complex estimates explicitly.
Define on the slit plane $\Omega_t=\C\setminus(-\infty,0]$
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:pressuref}
 f(t)=2\operatorname{Log}(\sqrt t+1)
          -\operatorname{Log}(\sqrt t+2)-\log2,
\end{equation}
with principal square root; $f(3)=0$. Every determinant logarithm
below is its analytic logarithm normalized to vanish at the calibration,
not an arbitrary pointwise principal logarithm of a determinant.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:log:complextheorem}
Uniformly on every compact $K\Subset\Omega_t$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:complexpressure}
 \log\frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}=nf(t)+O_K(1).
\end{equation}
For each fixed integer $k\ge1$, the $k$th derivatives have errors
$O_K(1)$ locally uniformly. Uniformly on compact subsets of
$\{\Re s>0\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:individualcomplex}
 \log\frac{Z_n(s)}{Z_n(\sqrt3)}=\frac n2 f(s^2)+O_K(1).
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every positive fugacity $s$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:allfugacity}
 \log Z_n(s)=\alpha n^2+\left[b+\tfrac12 f(s^2)\right]n
                  -\kappa\log n+O(1),
\end{equation}
locally uniformly on compact positive intervals. No limit of any
bounded remainder is asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Second route and limit, 5~October 2026]
Part~III, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}, proves the weaker statement $\frac1n\log(D_n(t)/D_n(3))\to f(t)$ on $\Re t>0$ by a different argument (finite Jacobi matrices and a pole-cleared determinant). Part~I proves that the bounded remainder converges: $\log(D_n(t)/D_n(3))-nf(t)=E_n(t)\to E(t)$ locally uniformly on $\C\setminus(-\infty,0]$.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{A scalar compression gap on the complex domain}
For $\delta=t-3\in\Omega$, every self-adjoint $T$ with
$0\le T\le I/3$ has
\[
 \|(I+\delta T)^{-1}\|
 \le\max_{0\le u\le1/3}|1+\delta u|^{-1}.
\]
This bounds all the relevant resolvents uniformly on compact subsets,
independently of $n$. If $K\Subset\Omega$, its segment hull
$\{v\delta:0\le v\le1,\delta\in K\}$ is also a compact subset of
$\Omega$: nonreal points retain the sign of their imaginary part,
and real endpoints and their segments lie to the right of $-3$.
Thus integration from zero along these segments is legitimate.

Put $T=M_\sigma$, $P=J_n$, $Q=I-P$. The kernel and H\"older estimates
used for \eqref{dsasm:log:variancebound} apply to $\sigma$ itself, giving
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:offdiagvariance}
 \|PTQ\|_\HS^2\le C
\end{equation}
uniformly. Define
\[
 g_n(\delta)=\tr\log(I_P+\delta PTP)
                  -\tr[P\operatorname{Log}(I+\delta T)P].
\]
For $\delta\ne0$, let $R=(I+\delta T)^{-1}$,
$R_P=(I_P+\delta PTP)^{-1}$ and $R_Q=(I_Q+\delta QTQ)^{-1}$.
Differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:gapderivative}
 g_n'(\delta)=\delta^{-1}\tr(PRP-R_P).
\end{equation}
The two block equations of $(I+\delta T)R=I$ give, with the order
of all factors retained,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:blockresolvent}
 PRP-R_P=\delta^2 R_PPTQ R_QQTP(PRP).
\end{equation}
Indeed the lower block says $QRP=-\delta R_QQTP(PRP)$; substitution
in the upper block proves \eqref{dsasm:log:blockresolvent}. The two off-diagonal
factors are Hilbert--Schmidt, so their product with the bounded
resolvents is trace class. Equations \eqref{dsasm:log:offdiagvariance}--
\eqref{dsasm:log:blockresolvent} imply
$|g_n'(\delta)|\le C_K|\delta|$ on a compact segment hull.
The value at zero is removable, with $g_n(0)=g_n'(0)=0$.
Integration proves $g_n(\delta)=O_K(1)$.

The global density bound \eqref{dsasm:log:densitybounds} also gives the scalar
mean directly and uniformly. Since
\[
 \operatorname{Log}(1+\delta u)=\int_0^u\frac{\delta}{1+\delta v}\,dv,
 \qquad |\operatorname{Log}(1+\delta u)|\le C_Ku
 \quad(0\le u\le1/3),
\]
the integrable endpoint envelope $w(\theta)/\delta(\theta)$ proves
\[
 \tr[P\operatorname{Log}(I+\delta T)P]
 =\frac n\pi\int_I\operatorname{Log}(1+\delta w(\theta))\,d\theta
                       +O_K(1).
\]
The integral equals $2f(3+\delta)$ on the positive real axis by
\eqref{dsasm:log:Jintegral}; analyticity and the identity theorem extend it
over $\Omega$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:scalcomplex}
 \log\det(I_P+\delta PTP)=2nf(3+\delta)+O_K(1).
\end{equation}

\subsection{The complex mixed Hardy gap}
For $0\le s,u\le1$ put
$R_{su}=[I+\delta(sA_n+uB_n)]^{-1}$. The mixed derivative of the
normalized logarithm is
$-\delta^2\tr(R_{su}A_nR_{su}B_n)$. For complex $\delta$ this
trace is not claimed positive. The exact resolvent factorization is
\begin{align*}
 A_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}
 ={}&(I+\delta sA_n)^{-1}A_n^{1/2}B_n^{1/2}\\
 &\quad\cdot[I-\delta uB_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}].
\end{align*}
The outside factors have compact-domain uniform bounds. Exchanging
$A_n,s$ with $B_n,u$ gives the reverse-order estimate as well.
Hilbert--Schmidt Cauchy--Schwarz yields
\begin{align*}
 |\tr(R_{su}A_nR_{su}B_n)|
 &\le\|B_n^{1/2}R_{su}A_n^{1/2}\|_\HS
        \|A_n^{1/2}R_{su}B_n^{1/2}\|_\HS\\
 &\le C_K\tr(A_nB_n)\le C_K.
\end{align*}
The two factors need not be adjoints. Integration on the unit square,
and equality of the spectra of $A_n,B_n$, give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:mixedcomplex}
 2\log\det(I+\delta A_n)
          -\log\det(I+\delta(A_n+B_n))=O_K(1).
\end{equation}
The trace-class cross-term comparison \eqref{dsasm:log:crossdet} works locally
uniformly on $\Omega$, by the same resolvent bound and trace-norm
convergence; the limiting relative operator is invertible there.
Its normalized logarithm is locally convergent. The earlier DSASM
relative factor has this property by Proposition~\ref{dsasm:log:relativeprop}
and Lemma~\ref{dsasm:log:phaselemma}. Combining \eqref{dsasm:log:scalcomplex} and
\eqref{dsasm:log:mixedcomplex} with these factors proves
\eqref{dsasm:log:complexpressure}. Cauchy's estimate on a slightly larger
compact neighborhood proves every fixed-order derivative assertion.
This is a complex proof of bounded errors, not differentiation of
real-axis boundedness.

\subsection{Interlacing transfers the complex estimate to individual sizes}
Let $r_{n,j}$ be the full real root list of
$q_n(x)=i^{-n}Z_n(ix)$, including zero roots and multiplicities.
Interlacing in \eqref{dsasm:log:weakinterlace} gives
\[
 d_n(r)=\#\{j:r_{n+1,j}\le r\}-\#\{j:r_{n,j}\le r\}\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Set $c=\sqrt3$ and, for $\Re s>0$, use right-half-plane logarithms
in the function
\[
 h_s(r)=\operatorname{Log}(s-ir)-\operatorname{Log}(c-ir).
\]
Monicity gives
$R_n(s):=\log[Z_n(s)/Z_n(c)]=\sum_jh_s(r_{n,j})$.
The function $h_s$ tends to zero at both real infinities, and
\[
 h_s'(r)=-i\left[(s-ir)^{-1}-(c-ir)^{-1}\right].
\]
An explicit uniform integrability bound is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:interlacecomplexbound}
 \int_{\R}|h_s'(r)|\,dr
 \le\frac{\pi|s-c|}{\sqrt{c\,\Re s}},
\end{equation}
by Cauchy--Schwarz and
$\int|s-ir|^{-2}dr=\pi/\Re s$.
Stieltjes integration by parts therefore proves
\[
 R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)=-\int_{\R}d_n(r)h_s'(r)\,dr=O_K(1).
\]
The boundary terms vanish even though $d_n(+\infty)=1$, because
$h_s(+\infty)=0$. This full-root argument includes the parity root
and common roots without an extra correction.

With normalized analytic branches the exact adjacent identity is
\[
 R_n(s)+R_{n+1}(s)
 =\log[D_n(s^2)/D_n(3)]+\operatorname{Log}(s/c).
\]
It holds without a multiple of $2\pi i$ because both sides exponentiate
to the same function and vanish at $s=c$. The map $s\mapsto s^2$
sends the right half-plane into $\Omega_t$. Combining the bounded
adjacent difference with \eqref{dsasm:log:complexpressure} proves
\eqref{dsasm:log:individualcomplex}. Adding \eqref{dsasm:log:W} on the positive real
axis proves \eqref{dsasm:log:allfugacity}.

\subsection{All fixed cumulants and an explicitly centered central limit theorem}
For a fixed $s>0$, give a DSASM $A$ probability $s^{S(A)}/Z_n(s)$,
and denote its nonzero-diagonal count by $S_n$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:meanvariancecoefs}
 m(s)=\frac12-\frac1{(s+1)(s+2)},\qquad
 v(s)=\frac{s(2s+3)}{(s+1)^2(s+2)^2}>0.
\end{equation}
The logarithmic moment generating function, in a small complex disk
about zero, is
\[
 \log\mathbb E_s e^{zS_n}
 =\log Z_n(se^z)-\log Z_n(s)
 =\frac n2[f(s^2e^{2z})-f(s^2)]+O(1)
\]
locally uniformly and analytically. Cauchy's estimates thus give,
for each fixed $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:cumulants}
 \operatorname{cum}_k(S_n)
 =n(s\partial_s)^{k-1}m(s)+O(1).
\end{equation}
Indeed $s^2f'(s^2)=m(s)$ and $sm'(s)=v(s)$, so in particular
\[
 \mathbb E_sS_n=nm(s)+O(1),\qquad
 \operatorname{Var}_sS_n=nv(s)+O(1).
\]
At $s=1$ these coefficients are $m(1)=1/3$ and $v(1)=5/36$.
All constants may depend on the fixed derivative order and fugacity;
no uniform assertion as $s\to0$ or $s\to\infty$ is intended.

The negative-root factorization supplies a short independent CLT
proof. If $P_n(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(t+\lambda_{n,j})$
with $\lambda_{n,j}>0$, then
\[
 \frac{P_n(s^2z)}{P_n(s^2)}
 =\prod_j\left[\frac{\lambda_{n,j}}{s^2+\lambda_{n,j}}
                    +\frac{s^2}{s^2+\lambda_{n,j}}z\right].
\]
Thus $S_n$ has the same law as $\eps_n+2\sum_jB_{n,j}$ for independent
Bernoulli variables with parameters $s^2/(s^2+\lambda_{n,j})$.
This is a representation of the total statistic, not independence
of actual matrix diagonal indicators. The centered summands are
bounded by two and the total variance is $nv(s)+O(1)\to\infty$.
Lindeberg's condition follows immediately, giving the actual-mean
CLT. The bounded mean and variance errors then give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:log:explicitCLT}
 \frac{S_n-nm(s)}{\sqrt{nv(s)}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathcal N(0,1).
\end{equation}
These distributional conclusions do not imply convergence of the
bounded enumeration remainder.
\begin{writenote}[Answered and strengthened, 5~October 2026]
The explicit centring answers Part~III's question~2 (Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}): $M_n(s)-nm(s)=O(1)=o(\sqrt n)$ by \eqref{dsasm:log:cumulants}. Part~I proves the convergence of the bounded enumeration remainder (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}) and the constant terms of the cumulants (Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants}).
\end{writenote}


\section{Further research and reproducibility}
\label{dsasm:log:sec:scope}
The analytic estimates prove boundedness at logarithmic scale. Four
questions remain beyond that conclusion.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{A limiting amplitude.} Put
$E_n=\log[D_n(1)/D_n(3)]-n\log(2/3)$. We have proved $E_n=O(1)$,
not convergence. Some relative Fredholm factors do converge, but the
scalar compression gap and mixed Hardy determinant gap have only
been bounded here. In particular, the model wave kernel contains an
$n$-dependent oscillatory term. Its uniform Hilbert--Schmidt bound
cannot be replaced by a Hilbert--Schmidt convergence claim.
\item \textbf{Parity at constant order.} Even a limit of the adjacent
centered determinant would not by itself prove a common count
amplitude. If
$x_n=\log a_n-\alpha n^2-\beta n+\kappa\log n$, knowledge of the
limit of $x_n+x_{n+1}$ permits opposite even and odd shifts.
A sufficiently precise adjacent-ratio or principal-minor estimate,
such as $x_{n+1}-x_n\to0$, would remove that ambiguity.
\item \textbf{Corrections beyond the bounded term.} A full
parity-sensitive expansion, including any fractional or oscillatory
correction terms predicted by the published conjecture, requires
control of endpoint and finite-cutoff effects beyond the present
ideal-norm bounds. Neither an all-order expansion nor a correction
transseries is established here.
\item \textbf{Effective constants.} The proof leaves the uniform
Jacobi, kernel-ideal, overlap and Fredholm constants uncomputed.
Quantifying them and a valid onset would turn the two-sided bounds
and inverse bracket into finite numerical certificates. The present
asymptotic localization alone is not such a certificate.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{writenote}[Status of the four questions, 5~October 2026]
\emph{Question~1 is answered} by Part~I: $E_n=E_n(1)\to E(1)$ (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}, \eqref{dsasm:Eexplicit}), and the limiting amplitude is $C_*=C_{\rm cal}2^{-1/4}e^{E(1)/2}$; Part~I controls the scalar compression gap by Proposition~\ref{dsasm:prop:scalarconstant} and retains the oscillatory wave term, \eqref{dsasm:movingK}, in the mixed limit \eqref{dsasm:Psidef}. Its numerical value is open (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~1). \emph{Question~2 is answered} by Part~I: the boundary limit \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget} gives $R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)\to\frac12f(s^2)$, \eqref{dsasm:adjacentdifference}, and $x_n\to\log C_*$, so in particular $x_{n+1}-x_n\to0$. \emph{Questions~3 and~4 remain open}; see Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, items~2--4.
\end{writenote}


The companion source package contains this complete TeX source,
a deterministic two-clean-build PDF script, closed file inventories,
and independent finite exact checks. Those checks exercise algebraic
normalizations, retained finite cutoffs, noncommuting finite-matrix
identities and inverse-center cancellation. They are supplementary:
no finite run proves the uniform analytic estimates or substitutes
for the all-size arguments in this report. The package README gives
the exact verification commands, expected scope and environment.
\clearpage
\part{Fugacity pressure and diagonal statistics}\label{dsasm:pr:part}
\parteqs{III.}
\partsource{Bundle Report~121, archive \file{A005163_Linear_Growth_Fugacity_Pressure_and_Limit_Laws_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{report121.tex} (27 pages), dated 2~October 2026. Delivered title block: ``REPORT 121 / DSASM fugacity pressure and diagonal statistics / Finite Jacobi matrices and cross size interlacing / 2 October 2026 / Standalone proofs and exact reproducibility companion''. Printed with \textbf{[write]} notes; Report~121's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+24$ here, its statement $k.j$ is $(k+24).j$, and its equation $(k)$ is $(\mathrm{III}.k)$. Its Sections~2, 4 and~5 repeat Part~II's Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration} and~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration} and are printed there; Sections~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:stability}, \ref{dsasm:pr:sec:calibration} and~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:logcalibration} keep their titles, their equation numbers and a pointer. Its Section~9 repeats Part~II's Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing} except for two identities, which Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing} prints. A reference to an equation of a repeated section points to its printed place in Part~II.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~III & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$M_n(s)$, $V_n^{\rm stat}(s)$ mean and variance of the diagonal count & Part~I: $M_n$ a modulation operator\\
$\beta(s)$ the linear coefficient \eqref{dsasm:pr:freeenergy} & Part~I: $B(s)$\\
$d=3-3\sqrt3/2$; $\ell_n=D_n'(3)/D_n(3)$ & Parts~II, III (logarithmic calibration): $d_{\rm ASM}=5/36$; Part~IV: $d=5/36$\\
$\nu_n$, $\nu$ root measures & Part~I: $\nu_t$ a source density\\
$r(x)$ tilted fugacity; $q(z)$, $r(z)$ polynomials & Parts~I, II: $r=\sqrt{\log T/\alpha}$\\
$n_0=\sqrt{\log T/\alpha}$ & Parts~I, II: $r$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $a_n$ count diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices and let
$Z_n(s)$ weight their number of nonzero diagonal entries by $s$.
We prove
\[
 \log a_n=\frac{n^2}{2}\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4}
                    +\frac n4\log3+o(n),
\]
thereby identifying the conjectured linear exponential coefficient.
The central result is an explicit fugacity pressure, locally uniform
with all derivatives in a complex half-plane. Its proof uses exact
finite Jacobi identities, total positivity, a determinant product with
a controlled Stirling remainder, and a pole-cleared determinant whose
small singular values have a uniformly integrable logarithmic tail.
A multiplicity-safe stable-pencil argument gives cross-size interlacing
and controls parity. We obtain a compactified limiting root measure,
all fixed cumulants, actual-mean central and parity-lattice local
central limit theorems, and an explicit macroscopic large-deviation
rate. For uniform DSASMs, the limiting mean and variance per order
are $1/3$ and $5/36$. The calibration trace limit is recovered as a
derivative of the pressure. The logarithmic correction, amplitude and
full conjectured asymptotic equivalent remain unresolved.
All prerequisites are included from explicitly cited published inputs;
finite exact checks supplement, rather than replace, the all-size proofs.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[Status of Part~III, 5~October 2026]
Every growth statement of this Part is implied by Part~II (with $O(1)$ errors) or Part~I (with limits): the pressure (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}) by Part~II's Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:complextheorem}, the linear coefficient by Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main} and Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}. The proofs here are different (finite Jacobi matrices, a controlled Stirling product, a pole-cleared determinant and a logarithmic singular-value tail, against Part~II's Fredholm and Hardy comparison) and are kept as a \emph{second route}. Found only in this Part: the limiting root measure \eqref{dsasm:pr:rootlaw} with its escaping half, Hoeffding's bound \eqref{dsasm:pr:hoeffding}, the parity-lattice local limit theorem \eqref{dsasm:pr:lclt} and the large-deviation principle \eqref{dsasm:pr:ldp}. The sentence ``The logarithmic correction, amplitude and full conjectured asymptotic equivalent remain unresolved'' is historical: Part~II proves the logarithmic correction and Part~I the amplitude and the full equivalent.
\end{writenote}
\section{Definitions and scope}\label{dsasm:pr:sec:main}

An alternating sign matrix is a square matrix with entries in
$\{-1,0,1\}$ whose nonzero entries alternate in sign in each row and
column and whose row and column sums are one. A diagonally symmetric
alternating sign matrix, or DSASM, satisfies $A=A^{\mathsf T}$.
Write
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:def}
\begin{split}
a_n&=|\DS(n)|,\qquad S(A)=|\{i:A_{ii}\ne0\}|,\\
Q_n(\mathbf s)&=\sum_{A\in\DS(n)}\prod_{i:A_{ii}\ne0}s_i,
\qquad Z_n(s)=Q_n(s,\ldots,s).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Use $Q_0=Z_0=a_0=1$. For $s>0$, the probability of $A$ is
$s^{S(A)}/Z_n(s)$; define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:meanvar}
M_n(s)=s\frac{Z_n'(s)}{Z_n(s)},\qquad
V_n^{\rm stat}(s)=s\frac{d}{ds}M_n(s).
\end{equation}
Thus $M_n(s)$ and $V_n^{\rm stat}(s)$ are the mean and variance of $S$.
Uniform DSASMs correspond to $s=1$, whereas the exactly solvable
calibration in this report is $s=\sqrt3$. These are different measures.
Matrix symbols $V_n$ below are unrelated to the variance notation.

Set
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:constants}
 g=\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4},\quad \alpha=\frac g2,\quad
 b=\frac{g+\log2}{2},\quad \kappa=\frac5{72},\quad
 d=3-\frac{3\sqrt3}{2}.
\end{equation}
The count $a_n$ is OEIS A005163. In the notation of
Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan~\cite{bfk}, $Z_n(s)=X_n(1,s,1)$.
Their Proposition 5.1 gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:adjacentcount} Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}.\end{equation}
Their Theorem 11.2 already establishes the leading quadratic growth
$\log a_n\sim\alpha n^2$. Their Conjecture 11.1 predicts
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:conjecture}
a_n\sim C_{\DS}\,e^{\alpha n^2+(\log3)n/4}n^{-5/72},\qquad C_{\DS}>0,
\end{equation}
with further parity-sensitive corrections. An amplitude and the full
equivalent in \eqref{dsasm:pr:conjecture} are not proved here.
\begin{writenote}[Historical, 5~October 2026]
Part~I proves the full equivalent and the existence of the amplitude, $C_{\DS}=C_*$ (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}); its numerical value and the parity-sensitive corrections remain open (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}).
\end{writenote}


\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:pr:main}
Put $\eps=n\bmod2$. The polynomial $Q_n$ is nonzero when every
$\Re s_i>0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:paritypoly}
Z_n(s)=s^\eps P_n(s^2),\quad
\deg P_n=\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\quad P_n(0)>0.
\end{equation}
All roots of $P_n$ are negative real numbers. The monic real-rooted
polynomials $q_n(x)=i^{-n}Z_n(ix)$ weakly interlace across adjacent
sizes, counting multiplicities. For every $n\ge1$ and $s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:adjmean}
 |M_n(s)-M_{n-1}(s)|\le1.
\end{equation}
For $D_n(t)=Z_n(\sqrt t)Z_{n+1}(\sqrt t)/\sqrt t$ and
$\ell_n=D_n'(3)/D_n(3)$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:maintarget}
\frac{\ell_n}{n}\longrightarrow1-\frac{\sqrt3}{2},\qquad
3\ell_n\le M_n(\sqrt3)\le3\ell_n+1,\qquad
\frac{M_n(\sqrt3)}n\longrightarrow d.
\end{equation}
All limits hold along the full sequence, with no parity subsequence
restriction and no assumed rate of convergence.
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Where this theorem is proved, 5~October 2026]
The stability and parity statements are proved in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability} (Report~121's Section~2, printed once); weak interlacing and \eqref{dsasm:pr:adjmean} in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}, with the bracket of \eqref{dsasm:pr:maintarget} proved in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing}, \eqref{dsasm:pr:meanbracket}. Part~II, \eqref{dsasm:log:firstmain}, strengthens the trace limit to $\ell_n=(n+1)(1-\sqrt3/2)+o(1)$.
\end{writenote}


The stronger central conclusion is the pressure theorem in
Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:pressure}:
\[
 \frac1n\log\frac{D_n(t)}{D_n(3)}
 \longrightarrow\log\frac{(\sqrt t+1)^2}{2(\sqrt t+2)}.
\]
It implies the growth formula \eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth} and the uniform-law
limits $M_n(1)/n\to1/3$ and $V_n^{\rm stat}(1)/n\to5/36$.
Sections~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:rootmeasure}--\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:ldp} state the root law and
probability consequences; Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:inverse} treats integer
threshold inversion. The sharper pressure route also proves the
first calibration trace limit without relying on that limit as an input.


The published inputs are the triangular six-vertex correspondence
and adjacent determinant of~\cite{bfk}, classical stability closure,
and explicit determinant and orthogonal-polynomial evaluations.
The stability operator is the degree-two case of the known MOD
operator~\cite[Section 8.4]{bb2}; the stable-pencil implication is
classical~\cite[Definition 1.1 and Lemma 1.8]{bb1}.
The shifted Jacobi identities are derived explicitly below, inspired
by the unshifted commuting construction of Casper--Zurri\'an~\cite{cz}.
These attributions do not imply a bibliographic priority claim for
any DSASM-specific consequence.

\section{An orientation polynomial and diagonal stability}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:stability}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~II]
Report~121's Section~2 is Part~II's Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability} word for word, with two exceptions: it writes $\eps$ for $\eps_n$, and its proof of the root structure ends ``This proves the structural part of Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:main}. For $n=1$, $P_1=1$ and the root statement is vacuous.'' Its labels are those of Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}, and its equations (III.9)--(III.15) are (II.11)--(II.17) there; Lemma~2.1 of Report~121 is Lemma~\ref{dsasm:log:boundarylemma}.
\end{writenote}
\setcounter{equation}{15}
\section{Two consequences for the diagonal statistic}\label{dsasm:pr:sec:consequences}

The root theorem allows a factorization
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:factorization}
 P_n(t)=c_n\prod_{j=1}^m(t+\gamma_j),\qquad
 m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\quad c_n>0,\quad\gamma_j>0.
\end{equation}
Multiplicity is included in this list. The proofs below do not need
$c_n=1$.

Write $P_n(t)=\sum_{k=0}^m c_{n,k}t^k$. Newton's inequalities give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:newton}
 \left(\frac{c_{n,k}}{\binom mk}\right)^2
 \ge \frac{c_{n,k-1}}{\binom m{k-1}}
      \frac{c_{n,k+1}}{\binom m{k+1}}
 \qquad(1\le k<m).
\end{equation}
In particular, these parity-reduced coefficients are log-concave after
binomial normalization. Strict positivity follows as well from
\eqref{dsasm:pr:factorization}.

For a fixed positive fugacity $s$, give a matrix $A$ probability
$s^{S(A)}/Z_n(s)$. The probability generating function of the
integer-valued total $(S-\eps)/2$ is
\[
 \frac{P_n(s^2z)}{P_n(s^2)}
 =\prod_{j=1}^m
   \left(\frac{\gamma_j}{s^2+\gamma_j}
              +\frac{s^2}{s^2+\gamma_j}z\right).
\]
Consequently this total has the same distribution as a sum of
independent Bernoulli variables with parameters
$s^2/(s^2+\gamma_j)$. Equivalently,
\[
 S\ \overset{\rm law}=\ \eps+2\sum_{j=1}^m B_j.
\]
This is an independent-Bernoulli representation of one total
statistic. It does not assert independence of the actual diagonal
entries or their nonzero indicators in a random matrix.

\section{The exact solvable calibration}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:calibration}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~II]
Report~121's Section~4 is Part~II's Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:calibration} word for word (checked by \texttt{diff}); its equations (III.18)--(III.27) are (II.25)--(II.34) there.
\end{writenote}
\setcounter{equation}{27}
\section{Logarithmic calibration without matching parity amplitudes}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:logcalibration}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~II]
Report~121's Section~5 is Part~II's Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration} word for word; its equations (III.28)--(III.32) are (II.35)--(II.39) there. Part~I, \eqref{dsasm:calibrationequiv}, now matches the parity amplitudes.
\end{writenote}
\setcounter{equation}{32}
\section{The finite calibration trace and its Jacobi matrix}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:jacobi}
All matrices in this section have indices $0,\ldots,n-1$. Put
\[
 V_n(i,j)=\binom{i+j+2}{i},\qquad Q_n^{\rm mat}=(I+V_n)^{-1}.
\]
The notation $Q_n^{\rm mat}$ is distinct from the generating polynomial
$Q_n(\mathbf s)$. Differentiating \eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet} at $t=3$ is legitimate
because $I+V_n$ is invertible. In the finite lower-Toeplitz convention,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Rdef}
 R(z)=\frac{\partial_tG_t(z)|_{t=3}}{G_3(z)}
 =\frac{z^2-z+1}{(2-z)(1+z)}
 =-1+\frac1{2-z}+\frac1{1+z}.
\end{equation}
The derivative matrix is $T_n(R)V_n$. Jacobi's determinant identity
and $V_n(I+V_n)^{-1}=I-(I+V_n)^{-1}$ give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:traceexact}
 \ell_n=\tr\bigl(T_n(R)V_n(I+V_n)^{-1}\bigr)
 =\frac n2-\tr\bigl(T_n(R)Q_n^{\rm mat}\bigr),
\end{equation}
since $R(0)=1/2$. The order of the Toeplitz and binomial factors and
the lower-shift convention in this identity will matter below.

\subsection{Symmetrization and reciprocal pairs}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Cdef}
\begin{split}
 d_i&=\sqrt{(i+1)(i+2)},\qquad D=\diag(d_i),\qquad
 C_n=D^{-1}V_nD,\\
 C_n(i,j)&=\frac{(i+j+2)!}{\sqrt{i!(i+2)!j!(j+2)!}},
 \qquad F_n=(I+C_n)^{-1}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Let $E=\diag((-1)^i)$, and let $L$ be the lower-triangular matrix
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Lfactor}
 L_{ij}=\frac{\sqrt{i!(i+2)!/[j!(j+2)!]}}{(i-j)!}
       =\binom ij\frac{d_i}{d_j}\qquad(i\ge j).
\end{equation}
Then $C_n=LL^{\mathsf T}$. Indeed, clearing the positive factorial factors reduces it to
Vandermonde's identity
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,j)}\binom{i}{k}\binom{j+2}{k+2}
 =\binom{i+j+2}{i+2}.
\]
Finite binomial inversion gives $L^{-1}=ELE$. Consequently
$B=LE$ satisfies $B^2=I$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:reciprocal}
 B C_n B=E L^{\mathsf T}L E=L^{-\mathsf T}L^{-1}=C_n^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The matrix $B$ need not be symmetric. Positive definiteness of $C_n$
and \eqref{dsasm:pr:reciprocal} imply reciprocal pairing of its positive
spectrum, with multiplicities. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:halftrace} \tr F_n=\frac n2.\end{equation}
This includes a possible eigenvalue one, whose contribution is $1/2$.

\subsection{Exact commutation and reflection}
Put $N=n(n+2)$ and define the symmetric tridiagonal matrix $J_n$ by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Jdef}
\begin{split}
 J_n(i,i)&=2i^3+9i^2+(12-N)i,\\
 J_n(k-1,k)&=J_n(k,k-1)=\sqrt{k(k+2)}[N-k(k+2)]
 \quad(1\le k<n).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Every off-diagonal entry is positive. The identities we need are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Jidentities}
 [J_n,C_n]=0,\qquad
 L^{-1}J_nL=-EJ_nE+2\tau_nI,
 \qquad \tau_n=\frac32(n-1)(n+3).
\end{equation}
They hold as finite matrix identities, including the last row.
Here is an algebraic verification that exposes both boundaries.

For a parameter $a>-1$, define explicitly
\[
 C_a(i,j)=\frac{\Gamma(i+j+a+1)}
 {\sqrt{i!\Gamma(i+a+1)j!\Gamma(j+a+1)}},
\]
\[
 L_a(i,j)=\frac{\sqrt{i!\Gamma(i+a+1)/[j!\Gamma(j+a+1)]}}{(i-j)!}
 \quad(i\ge j),
\]
with entries zero above the diagonal. Only $a=2$ is needed for DSASMs.
Define
\[
 f(k)=n(n+a)-k(k+a),\qquad
 b(i)=2i^3+3(a+1)i^2+[(a+1)(a+2)-n(n+a)]i.
\]
Let $J_a$ have diagonal $b(i)$ and off-diagonal
$\sqrt{k(k+a)}f(k)$. Dividing the $(i,j)$ commutator entry by its
positive moment entry reduces it to
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:commutatorpoly}
\begin{split}
 &(i+j+a+1)[f(i+1)-f(j+1)]\\
 &\quad+\frac{i(i+a)f(i)-j(j+a)f(j)}{i+j+a}
       +b(i)-b(j)=0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Substitution of the two polynomials, multiplication by $i+j+a$,
and cancellation prove the identity. Only $a=2$ is needed here,
so this denominator is strictly positive. The missing lower index
has its factor $i(i+a)=0$; the missing upper index has $f(n)=0$.
Thus \eqref{dsasm:pr:commutatorpoly} proves commutation for the finite section.

For the reflection identity set
$c=(a+1)[n(n+a)-a-1]$. Divide the $(i,j)$ entry of
$J_aL_a-L_a(-EJ_aE+cI)$ by $(L_a)_{ij}$ for $i\ge j$, and multiply by
$i-j+1$. The result is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:reflectionpoly}
\begin{split}
 &(i-j+1)\{(i-j)[f(i)-f(j+1)]+b(i)+b(j)-c\}\\
 &\quad +(i+1)(i+a+1)f(i+1)-j(j+a)f(j)=0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Again substitution gives the zero polynomial. For $i=j-1$, both
matrix entries are the same off-diagonal Jacobi entry; farther
above the diagonal both are zero. The boundary terms vanish exactly
as before. At $a=2$, $c=2\tau_n$, proving \eqref{dsasm:pr:Jidentities}.
These direct finite identities are the shifted counterpart of the
unshifted construction in~\cite[Theorems 3.2--3.3 and (4.4)]{cz};
the shifted formula is not attributed verbatim to that paper.

\subsection{Why the spectral orders coincide}
For increasing row indices $i_1<\cdots<i_r$ and column indices
$j_1<\cdots<j_r$, the corresponding unnormalized moment minor is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:andreief}
 \det\left[\int_0^\infty x^{i_p+j_q}x^2e^{-x}\,dx\right]_{p,q=1}^r
 =\int_{0<x_1<\cdots<x_r}
 \det[x_q^{i_p}]\det[x_q^{j_p}]\prod_{q=1}^r x_q^2e^{-x_q}\,d\mathbf x.
\end{equation}
This is the finite Andr\'eief identity, obtained by expanding both
determinants, applying Fubini, and grouping the $r!$ ordering regions.
Both generalized Vandermonde determinants are positive on the ordered
positive region: factoring an ordinary Vandermonde leaves a Schur
polynomial with nonnegative coefficients and a positive value there.
The density is positive and the region has positive measure. Therefore
every minor is strictly positive. Positive row and column scalings
preserve the signs, so $C_n$ is strictly totally positive.

The oscillation theorem for a strictly totally positive matrix gives
positive simple eigenvalues in descending order, with the $k$th
corresponding eigenvector having exactly $k-1$ sign changes; zeros
are deleted when counting changes. A precise modern statement is
\cite[Section 3.3 and Theorem 2]{amg}. For a real symmetric Jacobi
matrix with positive off-diagonals, the Sturm convention is the same:
the $k$th \emph{largest} eigenvalue has an eigenvector with $k-1$
sign changes. To see the orientation, the eigenvector entries, after
choosing its first entry positive, are the leading-principal monic
characteristic polynomials evaluated at the eigenvalue, divided by
successive positive off-diagonal products. The three-term recurrence
is a Sturm chain. Its largest eigenvector has no sign change, and
successive eigenvalues introduce one more; a zero entry is passed
using the recurrence, which forces opposite signs on its neighbors.
The Jacobi spectrum is simple because its first entry determines an
entire eigenvector by that recurrence.

Commutation in \eqref{dsasm:pr:Jidentities} makes the simple $J_n$ eigenvectors
also eigenvectors of $C_n$. The two oscillation counts place them in
the same descending order. Moreover, \eqref{dsasm:pr:reciprocal} and
\eqref{dsasm:pr:Jidentities} give
\[
 C_nB=BC_n^{-1},\qquad J_nB=B(2\tau_nI-J_n).
\]
Thus a simultaneous eigenpair $(\mu,\lambda)$ is paired with
$(2\tau_n-\mu,\lambda^{-1})$. Their ranks are reversed. In odd
size the unique central pair is $(\tau_n,1)$; in even size neither
threshold is an eigenvalue. We have proved the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:projector}
 \Pi_n=\one_{J_n<\tau_n}+\tfrac12\one_{J_n=\tau_n}
      =\one_{C_n<1}+\tfrac12\one_{C_n=1}.
\end{equation}
In this shared basis the $F_n$ eigenvalues decrease as the $J_n$
eigenvalues increase. This ordering is stronger than commutation
alone and is essential when a singular Toeplitz factor is present.

\section{A determinant product controls spectral smoothing}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:smoothing}
Write $(a)_k=a(a+1)\cdots(a+k-1)$, with $(a)_0=1$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:norms}
\begin{split}
 J_m^{\rm prod}&=\frac{m!(2)_m(3)_{3m}(4)_{3m}}{(2)_{2m}(3)_{2m}},\\
 H_{2m}^{\rm prod}&=\frac{2m!(3/2)_m(3/2)_{3m}(4)_{3m}}
                         {(3/2)_{2m}(5/2)_{2m}},\\
 H_{2m+1}^{\rm prod}&=\frac{(21/2)m!(5/2)_m(9/2)_{3m}(4)_{3m}}
                           {(5/2)_{2m}(7/2)_{2m}}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The superscripts distinguish these scalar products from the Jacobi
matrix and its later normalization. The determinant evaluation of Ciucu--Eisenk\"olbl--Krattenthaler--Zare
in \cite[Theorem 4.4]{ros}, specialized at $b=3$, and the plus-case norms
\cite[(3.5b,d) and Theorem 3.1]{ros}, give
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:plusminus}
\begin{split}
 \det(I-V_{2m})&=
 \frac{(-1)^m3^{2m}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(J_j^{\rm prod})^2}
      {\prod_{i=0}^{2m-1}i!(3)_i},\\
 \det(I+V_n)&=
 \frac{\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}H_k^{\rm prod}}{\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}i!(3)_i}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
For odd size the minus determinant is zero. The source minus matrix
has entries $i!(3)_i\delta_{ij}-(3)_{i+j}$. Multiplication on the left
by $\diag(i!)^{-1}$ and on the right by $\diag((3)_i)^{-1}$ gives
$I-V_n$ in exactly our orientation. The plus norms above are the
source norms multiplied by $2\cdot27^k$; the triangular polynomial
basis has diagonal signs, so its squared determinant is one.
This explains both scaling factors in \eqref{dsasm:pr:plusminus}.

Let
\[
 \Lambda_m=\frac{|\det(I-C_{2m})|}{\det(I+C_{2m})},\qquad \Lambda_0=1.
\]
By similarity and \eqref{dsasm:pr:plusminus},
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:lambdaratio}
\begin{split}
\frac{\Lambda_{m+1}}{\Lambda_m}
 &=\frac{9(J_m^{\rm prod})^2}{H_{2m}^{\rm prod}H_{2m+1}^{\rm prod}}\\
 &=\frac37\,
 \frac{(2)_m^2}{(3/2)_m(5/2)_m}
 \frac{(3)_{3m}^2}{(3/2)_{3m}(9/2)_{3m}}\\
 &\hspace{12mm}\cdot
 \frac{(3/2)_{2m}(5/2)_{2m}^2(7/2)_{2m}}
 {(2)_{2m}^2(3)_{2m}^2}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
No differentiation with respect to the discrete determinant parameter
is used.

\subsection{The controlled Stirling step}
For $x>0$, the usual Stirling remainder bound is
\[
 \log\Gamma(x)=(x-\tfrac12)\log x-x+\tfrac12\log(2\pi)
                 +\frac1{12x}+r(x),\qquad |r(x)|\le\frac1{360x^3}.
\]
For any of the finitely many positive $a,c$ below, Taylor expansion
at $x=am+c$ consequently yields, uniformly as $m\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:shiftstirling}
 \log\Gamma(am+c)=(am+c-\tfrac12)\log(am)-am
  +\tfrac12\log(2\pi)+\frac{B_2(c)}{2am}+O(m^{-2}),
\end{equation}
where $B_2(c)=c^2-c+1/6$. The finitely many fixed shifts make the
remainder constant uniform over every term in \eqref{dsasm:pr:lambdaratio}.
The gamma factors in that ratio have signed triples $(a,c,e)$
\[
\begin{gathered}
(1,2,2),\ (1,3/2,-1),\ (1,5/2,-1),\\
(3,3,2),\ (3,3/2,-1),\ (3,9/2,-1),\\
(2,3/2,1),\ (2,5/2,2),\ (2,7/2,1),\ (2,2,-2),\ (2,3,-2).
\end{gathered}
\]
Substituting them in \eqref{dsasm:pr:shiftstirling} cancels the coefficients
of $m\log m$, $m$, $\log m$ and the constant term, including the
fixed gamma normalizations in the rising factorials. The remaining
coefficient is
\[
 \sum_{(a,c,e)}\frac{eB_2(c)}{2a}=-\frac34.
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:lambdaasympt}
 \log\frac{\Lambda_{m+1}}{\Lambda_m}
 =-\frac3{4m}+O(m^{-2}),\qquad
 \Lambda_m=c\,m^{-3/4}(1+O(m^{-1})),\quad c>0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, subtract $-(3/4)\log(1+1/m)$ from the logarithmic increment.
The resulting series has a summable $O(m^{-2})$ tail, so
$\log\Lambda_m+(3/4)\log m$ converges to a finite real constant
with error $O(m^{-1})$. Exponentiation proves the second statement
and positivity of $c$. The stronger optional expansion begins
$-3/(4m)+3/(4m^2)-961/(1152m^3)$, but is not needed.

\subsection{Both parities and the half threshold}
For even $n=2m$, let $\lambda_j>0$ be the $C_n$ eigenvalues. They
avoid one by \eqref{dsasm:pr:plusminus}, and
\[
 \sum_j\log\frac{1+\lambda_j}{|1-\lambda_j|}
 =-\log\Lambda_m=\frac34\log m+O(1).
\]
If $u=\min(\lambda,\lambda^{-1})<1$, then
\[
 h_0(\lambda):=\frac{u}{1+u}
 \le\frac12\log\frac{1+u}{1-u}.
\]
Set $h_0(1)=1/2$. Off the threshold, $h_0(\lambda)$ is precisely the
error between $(1+\lambda)^{-1}$ and $\one_{\lambda<1}$, while at
the half-valued threshold of \eqref{dsasm:pr:projector} that error is zero.
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:smoothing}
 \|F_n-\Pi_n\|_1=O(\log(n+1))\qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
For completeness, the extension to odd $n$ does not use a zero
minus determinant. The entries of $C_n$ do not depend on $n$, so
$C_n$ is a principal section of $C_{n+1}$. Cauchy interlacing implies
that their eigenvalue counts in any interval differ by at most one.
Each superlevel set of the continuous unimodal function $h_0$ is an
interval, and its maximum is $1/2$. The layer-cake formula gives
\[
 |\tr h_0(C_n)-\tr h_0(C_{n+1})|\le\frac12.
\]
An odd size is thereby bounded by the following even size, proving
\eqref{dsasm:pr:smoothing} along both parities.
\section{Jacobi moments and mixed finite path limits}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:moments}
Normalize the Jacobi matrix by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Hprofile}
\begin{split}
 H_n&=(J_n-\tau_nI)/n^3,\\
 a(x)&=x(1-x^2),\quad b(x)=2x^3-x,\quad
 h(x,\theta)=b(x)+2a(x)\cos\theta.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The letter $H_n$ now denotes this matrix, not the orientation polynomial
used in Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:stability}. The coefficients of $H_n$ converge
uniformly to this profile with error $O(1/n)$. For the off-diagonal
coefficient, use
\[
 \frac{\sqrt{k(k+2)}}n\,
 \frac{n(n+2)-k(k+2)}{n^2}
 =a(k/n)+O(n^{-1}),
\]
uniformly in $1\le k<n$; the diagonal follows directly from
\eqref{dsasm:pr:Jdef}. Absolute row sums give a common bound $\|H_n\|\le K$.

\begin{lemma}\label{dsasm:pr:momentlemma}
For every continuous function $f$ on a common compact spectral
