interval,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:momentlimit}
 \frac1n\tr f(H_n)\longrightarrow
 \int_0^1\int_0^{2\pi}f(h(x,\theta))\,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,dx.
\end{equation}
The limiting measure has no atom at zero and assigns positive mass
to each sufficiently small interval immediately on either side of zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed power $r$, a diagonal entry of $H_n^r$ is a sum over
nearest-neighbor paths of length $r$ that start and end at that index.
Away from the $O(r)$ boundary rows, freeze each coefficient at $x=i/n$.
Uniform convergence of coefficients makes the resulting error $o(1)$
uniform over rows. The frozen sum is the constant Laurent coefficient
of $(b(x)+a(x)(z+z^{-1}))^r$, equivalently its circle integral.
All path terms are bounded uniformly in $n$, so the boundary rows
contribute $o(1)$ after division by $n$. Riemann summation proves
\eqref{dsasm:pr:momentlimit} for powers and hence for polynomials. Uniform
polynomial approximation on $[-K,K]$ proves it for continuous $f$.

For each $0<x<1$, $a(x)>0$ and the equation $h(x,\theta)=0$ has at
most two solutions in $[0,2\pi]$. Fubini therefore gives zero mass at
zero. At $x=1/2$, zero is attained at $\cos\theta=1/3$, with nonzero
$\theta$ derivative. A small neighborhood of this point has positive
area on each side of zero and in each sufficiently small adjacent
interval. This proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The following extension will allow products containing the shift as
well as a discontinuous spectral cutoff. Let
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:weightedshift}
 A=D^{-1}S_nD,
 \qquad A_{i,i-1}=w_i=\sqrt{\frac{i}{i+2}}\quad(1\le i<n).
\end{equation}
Then $\|A\|\le1$. For every fixed noncommutative polynomial in
$A,A^*,H_n$, the normalized trace converges to the same phase-space
integral, with scalar substitutions
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:mixedsymbol}
 A\mapsto z=e^{i\theta},\qquad A^*\mapsto\bar z,\qquad
 H_n\mapsto h(x,\theta).
\end{equation}
Indeed, each word has a fixed bandwidth and finitely many contributing
paths. For $i/n\to x>0$, the weights $w_i$ tend to one. Discard the
first $\eta n$ rows and the bounded endpoint rows, apply the uniform
path argument on the remainder, and then let $\eta\downarrow0$.
The uniform norm bounds control the discarded rows. The convention
$z$ or $\bar z$ for the lower shift changes no circle integral here.

Put $U=I-F_n$ and $P_+=\one_{H_n>0}$. By \eqref{dsasm:pr:smoothing},
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:HSsmooth}
 \|U-P_+\|_{\rm HS}=o(\sqrt n).
\end{equation}
The possible central rank-one half value contributes only $O(1)$
to the squared Hilbert--Schmidt norm. To replace $P_+$ by polynomials
in $H_n$, choose a continuous cutoff that agrees with the step off
$[-\eta,\eta]$ and lies in $[0,1]$, then approximate it uniformly by
a polynomial. Equation~\eqref{dsasm:pr:momentlimit} and the absence of a zero
atom make the normalized squared Hilbert--Schmidt error arbitrarily
small. Norms of all approximants can be bounded, for example by two.
In a fixed mixed word, telescope one changed factor at a time and use
$|\tr XY|\le\|X\|_{\rm HS}\|Y\|_{\rm HS}$ together with the bounded
norms of the remaining factors. Consequently the mixed-trace limit
\eqref{dsasm:pr:mixedsymbol} remains valid with
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Usymbol}
 U\mapsto\one_{h(x,\theta)>0}.
\end{equation}
This is a trace statement about finite matrices; it neither assumes
that $A$ commutes with $H_n$ nor asserts operator-norm convergence to
a discontinuous function.

\section{Cross size interlacing and parity control}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:interlacing}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~II, except the identities below]
Report~121's Section~9 is Part~II's Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}, with these differences only: the root formula \eqref{dsasm:log:rootmean} is written $M_n(s)=\sum_{j=1}^n s^2/(s^2+r_{n,j}^2)$, since $M_n$ was defined in \eqref{dsasm:pr:meanvar}; the following two identities and their paragraph are inserted after the proof of the adjacent mean bound; and the last sentence differs. Its equations (III.57)--(III.62) and (III.65) are (II.18)--(II.23) and (II.24) there. The inserted text and the last sentence are printed here as delivered.
\end{writenote}
\setcounter{equation}{62}
The determinant definition gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:adjderivative}
 2t\frac{D_n'(t)}{D_n(t)}=M_n(\sqrt t)+M_{n+1}(\sqrt t)-1.
\end{equation}
At $t=3$, write $\delta_n=M_{n+1}(\sqrt3)-M_n(\sqrt3)\in[-1,1]$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:meanbracket}
 M_n(\sqrt3)=3\ell_n+\frac{1-\delta_n}{2}
 \in[3\ell_n,3\ell_n+1].
\end{equation}
Thus a trace limit transfers to the individual mean with a bounded
error; an alternating sum of uncontrolled $o(n)$ errors is never used.
More generally, integrating the adjacent bound against $ds/s$ gives
\eqref{dsasm:log:ratioadjacent}.
\setcounter{equation}{65}
This version will control parity for the individual free energy.

\section{The fugacity pressure}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:pressure}
The pole in $R$ at $-1$ prevents a direct bounded-symbol treatment of
\eqref{dsasm:pr:traceexact}. A finite multiplication clears that pole before
any asymptotic passage. We first prove the pressure on $3<t<4$, where
an accretivity estimate controls the entire lower singular-value tail,
and then use negative real roots to continue the result analytically.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}
For $\Re t>0$, define $p_n(t)$ as the analytic logarithm satisfying
$p_n(3)=0$ of $D_n(t)/D_n(3)$, divided by $n$. Then, locally uniformly
with every complex derivative,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:pressurelimit}
 p_n(t)\longrightarrow
 f(t)=\log\frac{(\sqrt t+1)^2}{2(\sqrt t+2)}.
\end{equation}
The square root and logarithms use their branches real on the positive
axis. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:pressurevalues}
\begin{gathered}
 f(1)=\log(2/3),\quad f(3)=0,\quad f(4)=\log(9/8),\\
 f'(t)=\frac{\sqrt t+3}{2\sqrt t(\sqrt t+1)(\sqrt t+2)},\\
 f'(3)=1-\frac{\sqrt3}{2},\qquad f''(3)=\frac14-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Second route, 5~October 2026]
Part~II, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:complextheorem}, proves $\log(D_n(t)/D_n(3))=nf(t)+O_K(1)$ on the larger domain $\C\setminus(-\infty,0]$, and Part~I proves that the remainder converges. The proof below is a different, independent route to the $o(n)$ statement and is kept as such.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{A bounded finite determinant after clearing the pole}
Keep the weighted shift $A$ from \eqref{dsasm:pr:weightedshift}, and put
\[
 q(z)=2+z-z^2=(2-z)(1+z),\qquad
 r(z)=1-z+z^2=3-q(z),\qquad U=I-F_n.
\]
Here the polynomial $q$ has no size subscript and is unrelated to the
interlacing polynomials $q_n$. Since $G_t$ is affine in $t$,
\eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet} gives the exact finite identity, for $\delta=t-3$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:poleclear}
\begin{split}
 \frac{D_n(3+\delta)}{D_n(3)}
 &=\det[I+\delta R(A)U]
 =\frac{\det\mathcal M_n(\delta)}{2^n},\\
 \mathcal M_n(\delta)&=q(A)+\delta r(A)U.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Indeed, after the diagonal similarity the base matrix is $I+C_n$ and
the perturbation is $\delta R(A)C_n$. Right multiplication by
$(I+C_n)^{-1}$ proves the first equality. Left multiplication by
$q(A)$ proves the second, since $\det q(A)=q(0)^n=2^n$.
The inverse $q(A)^{-1}$ is used only in this exact algebra; it is not
estimated. Both $A$ and $U$ are contractions, so
$\mathcal M_n(\delta)$ has uniformly bounded norm for bounded $\delta$.

\subsection{Accretivity and the logarithmic lower tail}
Fix $0<\delta<1$ and set
\[
 B_\delta=I-\delta U,\qquad
 N_\delta=q(A)+3\delta U B_\delta^{-1}.
\]
The matrix $U$ is symmetric with eigenvalues in $(0,1)$, so
$(1-\delta)I\le B_\delta\le I$ and $UB_\delta^{-1}\ge0$.
A direct multiplication, in the displayed order, gives
$\mathcal M_n=N_\delta B_\delta$. For
$\Re T=(T+T^*)/2$, the contraction property of $A$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:accretive}
 \Re q(A)=2I+\Re A-\Re(A^2)\ge I+\Re A=:B,
 \qquad \Re N_\delta\ge B.
\end{equation}
The diagonal sign matrix $E$ changes $B$ to $I-\Re A$, with diagonal
one and off-diagonals $-w_i/2$. Since $1/\sqrt3\le w_i<1$, let
$w_*=1/\sqrt3$ and let $B_0$ have diagonal one and off-diagonals
$-1/2$. The difference
$EBE-w_*B_0$ has diagonal $1-w_*$ and off-diagonals
$-(w_i-w_*)/2$. Its absolute off-diagonal row sum is at most
$1-w_*$, so it is positive semidefinite, as follows from
$2|xy|\le|x|^2+|y|^2$. Hence $EBE\ge w_*B_0$.

List eigenvalues and singular values in increasing order. The
Dirichlet eigenvectors of $B_0$ are discrete sines, giving
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:eigenlower}
 \lambda_k(B)\ge w_*\left(1-\cos\frac{k\pi}{n+1}\right)
 \ge2w_*\left(\frac{k}{n+1}\right)^2\quad(1\le k\le n).
\end{equation}
The last bound uses $\sin y\ge2y/\pi$ on $0\le y\le\pi/2$.
For any matrix $N$ whose Hermitian part is positive, singular-value
minmax gives $s_k(N)\ge\lambda_k(\Re N)$. Namely, on the span of
the largest $n-k+1$ eigenvectors of $\Re N$,
\[
 \|Nv\|\,\|v\|\ge\Re\langle v,Nv\rangle
 \ge\lambda_k(\Re N)\|v\|^2,
\]
and the max--min characterization proves the claim.
Also $s_k(NB_\delta)\ge(1-\delta)s_k(N)$, by the minmax
characterization and the lower norm bound for $B_\delta$.
Combining these facts gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:singularlower}
 s_k(\mathcal M_n(\delta))\ge
 c_\delta\left(\frac{k}{n+1}\right)^2,
 \qquad c_\delta=\frac{2(1-\delta)}{\sqrt3}>0.
\end{equation}
This proves invertibility but provides much more than a bound for
only the smallest singular value. If
$N_n(\eta)=\#\{k:s_k(\mathcal M_n)<\eta\}$, then
$N_n(\eta)\le2n\sqrt{\eta/c_\delta}$, and Tonelli's theorem yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:logtail}
 \frac1n\sum_{s_k<\eta}\log\frac\eta{s_k}
 =\int_0^\eta\frac{N_n(u)}n\frac{du}{u}
 \le4\sqrt{\frac\eta{c_\delta}}.
\end{equation}
Thus the negative logarithmic tail is uniformly integrable. A weak
singular-value limit alone, even together with a polynomial lower
bound for the very smallest value, would not justify the logarithmic
determinant limit without this integrated estimate.

\subsection{From mixed moments to the logarithmic determinant}
The mixed trace limits \eqref{dsasm:pr:mixedsymbol}--\eqref{dsasm:pr:Usymbol} imply,
for every nonnegative integer $k$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:singularmoments}
 \frac1n\tr[(\mathcal M_n^*\mathcal M_n)^k]
 \longrightarrow\int_0^1\int_0^{2\pi}
 |m_\delta(x,\theta)|^{2k}\,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,dx,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 m_\delta(x,\theta)=q(z)+\delta r(z)\one_{h(x,\theta)>0},
 \qquad z=e^{i\theta}.
\]
Uniform norm bounds and polynomial approximation show weak convergence
of the empirical singular-value measures to the distribution of
$|m_\delta|$. To pass to $\log$, first use the continuous bounded
function $\log\max(\eta,x)$ on the common compact support.
Equation~\eqref{dsasm:pr:logtail} bounds the difference from $\log x$ uniformly
in $n$ by $4\sqrt{\eta/c_\delta}$. The limiting measure has the
same bound, by bounded truncations and monotone convergence. Letting
$\eta\downarrow0$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:logdetlimit}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log|\det\mathcal M_n(\delta)|
 =\int_0^1\int_0^{2\pi}\log|m_\delta(x,\theta)|
                      \,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,dx.
\end{equation}
For real $t>0$, $D_n(t)>0$ by its negative-root factorization; thus
\eqref{dsasm:pr:poleclear} has no phase ambiguity. The elementary circle mean
of $\log|q(e^{i\theta})|$ is $\log2$: factor $q=(2-z)(1+z)$ and
apply the power-series mean to the first factor and its boundary
limit to the second. The logarithm at $z=-1$ is integrable.
Equations~\eqref{dsasm:pr:poleclear} and \eqref{dsasm:pr:logdetlimit} now give, for
$3<t<4$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:pressureintegral}
 \lim p_n(t)=\int_0^1\int_0^{2\pi}
 \one_{h>0}\log\left|\frac{q(z)+(t-3)r(z)}{q(z)}\right|
 \,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,dx.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Exact evaluation of the phase space integral}
For $0\le\theta\le\pi$, the measure of $\{x\in[0,1]:h(x,\theta)>0\}$
is
\[
 Y(\theta)=
 \begin{cases}1,&0\le\theta\le\pi/3,\\
 1-X(\theta),&\pi/3<\theta\le\pi,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad X(\theta)=\sqrt{\frac{1-2\cos\theta}{2(1-\cos\theta)}}.
\]
This follows by factoring
$h=x[(2\cos\theta-1)+2(1-\cos\theta)x^2]$.
The substitution
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:phisub}
 \sin^2(\theta/2)=\frac{1+3\sin^2\phi}{4},
 \quad 0\le\phi\le\pi/2,
 \qquad
 X(\theta)\,d\theta=\frac{6\sin^2\phi}{1+3\sin^2\phi}\,d\phi
\end{equation}
shows that $\pi^{-1}\int_{\pi/3}^{\pi}X(\theta)\,d\theta=1/2$;
hence the mean of $Y$ is $1/2$.

For $3<t<4$, let $a>1$ be determined by
$a(a+1)=(t-1)/(4-t)$ and put $A_0=a^2+a+1=3/(4-t)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:scalarfactor}
 q(z)+(t-3)r(z)=(4-t)(a+1-z)(a+z).
\end{equation}
Both roots are strictly outside the unit circle. We may therefore
differentiate \eqref{dsasm:pr:pressureintegral} on any compact subinterval of
$(3,4)$. Here are the integral details, including the branch sign.
For real $c>1$ or $c<-1$, define
\[
 L(c)=\frac1\pi\int_{\pi/3}^{\pi}
 X(\theta)\Re\frac1{c-e^{i\theta}}\,d\theta.
\]
With $w=\sin^2\phi$, the integrand after \eqref{dsasm:pr:phisub} is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Ldecomposition}
\begin{split}
 &\frac{6w}{1+3w}\,
 \frac{c-1/2+3w/2}{c^2-c+1+3cw}\\
 &\qquad=-\frac2{(c-1)(3w+1)}+\frac1c
 +\frac{(c+1)(c^2-c+1)}{c(c-1)(c^2-c+1+3cw)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The elementary integral
$\int_0^{\pi/2}(A+B\sin^2\phi)^{-1}d\phi
 =\pi/[2\sqrt{A(A+B)}]$ for $A,A+B>0$ follows by $y=\tan\phi$.
Using it in \eqref{dsasm:pr:Ldecomposition} gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:Lvalue}
 L(c)=\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(c+1)\sqrt{c^2-c+1}-1}{2c(c-1)}.
\end{equation}
The full-circle mean of $\Re(c-z)^{-1}$ is $1/c$ for $|c|>1$.
Writing $F(t)$ for the right side of \eqref{dsasm:pr:pressureintegral},
\eqref{dsasm:pr:scalarfactor} and \eqref{dsasm:pr:Lvalue} yield
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:dFda}
 \frac{dF}{da}=
 \frac{2a+1-\sqrt{A_0}}{a(a+1)}-\frac{2a+1}{2A_0}.
\end{equation}
The last term is the derivative of $(1/2)\log(4-t)$.
Since $dt/da=3(2a+1)/A_0^2$ and
$\sqrt{A_0}/\sqrt{4A_0-3}=1/\sqrt t$, simplification gives
\[
 F'(t)=\frac{\sqrt t+3}{2\sqrt t(\sqrt t+1)(\sqrt t+2)}.
\]
Finally $F(t)\to0$ as $t\downarrow3$, with a quantitative
$L^1$ estimate at the approaching unit-circle root. Put $\eta=a-1$
and $u=\pi-\theta$. Then
\[
 \left|\frac{a+e^{i\theta}}{1+e^{i\theta}}\right|^2
 =a+\frac{\eta^2}{4\sin^2(u/2)}.
\]
Using $\sin(u/2)\ge u/\pi$ bounds the integral of its nonnegative
logarithm by $O(\log a)$ plus a constant multiple of
$\int_0^\pi\log(1+C\eta^2/u^2)\,du=O(\eta)$, as seen by $u=\eta v$
and integrability on $(0,\infty)$. The other root factor stays bounded
away from zero and converges uniformly. Since $Y$ is bounded, the
pressure integral tends to zero. This fixes the integration constant
without differentiation at the boundary.
Integrating $F'$ with this initial value gives
\[
 F(t)=2\log\frac{\sqrt t+1}{\sqrt3+1}
       -\log\frac{\sqrt t+2}{\sqrt3+2}
     =\log\frac{(\sqrt t+1)^2}{2(\sqrt t+2)}.
\]
Differentiation directly at $t=3$ under the original integral would
miss a boundary contribution from the unit-circle root. The argument
has differentiated only for $t>3$ and will extend the answer analytically.

\subsection{Analytic continuation from negative real roots}
Parity gives $D_n=P_nP_{n+1}$; its degree is exactly $n$, all of its
roots are strictly negative, and it is monic. Write those roots as
$-\lambda_{n,j}$, $\lambda_{n,j}>0$, with multiplicity. On $\Re t>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:normalfamily}
 p_n(t)=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
  [\operatorname{Log}(t+\lambda_{n,j})-\log(3+\lambda_{n,j})],
 \qquad |p_n'(t)|\le\frac1{\Re t}.
\end{equation}
Since $p_n(3)=0$, integrate this bound on the straight segment from
$3$ to $t$. It gives uniform boundedness on every compact subset
of the right half-plane. The family is normal. Every subsequential
analytic limit agrees on $(3,4)$ with the function just evaluated,
so the identity theorem identifies all subsequential limits. Thus
convergence is locally uniform throughout the half-plane; Cauchy's
integral formula then gives convergence of every derivative.
For explicit branch control, if $s=\sqrt t$ then $\Re s>0$ and
\[
 \frac{(s+1)^2}{2(s+2)}=\frac12\left(s+\frac1{s+2}\right)
\]
has positive real part. Its principal logarithm is analytic, agrees
with the real formula and vanishes at $t=3$.
This proves Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}.

In particular, the first derivative at $3$ proves the trace limit
in \eqref{dsasm:pr:maintarget}. Combining it with \eqref{dsasm:pr:meanbracket} proves
the calibrated individual mean limit. This proof has handled the
singular Toeplitz factor through the pole-cleared determinant and its
integrated logarithmic tail. It does not use an unjustified
bounded-symbol estimate on $R(A)$, and it does not assume the trace
limit as an input. Below $t=3$, a naive absolute-value continuation
of \eqref{dsasm:pr:pressureintegral} is not justified by this argument; the
analytic continuation in \eqref{dsasm:pr:normalfamily} is the one being used.
\section{Individual free energy and the conjectured linear coefficient}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:freeenergy}
Let $c=\sqrt3$ and $R_n(s)=\log[Z_n(s)/Z_n(c)]$.
Equation~\eqref{dsasm:log:ratioadjacent} bounds
$|R_{n+1}(s)-R_n(s)|$ by the fixed constant $|\log(s/c)|$.
The adjacent determinant definition also gives exactly
\[
 R_n(s)+R_{n+1}(s)=\log\frac{D_n(s^2)}{D_n(3)}+\log(s/c).
\]
Together with Theorem~\ref{dsasm:pr:pressuretheorem}, these identities yield
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:individualpressure}
 \frac1n\log\frac{Z_n(s)}{Z_n(\sqrt3)}\longrightarrow\frac12f(s^2)
 \qquad(s>0).
\end{equation}
This is an individual-size result on both parities, with a bounded
adjacent correction before taking the limit.

Combining \eqref{dsasm:pr:individualpressure} with the bounded calibration
expansion \eqref{dsasm:log:W} proves, for every fixed $s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:freeenergy}
 \log Z_n(s)=\alpha n^2+\beta(s)n+o(n),\qquad
 \beta(s)=b+\log(s+1)-\tfrac12\log(2(s+2)).
\end{equation}
At $s=1$, $b=(3/4)\log3-(1/2)\log2$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \log a_n=\frac{n^2}{2}\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4}
                 +\frac n4\log3+o(n).}
\end{equation}
Thus the conjectured linear exponential coefficient is determined.
The $o(n)$ error may dominate $\log n$; neither a logarithmic
coefficient, a power of $n$, nor an amplitude can be inferred from
\eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth}. In particular, inserting $-\kappa\log n$ into
its remainder would be a valid but uninformative rewriting, not a
proof of the conjectured factor $n^{-5/72}$. The older fixed-slope
upper and lower envelopes with bounded remainders remain compatible
with this sharper slope, but their remainder strength does not
transfer to the coincident limiting slope.
\begin{writenote}[Superseded, 5~October 2026]
Part~II proves the logarithmic term, $\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1)$ (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main}), and Part~I the full equivalent (Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}). ``The older fixed-slope upper and lower envelopes'' are Part~IV's \eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes}.
\end{writenote}


For use in differentiation, the convergence in
\eqref{dsasm:pr:individualpressure} is analytic at the level of $P_n$.
Specifically,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:individualanalytic}
 \frac1n\log\frac{P_n(t)}{P_n(3)}\longrightarrow\frac12f(t)
 \quad(\Re t>0)
\end{equation}
locally uniformly with every derivative. The proof repeats
\eqref{dsasm:pr:normalfamily}: there are $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ negative roots,
so the normalized derivative is bounded by $1/(2\Re t)$, and the
real-axis limit is \eqref{dsasm:pr:individualpressure} after removing the
$O(1/n)$ parity term. Normality, the identity theorem and Cauchy's
formula complete the argument.

There is also a direct half-plane statement in $s$. All $n$ roots of
$Z_n$ lie on the imaginary axis, including the parity zero. The
analytic branch $n^{-1}\operatorname{Log}[Z_n(s)/Z_n(\sqrt3)]$ has
derivative bounded by $1/\Re s$. The same normal-family argument
therefore extends \eqref{dsasm:pr:individualpressure}, with every derivative,
to all $\Re s>0$, with limit
\[
 \operatorname{Log}(s+1)-\tfrac12\operatorname{Log}(s+2)-\tfrac12\log2.
\]
This avoids restricting the complex fugacity to the smaller sector
where $\Re(s^2)>0$.


\section{The limiting root measure}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:rootmeasure}
Regard $[0,\infty]$ as a compact interval by adding the point at
infinity. Let $\nu_n$ place mass $1/n$ at each positive magnitude
$\lambda_{n,j}$ of a root of $D_n$, with multiplicity.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:rootlaw}
 \nu_n\ \Longrightarrow\
 \nu=\frac12\delta_\infty+
 \frac{3}{\pi\sqrt\lambda(\lambda+1)(\lambda+4)}\,d\lambda,
 \qquad\lambda>0.
\end{equation}
The same limit holds for the magnitudes of roots of $P_n$, normalized
by their degree $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$.

Here is a proof that accounts for the escaping mass. By compactness,
any subsequence has a further weak limit. The kernel
$(t+\lambda)^{-1}$, defined to be zero at infinity, is continuous on
this compactification for $t>0$. The derivative convergence in
\eqref{dsasm:pr:pressurelimit} therefore identifies its transform as $f'(t)$.
To verify the candidate measure directly, substitute $\lambda=y^2$:
its finite part becomes
\[
 \frac6{\pi(y^2+1)(y^2+4)}\,dy
 =\frac2\pi\left(\frac1{y^2+1}-\frac1{y^2+4}\right)dy.
\]
Its total mass is $1/2$. With $s=\sqrt t$, the elementary integral
$\int_0^\infty[(y^2+a^2)(y^2+b^2)]^{-1}dy
 =\pi/[2ab(a+b)]$ gives its Stieltjes transform
\[
 \frac1{s(s+1)}-\frac1{2s(s+2)}
 =\frac{s+3}{2s(s+1)(s+2)}=f'(t).
\]
The remaining mass $1/2$ is placed at infinity.

For uniqueness, map $\lambda$ to $u=(3+\lambda)^{-1}\in[0,1/3]$,
with $u=0$ at infinity. Derivatives of the Stieltjes transform at
$t=3$ determine all positive moments of $u$, and total mass determines
the zeroth moment. Uniform polynomial approximation identifies the
measure. Every subsequential limit is therefore \eqref{dsasm:pr:rootlaw}.
For $P_n$, divide the derivative limit in
\eqref{dsasm:pr:individualanalytic} by $\lfloor n/2\rfloor/n\to1/2$ to
obtain the same transform and repeat the argument.

The atom at infinity is essential. Every finite polynomial has finite
roots, but half of their normalized mass escapes every bounded set.
It is not correct to treat the displayed finite density, whose mass
is $1/2$, as a probability density of total mass one. Nor is it valid
to interchange the large-size limit with $s\to\infty$ without further
estimates.

\section{Means cumulants and Gaussian limits}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:statistics}
For fixed $s>0$, differentiation of
\eqref{dsasm:pr:individualanalytic}, including the parity factor in
$Z_n(s)=s^\eps P_n(s^2)$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:mvlimits}
\begin{split}
 \frac{M_n(s)}n&\longrightarrow m(s)
   =\frac12-\frac1{(s+1)(s+2)},\\
 \frac{V_n^{\rm stat}(s)}n&\longrightarrow v(s)
   =\frac{s(2s+3)}{(s+1)^2(s+2)^2}>0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Indeed the first limit is $t f'(t)$ at $t=s^2$, and the second is
$2t f'(t)+2t^2f''(t)=s\,d m(s)/ds$.
More generally, if $\operatorname{cum}_k(S)$ is the $k$th cumulant,
then for every fixed $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:cumulants}
 \frac{\operatorname{cum}_k(S)}n\longrightarrow
 (s\partial_s)^{k-1}m(s).
\end{equation}
These limits are uniform when $s$ ranges over a fixed compact subset
of $(0,\infty)$, by local analytic convergence. This concerns any
fixed derivative order, not derivative orders increasing with $n$.

For uniform DSASMs, the first four cumulant limits are
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:uniformstats}
 \frac13,\qquad\frac5{36},\qquad-\frac1{27},\qquad-\frac{19}{216}.
\end{equation}
At the calibration,
\[
 m(\sqrt3)=3-\frac{3\sqrt3}{2},\qquad
 v(\sqrt3)=\frac{21}{2}-6\sqrt3.
\]
In particular $S/n\to m(s)$ in probability, since its variance is
$O(n)$. These formulas describe the nonzero diagonal count, not the
signed diagonal sum.

\subsection{Concentration and the central limit theorem}
The independent-Bernoulli representation already proved above gives,
with $m_n=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$,
\[
 S\overset{\rm law}=\eps+2\sum_{j=1}^{m_n}B_{n,j},\qquad
 p_{n,j}=\mathbb P(B_{n,j}=1)=\frac{s^2}{s^2+\gamma_{n,j}}.
\]
It represents the distribution of the total; it does not assert
independence of the actual diagonal indicators in a DSASM.
The standard exponential bound for a variable in an interval of length
one follows by convexity of its exponential and gives Hoeffding's
inequality for their sum. After scaling by two,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:hoeffding}
 \mathbb P_s(|S-M_n(s)|\ge x)
 \le2\exp[-x^2/(2m_n)]\qquad(n\ge2).
\end{equation}
For any fixed $\eta>0$, \eqref{dsasm:pr:mvlimits} implies exponentially high
probability of $|S/n-m(s)|<\eta$. Precisely,
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P_s(|S/n-m(s)|\ge\eta)
 \le-\eta^2.
\]

Let $\mu_n=M_n(s)$, $\sigma_n^2=V_n^{\rm stat}(s)$ and
$T_n=(S-\eps)/2$, with $v_n=\operatorname{Var}(T_n)=\sigma_n^2/4$.
Since $v_n\asymp n$, the elementary characteristic-function expansion
for a centered Bernoulli variable $X=B-p$,
\[
 \log\mathbb E e^{itX}=-\tfrac12p(1-p)t^2
                       +O(p(1-p)|t|^3),
\]
is uniform in $p\in[0,1]$ for small real $t$. Its uniform remainder
follows by Taylor's theorem and
$\mathbb E|X|^3\le p(1-p)$, followed by the expansion of the logarithm.
Summing the independent terms at $t=z/\sqrt{v_n}$ leaves error
$O(|z|^3/\sqrt{v_n})$ for fixed $z$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:clt}
 \frac{S-\mu_n}{\sigma_n}\Longrightarrow N(0,1).
\end{equation}
The variance scale can be replaced by $\sqrt{nv(s)}$ using
\eqref{dsasm:pr:mvlimits}. The center cannot yet be replaced by $nm(s)$:
$\mu_n=nm(s)+o(n)$ does not imply the $o(\sqrt n)$ error required
for that substitution.
\begin{writenote}[Now possible, 5~October 2026]
Part~II proves $\mu_n=M_n(s)=nm(s)+O(1)$, by \eqref{dsasm:log:cumulants}, and hence the explicitly centred CLT \eqref{dsasm:log:explicitCLT}; Part~I gives $M_n(s)-nm(s)$ a limit (Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants}). The local theorem \eqref{dsasm:pr:lclt} below may therefore also be centred at $nm(s)$: since $\mu_n-nm(s)=O(1)$ and $\sigma_n\asymp\sqrt n$, the argument of $\varphi$ changes by $O(1/\sigma_n)$ uniformly in $k$, and $\varphi$ is Lipschitz.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{The parity lattice local central limit theorem}
For $|t|\le\pi$, independence gives
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:charbound}
\begin{split}
 |\mathbb E e^{it(T_n-\mathbb ET_n)}|
 &=\prod_j\sqrt{1-4p_{n,j}(1-p_{n,j})\sin^2(t/2)}\\
 &\le e^{-2v_n\sin^2(t/2)}\le e^{-2v_nt^2/\pi^2}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
After rescaling $t=z/\sqrt{v_n}$ and extending the rescaled function
by zero outside $[-\pi\sqrt{v_n},\pi\sqrt{v_n}]$, the right side is
an integrable Gaussian majorant independent of $n$. Pointwise
convergence from the preceding proof and dominated convergence give
convergence in $L^1(\R)$ to $e^{-z^2/2}$. Fourier inversion now yields,
uniformly over all integers $j$,
\[
 \sqrt{v_n}\,\mathbb P_s(T_n=j)
 -\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}
       e^{-(j-\mathbb ET_n)^2/(2v_n)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Writing $\varphi(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/2}$, this is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:lclt}
 \sup_{k\equiv n\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2)}
 \left|\frac{\sigma_n}{2}\mathbb P_s(S=k)
       -\varphi\left(\frac{k-\mu_n}{\sigma_n}\right)\right|
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The parity restriction and the factor $\sigma_n/2$ are necessary.
Values off this lattice have probability zero. This local theorem
still uses the actual mean and standard deviation; it does not supply
an unproved explicit centering correction or a uniform formula in
fugacities tending to zero or infinity.

\section{Macroscopic large deviations}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:ldp}
The individual pressure determines a full speed-$n$ large-deviation
principle for $X_n=S/n$ under each fixed fugacity $s>0$. Its limiting
logarithmic moment generating function is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:scgf}
 \Lambda_s(\lambda)
 =\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb E_s e^{\lambda S}
 =\log\frac{se^\lambda+1}{s+1}
      -\frac12\log\frac{se^\lambda+2}{s+2}\qquad(\lambda\in\R).
\end{equation}
It is smooth and strictly convex, with derivative $m(se^\lambda)$
and derivative range $(0,1/2)$.
For $0<x<1/2$, put
\[
 r(x)=\frac{\sqrt{1+8/(1-2x)}-3}{2}.
\]
Then the good rate function is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:rate}
 I_s(x)=x\log\frac{r(x)}s
       -\log\frac{r(x)+1}{s+1}
       +\frac12\log\frac{r(x)+2}{s+2},
 \qquad 0<x<\frac12,
\end{equation}
with continuous endpoint values
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:rateends}
\begin{split}
 I_s(0)&=\log(s+1)-\tfrac12\log(s+2)+\tfrac12\log2,\\
 I_s(1/2)&=\log(s+1)-\tfrac12\log(s+2)-\tfrac12\log s,
\end{split}
\end{equation}
and $I_s(x)=+\infty$ outside $[0,1/2]$. Precisely, for every open
set $G\subset\R$ and closed set $F\subset\R$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:ldp}
\begin{split}
 \liminf_n\frac1n\log\mathbb P_s(X_n\in G)&\ge-\inf_{x\in G}I_s(x),\\
 \limsup_n\frac1n\log\mathbb P_s(X_n\in F)&\le-\inf_{x\in F}I_s(x).
\end{split}
\end{equation}

Here is a direct proof. The expression in \eqref{dsasm:pr:rate} is the Legendre
transform $\sup_\lambda(\lambda x-\Lambda_s(\lambda))$: solving
$m(r)=x$ gives the displayed $r(x)$ and optimizer $\lambda=\log(r/s)$.
Taking $\lambda\to\pm\infty$ gives \eqref{dsasm:pr:rateends} and infinity
outside $[0,1/2]$. These formulas show continuity on its finite domain
and lower semicontinuity everywhere; compact sublevel sets follow.

For the lower bound at an interior point $x$, exponentially tilt
by the optimizer. The exact tilted measure is simply fugacity $r(x)$.
Under that measure $X_n\to x$ in probability by \eqref{dsasm:pr:mvlimits}.
On $|X_n-x|<\eta$, change of measure gives the lower exponent
$\Lambda_s(\lambda)-\lambda x-|\lambda|\eta+o(1)$, since the tilted
probability tends to one. Let $\eta\downarrow0$ within a given open
set. Endpoints are approached from $(0,1/2)$ using continuity of the
rate. If the open set has no point in $[0,1/2]$, its lower bound is
vacuous.

For the upper bound, an exponential Markov inequality at any real
$\lambda$ bounds a neighborhood of $x$ by the affine exponent
$\lambda x-\Lambda_s(\lambda)$, with an arbitrarily small local error.
For any number strictly below $\inf_F I_s$, choose one such affine
bound at every point of $F\cap[0,1]$. This compact set has a finite
subcover, and a union bound proves the desired upper bound. If the
infimum is infinite, the same argument works for every finite number.
The support $X_n\in[0,1]$ handles points outside that compact set.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:pr:ldp} without a separate general large-deviation
theorem.

For each fixed $\delta>0$, the upper tail
$\mathbb P_s(X_n\ge1/2+\delta)$ is superexponentially small at speed
$n$. No $n^2$-scale rate is identified. The endpoint values in
\eqref{dsasm:pr:rateends} govern neighborhoods in the large-deviation
principle; they are not asymptotics for exact point probabilities,
which can vanish because of parity. In particular this statement
does not say that the event $X_n>1/2$ without a fixed gap is
superexponentially small.

\section{Inverting the count threshold}
\label{dsasm:pr:sec:inverse}
From $Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}$ and the positive contribution of the identity
matrix, $a_{n+1}>a_n$. For $T>1$ let
\[
 N(T)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge T\},\qquad
 n_0=\sqrt{\frac{\log T}{\alpha}},\qquad \beta=\frac14\log3.
\]
There exists a function $\eta(T)\downarrow0$ (monotonicity can be
imposed by taking a decreasing tail envelope) such that, for all
sufficiently large $T$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:pr:inversebound}
 \left\lceil n_0-\frac\beta{2\alpha}-\eta(T)\right\rceil
 \le N(T)\le
 \left\lceil n_0-\frac\beta{2\alpha}+\eta(T)\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
To prove this, write \eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth} as
$\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n+n\varepsilon_n$, with
$\varepsilon_n\to0$. For each small fixed $\varepsilon>0$, the
functions $\alpha x^2+(\beta\pm\varepsilon)x$ are increasing for
large $x$ and bound the sequence eventually. Their positive roots
at level $\log T$ are
\[
 n_0-\frac{\beta\pm\varepsilon}{2\alpha}+O(n_0^{-1}).
\]
As in any monotone envelope inversion, the upper count envelope
gives a lower ceiling for $N(T)$, and the lower count envelope gives
an upper ceiling. A diagonal choice of $\varepsilon\downarrow0$
and increasing thresholds proves \eqref{dsasm:pr:inversebound}.

The unrounded width tends to zero, so the bracket contains at most
two consecutive integers eventually. The vanishing error must remain
inside the ceilings; near an integer threshold it can change the
answer. One cannot assert the real-valued estimate
$N(T)=n_0-\beta/(2\alpha)+o(1)$ for an integer-valued staircase.
There is no effective numerical onset here, and no identified
$\log n_0/n_0$ correction from the new $o(n)$ growth remainder.
For the strict threshold $\min\{n:a_n>T\}$, replace each ceiling
in \eqref{dsasm:pr:inversebound} by $\lfloor\,\cdot\,\rfloor+1$.
\begin{writenote}[Superseded, 5~October 2026]
Part~II's Corollary~\ref{dsasm:log:inverseintro} identifies the $\log n_0/n_0$ correction ($\kappa\log r/(2\alpha r)$ in the centre), and Part~I's Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse} adds the amplitude term with a vanishing error before the ceilings.
\end{writenote}

\section{Remaining questions and reproducibility}\label{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}

The linear coefficient and fixed-fugacity laws resolve several
questions left by one-point growth bounds. They leave specific
subleading problems open.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can the pressure error be controlled sharply enough to identify
the logarithmic term at $s=1$, then a positive amplitude in
\eqref{dsasm:pr:conjecture}? The logarithmic-tail argument here gives convergence,
not a remainder expansion.
\item Can one prove $M_n(s)-nm(s)=o(\sqrt n)$, or a finer expansion,
uniformly on compact fugacity intervals? This would permit explicit
limiting-mean centering in the Gaussian results.
\item At what scales does the escaping half of the root measure move
to infinity? The compactified limit alone does not determine a joint
large-$n$, large-fugacity regime or tails at speed $n^2$.
\item Can one obtain coefficient asymptotics with relative errors and
explicit prefactors away from the central window? The local central
limit theorem gives uniform additive errors, and the large-deviation
principle gives logarithmic probabilities, neither of which supplies
such a prefactor.
\item Can quantitative constants and onsets be carried through the
singular-value limit and inversion to give a certified finite-target
threshold algorithm? The present inverse bracket is eventual and
non-effective.
\end{enumerate}
The full equivalent, possible parity-dependent corrections, and
root simplicity remain outside the proved conclusions. Weak
interlacing permits common or multiple roots and is all that was used.
No claim of external peer review, formal proof verification, or global
bibliographic novelty is made.
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions, 5~October 2026]
\emph{Question~1 is answered}: the logarithmic term is $-\kappa\log n$ with bounded error (Part~II, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:log:main}), and the positive amplitude exists (Part~I, Theorem~\ref{dsasm:thm:main}); its value is open (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~1). \emph{Question~2 is answered}: $M_n(s)-nm(s)=O(1)$ uniformly on compact fugacity intervals (Part~II, \eqref{dsasm:log:cumulants}, whose proof is locally uniform in~$s$, although its constants are stated only to depend on the fugacity), and it converges, locally uniformly on positive fugacity intervals as Part~I's Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:cumulants} states (precision added 5~October 2026). \emph{Questions~3--5 remain open}. For question~3, uncertified evidence from the intake dossier: the exact $P_{60}$ has largest root about $7.3\cdot10^{32}$, and the fractions of its roots with magnitude below $1$, $4$, $10$, $100$ are $0.367$, $0.467$, $0.500$, $0.533$, against $0.352$, $0.455$, $0.485$, $0.499$ for $\nu([0,x])$, whose finite part has total mass $\frac12$, consistent with half the mass escaping. (Dated note, 5~October 2026, after the independent check recorded at the end of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}: the comparison values first read ``for the normalized finite part of $\nu$''; that normalization would double them.) Of the last paragraph, the full equivalent is now proved (Part~I); the parity-dependent corrections (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~2) and \emph{root simplicity} remain open: whether the roots of $P_n$ are simple and the interlacing \eqref{dsasm:log:weakinterlace} strict is not known, and the exact $P_n$, $n\le31$, give no counterexample (dossier). At the independent check, every exact $P_n$ with $n\le64$ is squarefree with $P_n(0)>0$, and $\gcd(P_n,P_{n+1})=1$ for $n<64$; so for $n\le64$ the roots are simple and the interlacing is strict (added 5~October 2026).
\end{writenote}


The companion supplies this TeX source, a deterministic PDF build,
standard-library exact checks, fixtures, integrity tests and closed
file inventories. Primary papers are linked below; no source-paper
PDFs or private working notes are included. The checker independently
constructs finite matrices and polynomials and verifies pinned finite
ranges and exact symbolic identities. Such checks catch indexing,
normalization, sign and implementation errors; the asymptotic
statements follow from the proofs in this report, not an extrapolation
of checked sizes.

The outer inventory rejects missing, changed and unexpected files,
including unexpected directories and symlinks. Negative tests exercise
these failure paths both normally and under Python optimization.
The mathematical checker has its own closed inventory and guarded
negative tests. Checksums detect modifications but are not authenticated
signatures. The README states exact commands, checked ranges and
software requirements.

The PDF builder compiles in two independent temporary directories,
fixes metadata, rejects overfull boxes and unresolved references,
and requires byte-identical PDFs. The archive uses sorted entries
and fixed ZIP metadata. Fresh extraction, verification, rebuilding
and repacking are tested in the same toolchain. This is same-toolchain
reproducibility, not a guarantee of identical output from different
TeX distributions, fonts, Python versions or compression libraries.
Every page is rendered for visual inspection before delivery.

\begin{partabstract}
Let $a_n$ count diagonally symmetric alternating sign matrices of order
$n$, and let $Z_n(s)$ record their number of nonzero diagonal entries.
We prove that $Z_n(s)=s^{n\bmod2}P_n(s^2)$, where all zeros of $P_n$
are strictly negative and real, with multiplicities allowed. The proof
applies a degree-two case of the known Borcea--Br\"and\'en MOD operator
to the published triangular six-vertex model. Finite determinant
transformations give an exactly solvable calibration at $s=\sqrt3$.
Combining that calibration with $Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}$, Jensen's inequality
and a quantitative two-step contraction yields, eventually,
\[
 \alpha n^2+pn-\frac5{72}\log n-C
 \ \le\ \log a_n\ \le\
 \alpha n^2+qn-\frac5{72}\log n+C,
\]
where $\alpha=\frac12\log(3\sqrt3/4)$,
$p\approx0.2632463454$ and $q\approx0.2960487269$ are precisely defined
fixed-point constants. A threshold inverse has an eventual bracket
of at most two consecutive integers; its error constants and onset
are existential. This is a partial result. The positive gap $q-p$
leaves the true linear term, actual logarithmic power and amplitude
undetermined, and the full equivalent in Behrend--Fischer--Koutschan's
Conjecture 11.1 remains open here. No bibliographic priority or
external peer-review claim is made.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[Status of Part~IV, 5~October 2026]
The envelopes and the inverse of this Part are superseded: Part~III identifies the linear coefficient $\beta=\frac14\log3\in(p,q)$, Part~II proves $\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1)$, and Part~I proves the full equivalent of Conjecture~11.1's leading term; so the sentence ``The positive gap $q-p$ leaves the true linear term, actual logarithmic power and amplitude undetermined, and the full equivalent \ldots\ remains open here'' is historical. The Part is kept for its method, which appears nowhere else in the report: Jensen and chord inequalities on the negative roots at the fugacities $1$, $\sqrt3$, $2$ (Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:jensen}), a projected two-step contraction (Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:trapping}) and exact interval certificates for $p$ and $q$; it uses only the exact prerequisites of Sections~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability}--\ref{dsasm:log:sec:logcalibration} and~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:consequences}, no operator theory.
\end{writenote}
\section{The model and the precise scope}\label{dsasm:env:sec:main}

An alternating sign matrix is a square matrix with entries in
$\{-1,0,1\}$ whose nonzero entries alternate in sign along every row
and column and sum to one on each such line. A diagonally symmetric
alternating sign matrix (DSASM) satisfies $A=A^{\mathsf T}$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:def}
\begin{split}
 a_n&=|\DS(n)|,\qquad S(A)=|\{i:A_{ii}\ne0\}|,\\
 Q_n(s_1,\ldots,s_n)&=\sum_{A\in\DS(n)}\prod_{i:A_{ii}\ne0}s_i,
 \qquad Z_n(s)=Q_n(s,\ldots,s).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $n\ge1$ is the matrix order, and $a_0=1$ can be added by the empty
matrix convention. The count is OEIS A005163~\cite{oeis}; in the
notation of Behrend, Fischer and Koutschan~\cite{bfk},
$Z_n(s)=X_n(1,s,1)$. In particular, $Z_n(1)=a_n$.
There is no factorial normalization or shift in the meaning of $a_n$.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:constants}
 g=\log\frac{3\sqrt3}{4},\qquad \alpha=\frac g2,\qquad
 b=\frac{g+\log2}{2},\qquad
 \theta=\frac{\log3}{\log4},\qquad \kappa=\frac5{72}.
\end{equation}
On $J=[b-g,b]$, define
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:maps}
\begin{split}
 \ell(x)&=\frac b\theta-g-\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}x,\\
 u(x)&=x-g+\frac12\log\!\left(\frac{3e^{2(b-x)}-1}{2}\right).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The logarithm is well-defined on $J$. Section~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:trapping}
proves that $\ell\circ u$ has exactly one fixed point $p$ in $J$.
Set $q=u(p)$, so $p=\ell(q)$.

\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:env:main}
For every $n\ge1$, $Q_n$ is nonzero whenever every $\Re s_i>0$.
Writing $\eps=n\bmod2$, there is a polynomial with nonnegative integer
coefficients such that
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:paritypoly}
 Z_n(s)=s^\eps P_n(s^2),\qquad
 \deg P_n=\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\qquad P_n(0)>0.
\end{equation}
All zeros of $P_n$ are strictly negative and real. They need not be
simple for the purposes of this theorem.

There are constants $C>0$ and $n_*$ such that for all integers $n\ge n_*$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:envelopes}
 \alpha n^2+pn-\kappa\log n-C
 \le\log a_n\le
 \alpha n^2+qn-\kappa\log n+C.
\end{equation}
The constants satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:decimalshort}
 p\approx0.2632463454,\qquad q\approx0.2960487269,\qquad p<q.
\end{equation}
The corresponding threshold inverse is described in
Theorem~\ref{dsasm:env:inverse}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{writenote}[Superseded, 5~October 2026]
The linear coefficient is $\beta=\frac14\log3=0.2746530722\ldots$, strictly between $p$ and $q$ (Part~III, \eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth}); Part~II proves $\log a_n=\alpha n^2+\beta n-\kappa\log n+O(1)$ for all $n\ge1$, and Part~I the full equivalent. The structural statements are proved in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:stability} (this Part's Section~2 is printed there).
\end{writenote}


Equivalently, fixed positive constants $c_-,c_+$ give
\[
 c_- n^{-\kappa}e^{\alpha n^2+pn}
 \le a_n\le
 c_+ n^{-\kappa}e^{\alpha n^2+qn}
 \quad(n\ge n_*).
\]
The $n^{-\kappa}$ factors belong to these two different exponential
envelopes. Because $p<q$, their presence does not identify the power
of $n$ at the unknown true linear coefficient.

\subsection{Established inputs and what is proved here}
We use version 3 of the primary paper~\cite{bfk}, dated 1 October 2026.
Its Section 5.1 supplies the triangular six-vertex bijection;
Equation (4.11) supplies the adjacent determinant;
Proposition 5.1 supplies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:adjacentcount}
 Z_n(2)=a_{n+1}\qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
The quadratic growth rate $\log a_n\sim\alpha n^2$ was already
established in its Theorem 11.2. It is not a new conclusion claimed
by this report.

The stability-preserving MOD operator is established prior art in
Borcea--Br\"and\'en~\cite[Section 8.4]{bb2}. We give its elementary
degree-at-most-two proof and apply it to the DSASM graph. The classical
shifted determinant evaluation used for calibration is recorded in
Krattenthaler~\cite[Theorem 34, Equation (3.24)]{kratt}. All reductions
from those inputs to \eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes} are proved below. Finite exact
checks accompany the proof but do not substitute for it.

The leading equivalent predicted in \cite[Conjecture 11.1]{bfk} is
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:conjecture}
 a_n\sim C_{\DS}\,e^{\alpha n^2+(\log3)n/4}n^{-5/72},
 \qquad C_{\DS}>0.
\end{equation}
The source also conjectures parity-sensitive finer corrections.
Neither \eqref{dsasm:env:conjecture} nor those corrections are proved here.
Even existence of a limit for $(\log a_n-\alpha n^2)/n$ remains
unresolved by our argument. The conjectured value
$(\log3)/4\approx0.2746530722$ lies strictly between $p$ and $q$.
No equality of the two parity-dependent calibration amplitudes is
asserted or used.
\begin{writenote}[Historical, 5~October 2026]
Part~III proves that $(\log a_n-\alpha n^2)/n\to(\log3)/4$; Part~I proves \eqref{dsasm:env:conjecture} and the equality of the two parity calibration amplitudes, \eqref{dsasm:calibrationequiv}. The finer corrections remain unproved (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}).
\end{writenote}

\section{Jensen and chord inequalities for the roots}
\label{dsasm:env:sec:jensen}
For $n\ge2$, use the factorization \eqref{dsasm:pr:factorization} and put
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:xi}
 \xi_j=\log\frac{4+\gamma_j}{1+\gamma_j},\qquad
 \phi(x)=\log\frac{1+2e^x}{3}.
\end{equation}
Then $0<\xi_j<\log4$ and
\[
 \phi(\xi_j)=\log\frac{3+\gamma_j}{1+\gamma_j}.
\]
The function $\phi$ is increasing and strictly convex, since
\[
 \phi'(x)=\frac{2e^x}{1+2e^x}>0,\qquad
 \phi''(x)=\frac{2e^x}{(1+2e^x)^2}>0.
\]
Moreover, $\phi(0)=0$ and $\phi(\log4)=\log3$, so the endpoint chord
has slope $\theta$. Jensen's inequality and that chord give the exact
bounds
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:Jensen}
 m\phi\left(\frac{D_n}{m}\right)\le E_n\le\theta D_n,
 \qquad m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor,
\end{equation}
where, writing $L_n=\log a_n$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:DE}
\begin{split}
 D_n&=\log\frac{P_n(4)}{P_n(1)}
       =L_{n+1}-L_n-\eps\log2,\\
 E_n&=\log\frac{P_n(3)}{P_n(1)}
       =W_n-L_n-\frac\eps2\log3.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The equalities use \eqref{dsasm:env:adjacentcount}. The parity factors have been
retained. In the chord inequality they cancel exactly, because
$\theta\log2=(\log3)/2$. The exceptional case $n=1$ has $m=0$ and
is not included in Jensen's inequality; it has no asymptotic effect.

\subsection{A bounded corrected residual}
Normalize by
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:yn}
 y_n=\frac{L_n-\alpha n^2+\kappa\log n}{n}.
\end{equation}
Positivity of the coefficients makes $Z_n(s)$ increasing on $s>0$.
Together with \eqref{dsasm:env:adjacentcount} this gives, for $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:coarse}
 Z_{n-1}(\sqrt3)\le a_n\le Z_n(\sqrt3).
\end{equation}
By \eqref{dsasm:log:W}, the upper bound yields $y_n\le b+O(1/n)$.
For the lower bound, expand
$\alpha(n-1)^2+b(n-1)-\kappa\log(n-1)$; it yields
$y_n\ge b-g-O(1/n)$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:distance}
 \dist(y_n,J)=O(1/n),\qquad J=[b-g,b].
\end{equation}
In particular the $y_n$ are bounded. This does not assert that they
converge or lie exactly inside $J$.

Substituting \eqref{dsasm:env:yn} and \eqref{dsasm:log:W} into \eqref{dsasm:env:DE} gives the uniform
expansions
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:DEexpansion}
\begin{split}
 E_n&=n(b-y_n)+O(1),\\
 D_n&=n(g+y_{n+1}-y_n)+O(1).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
For the second line, the additional terms are
$\alpha+y_{n+1}-\kappa\log(1+1/n)-\eps\log2$, all uniformly
bounded. Hence the chord half of \eqref{dsasm:env:Jensen} implies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:lowerrec}
 y_{n+1}\ge\ell(y_n)-O(1/n).
\end{equation}

The inverse of $\phi$ is
\[
 \psi(v)=\log\frac{3e^v-1}{2},\qquad v>-\log3.
\]
The Jensen half of \eqref{dsasm:env:Jensen} gives
$D_n\le m\psi(E_n/m)$. Since $m=n/2+O(1)$,
\[
 \frac{E_n}{m}=2(b-y_n)+O(1/n).
\]
By \eqref{dsasm:env:distance}, these arguments lie in a fixed compact
neighborhood of $[0,2g]$ contained in $(-\log3,\infty)$.
The derivative of $\psi$ is bounded there. Therefore
\[
 D_n\le\frac n2\psi\bigl(2(b-y_n)\bigr)+O(1),
\]
and the second line of \eqref{dsasm:env:DEexpansion} yields
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:upperrec}
 y_{n+1}\le u(y_n)+O(1/n).
\end{equation}
All constants here are independent of parity; no limiting root
distribution and no limiting value of $y_n$ was assumed.

\section{A projected two step contraction}
\label{dsasm:env:sec:trapping}
Set $a=(1-\theta)/\theta$. On $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:derivatives}
 \ell'(x)=-a,\qquad
 u'(x)=-\frac1{3e^{2(b-x)}-1},\qquad |u'(x)|\le\frac12.
\end{equation}
Both maps decrease. Since $1/2<\theta<1$, we have $0<a<1$.
At the endpoints,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:endpoints}
\begin{split}
 \ell(b)=u(b)&=b-g,\\
 \ell(b-g)&=b-g+ag,\\
 u(b-g)&=b-2g+\frac12\log\frac{65}{32}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
For the last identity, $e^{2g}=27/16$. Its lower bound $u(b-g)\ge b-g$
follows from monotonicity and $u(b)=b-g$; its upper bound
$u(b-g)\le b$ follows from $65/32<(27/16)^2$.
The other endpoint values visibly belong to $J$. Thus both maps
preserve $J$.

Define $F=\ell\circ u$ and $G=u\circ\ell$. They increase and are
contractions of $J$ with common Lipschitz bound
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:rho}
 \rho=\frac{1-\theta}{2\theta}<1.
\end{equation}
The contraction theorem gives a unique fixed point $p$ of $F$.
Then $q=u(p)$ satisfies $p=\ell(q)$ and $G(q)=q$, and is the unique
fixed point of $G$.

\subsection{Why projection is needed}
It would not be correct to apply the derivative bound
$|u'|\le1/2$ directly to all $y_n$, because \eqref{dsasm:env:distance} only
places them within $O(1/n)$ of $J$. Let
\[
 z_n=\max\{b-g,\min\{y_n,b\}\}
\]
be their projection onto $J$. Then $|y_n-z_n|=O(1/n)$. The maps are
smooth in a fixed neighborhood of $J$, so
\eqref{dsasm:env:lowerrec}--\eqref{dsasm:env:upperrec} imply, for a fixed $C_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:projected}
 z_{n+1}\ge\ell(z_n)-\frac{C_0}{n},\qquad
 z_{n+1}\le u(z_n)+\frac{C_0}{n}
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $n$.

Both $z_{n+1}$ and $u(z_n)$ lie in $J$. Since $\ell$ decreases and is
$a$-Lipschitz, the second inequality in \eqref{dsasm:env:projected} implies
$\ell(z_{n+1})\ge\ell(u(z_n))-aC_0/n$.
Apply the first inequality at $n+1$. Similarly, both $z_{n+1}$ and
$\ell(z_n)$ lie in $J$, so the first inequality gives
$u(z_{n+1})\le u(\ell(z_n))+C_0/(2n)$; apply the second inequality
at $n+1$. Increasing the constant if necessary, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:twostep}
 z_{n+2}\ge F(z_n)-\frac{C_1}{n},\qquad
 z_{n+2}\le G(z_n)+\frac{C_1}{n}.
\end{equation}
In particular, no derivative estimate is used at an uncontrolled
point outside $J$.

\subsection{Quantitative trapping with a bounded log remainder}
Define $d_n^-=(p-z_n)_+$ and $d_n^+=(z_n-q)_+$, where
$x_+=\max(x,0)$. Since $F$ and $G$ increase, fix $p$ and $q$
respectively, and are $\rho$-Lipschitz, \eqref{dsasm:env:twostep} implies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:deficits}
 d_{n+2}^-\le\rho d_n^-+\frac{C_1}{n},\qquad
 d_{n+2}^+\le\rho d_n^++\frac{C_1}{n}.
\end{equation}
Iterate either inequality separately on each parity. For a sufficiently
large starting index $N$ of that parity and $n=N+2k$,
\[
 d_n^\pm\le\rho^k d_N^\pm
   +C_1\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{\rho^j}{n-2-2j}.
\]
For $j\le k/2$, the denominator is bounded below by a fixed positive
multiple of $n$, except for finitely many small cases absorbed into
the constant. This part is $O(1/n)$ by the geometric sum. In the
remaining part $j>k/2$, use denominator at least $N$; its total is
$O(\rho^{k/2})$. The initial term is also exponentially small.
Thus $d_n^\pm=O(1/n)$, uniformly on both parities.

It follows that $p-O(1/n)\le z_n\le q+O(1/n)$. Undoing the
projection gives the same bounds for $y_n$, and multiplying
\eqref{dsasm:env:yn} by $n$ proves \eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes}. The bounded remainder is
therefore a consequence of quantitative trapping, not merely of a
liminf--limsup argument. In particular,
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log a_n-\alpha n^2}{n}\ge p,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log a_n-\alpha n^2}{n}\le q.
\]

\subsection{Certified fixed point intervals}
The exact rational interval computation in the companion proves
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:pinterval}
\begin{split}
 0.2632463454286169293260401267680&<p,\\
 p&<0.2632463454286169293260401267681,
\end{split}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:qinterval}
\begin{split}
 0.2960487268742950122628240495510507&<q,\\
 q&<0.2960487268742950122628240495510786.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
In particular $q>p$, and the inverse width obeys
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:width}
 0.12537<\frac{q-p}{g}<0.12538<1,
 \qquad \frac{q-p}{g}\approx0.1253798292.
\end{equation}
For completeness, a certificate requires only explicit rational tails.
For $x\ge1$, set $r=(x-1)/(x+1)$ and use
\[
 2\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\frac{r^{2k+1}}{2k+1}
 \le\log x\le
 2\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\frac{r^{2k+1}}{2k+1}
 +\frac{2r^{2M+1}}{(2M+1)(1-r^2)}.
\]
For $0<x<1$, use $\log x=-\log(1/x)$. For $0\le x<N+2$,
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^N\frac{x^k}{k!}\le e^x\le
 \sum_{k=0}^N\frac{x^k}{k!}
 +\frac{x^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\frac1{1-x/(N+2)}.
\]
Interval arithmetic with these tails bounds $F(p_-)-p_->0$ and
$F(p_+)-p_+<0$ at the rational endpoints of \eqref{dsasm:env:pinterval}.
The unique fixed point lies between them. Monotonicity of $u$ then
bounds $q$ between $u(p_+)$ and $u(p_-)$, giving
\eqref{dsasm:env:qinterval}--\eqref{dsasm:env:width}. No floating-point sign decision
participates in the certificate.

\section{The threshold inverse and its integer rounding}
\label{dsasm:env:sec:inverse}
Since the identity matrix has $S=n>0$,
$Z_n(2)>Z_n(1)$, so \eqref{dsasm:env:adjacentcount} implies
$a_{n+1}>a_n$. The growth bound also shows $a_n\to\infty$.
For $T>1$ define
\[
 N(T)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge T\},\qquad
 n_0=\sqrt{\frac{\log T}{\alpha}},\qquad
 c(T)=n_0+\frac{\kappa\log n_0}{2\alpha n_0}.
\]
\begin{theorem}\label{dsasm:env:inverse}
There are constants $K>0$ and $T_*$ such that, for all $T\ge T_*$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:inverseceil}
 \ceil{c(T)-\frac{q}{2\alpha}-\frac K{n_0}}
 \le N(T)\le
 \ceil{c(T)-\frac{p}{2\alpha}+\frac K{n_0}}.
\end{equation}
The difference between the two unrounded arguments tends to
$(q-p)/g\approx0.1253798292<1$. Consequently the bracket eventually
contains at most two consecutive integers. No explicit numerical
value of $K$ or $T_*$ is supplied by this report.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose $C$ for \eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes} and define, for large real $x$,
\[
 B_+(x)=\alpha x^2+qx-\kappa\log x+C,\qquad
 B_-(x)=\alpha x^2+px-\kappa\log x-C.
\]
Both functions are strictly increasing once $x$ is large enough.
Let $r_-(T)$ be the large solution of $B_+(x)=\log T$, and
$r_+(T)$ the large solution of $B_-(x)=\log T$. For large $T$,
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:rootceil}
 \ceil{r_-(T)}\le N(T)\le\ceil{r_+(T)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $\log T\le L_{N(T)}\le B_+(N(T))$ proves the lower bound.
For the upper bound, the integer $\ceil{r_+(T)}$ is large enough for
\eqref{dsasm:env:envelopes}, and its $B_-$ value is at least $\log T$.

For a fixed real $r$ and fixed constant $c$, the large root of
$\alpha x^2+rx-\kappa\log x+c=\log T$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{dsasm:env:scalarinverse}
 x=n_0-\frac r{2\alpha}
       +\frac{\kappa\log n_0}{2\alpha n_0}+O(n_0^{-1}).
\end{equation}
To check the stated error, substitute the three displayed terms into
the equation. The $O(n_0)$ and $\log n_0$ terms cancel, leaving a
bounded residual, including $c-r^2/(4\alpha)$ and an $o(1)$ term.
The derivative of the left side is $2\alpha n_0+O(1)$ in this
neighborhood, so its root differs by $O(1/n_0)$.
Apply this with $(r,c)=(q,C)$ and $(p,-C)$, enlarge a common $K$,
and retain the errors inside the monotone ceilings in \eqref{dsasm:env:rootceil}.
This proves \eqref{dsasm:env:inverseceil}. Its unrounded width is
$(q-p)/(2\alpha)+2K/n_0$, which tends to \eqref{dsasm:env:width}.
\end{proof}

A width less than one before rounding need not produce a single
integer. The two ceilings can differ by one, and errors cannot be
discarded near an integer threshold. Thus \eqref{dsasm:env:inverseceil} is an
eventual theoretical bracket, not a certified finite-target inversion
algorithm or an $o(1)$-accurate real approximation to an integer
sequence. If the definition is instead $\min\{n:a_n>T\}$, replace
both ceilings in \eqref{dsasm:env:rootceil} and \eqref{dsasm:env:inverseceil} by
$\lfloor\,\cdot\,\rfloor+1$; this preserves the strict inequality
at exact count thresholds.
\begin{writenote}[Superseded, 5~October 2026]
Parts~III, II and~I give successively sharper brackets: \eqref{dsasm:pr:inversebound}, Corollary~\ref{dsasm:log:inverseintro} and Corollary~\ref{dsasm:cor:inverse}. None is effective.
\end{writenote}


\section{Further questions and reproducibility}\label{dsasm:env:sec:questions}

\subsection{What would advance the full asymptotic problem}
The present bounds leave a definite linear-scale gap. The following
questions are narrower than asking directly for the full equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can one prove existence of a single limit for
$(\log a_n-\alpha n^2)/n$, and identify it as $(\log3)/4$?
The one-point Jensen and chord information used here does not force
that convergence.
\item Can more information about the negative roots of $P_n$ sharpen
the relation between its values at $1$, $3$ and $4$? A limiting root
statistic or a quantitative improvement to Jensen could potentially
reduce the interval $[p,q]$. Such a limit is not proved here.
\item Can one determine the large-$n$ behavior of
$D_n(1)/D_n(3)$ using the finite representation \eqref{dsasm:log:Gdet}?
Its denominator is exactly known. Even an adjacent-product
asymptotic requires additional parity control before it determines
individual $a_n$ amplitudes.
\item Can the derivative of the calibration determinant be analyzed
through its inverse-matrix trace? The finite determinant permits an
exact derivative formula, but no limiting trace evaluation is assumed
or established in this report.
\item Can explicit uniform constants be carried through Stirling,
the two-step trapping argument and scalar inversion to make the
threshold bracket effective for a specified finite target?
\end{enumerate}
Root simplicity and cross-size interlacing are not addressed by this
report. Neither is needed for the bounds. Likewise, a common
calibration amplitude is neither needed nor claimed.
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions, 5~October 2026]
\emph{Question~1 is answered} by Part~III, \eqref{dsasm:pr:lineargrowth} (and with bounded error by Part~II). \emph{Question~2} is overtaken: Part~III proves the limiting root statistic \eqref{dsasm:pr:rootlaw}, and the interval $[p,q]$ is no longer needed. \emph{Question~3 is answered}: $\log(D_n(1)/D_n(3))=n\log(2/3)+O(1)$ (Part~II, \eqref{dsasm:log:Dmain}) and the remainder converges to $E(1)$ (Part~I); the parity control it asks for is Part~I's boundary limit \eqref{dsasm:Ftarget}. \emph{Question~4 is answered}: the trace limit is \eqref{dsasm:pr:maintarget} of Part~III and, with error $o(1)$, \eqref{dsasm:log:firstmain} of Part~II. \emph{Question~5 remains open} (Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}, item~4). Of the last paragraph: weak cross-size interlacing, with multiplicities, is proved in Part~II, Section~\ref{dsasm:log:sec:interlacing}, and a common calibration amplitude in Part~I, \eqref{dsasm:calibrationequiv}; root simplicity and strict interlacing remain open (Section~\ref{dsasm:pr:sec:questions}). (Corrected after the independent check recorded at the end of Section~\ref{dsasm:sec:further}; the sentence first read ``cross-size interlacing is proved'' and ``root simplicity remains open''.)
\end{writenote}


\subsection{The reproducibility companion}
The report source, deterministic PDF build, exact verification scripts,
fixtures and closed inventories are supplied in the companion ZIP.
The primary papers are linked in the references rather than copied
into the archive. The archive contains no private working notes or
source-paper PDFs.

The mathematical certificates use exact rational or integer
arithmetic. The fixed-point intervals use explicit logarithm and
exponential tails, not floating-point evidence. Small-size orientation
and determinant computations check the model, indexing and formulas;
they do not prove stability or an asymptotic statement for all sizes.
The general proofs are the arguments of Sections~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:stability}
through~\ref{dsasm:env:sec:inverse}.

The companion README describes the precise checked ranges and all
commands. Every executable guard remains active under Python's
optimization flag. Its negative tests deliberately change fixtures,
schemas and manifests and require the intended failure diagnostic.
The package inventory rejects missing, changed and unexpected files.
Checksums detect modification; they are not authenticated signatures.

The PDF builder fixes dates and metadata, compiles in two independent
temporary directories, rejects overfull boxes and unresolved
references, and requires byte-identical PDFs. Archive creation uses
sorted paths and fixed metadata. Fresh extraction, rebuilding,
verification and repacking are tested in the same toolchain. This is
same-toolchain reproducibility, not a claim that every TeX, font,
Python or compression version produces identical bytes. Every page
of the delivered PDF is rendered and visually checked.
